Symmetric varieties for endoscopic groups
Spencer Leslie

Abstract
. We introduce a notion of endoscopic varieties for a symmetric variety X \mathrm{X} , and establish the foundational properties of these varieties such as matching of stable semi-simple orbits. To do this, we introduce certain automorphism groups of homogeneous spherical varieties, which encode the fine rational structure needed to work over non-algebraically closed fields. In particular, we establish the existence and uniqueness of the corresponding symmetric varieties under a mild restriction of the characteristic of the field of definition. We conjecture that this construction plays a role analogous to endoscopic groups in the context of the relative trace formula. As evidence, we show how our construction gives a pre-stabilization of regular elliptic terms of relative trace formulae for many symmetric varieties. When the cotangent bundle of the symmetric variety is hyperspherical, we relate our theory to the Hamiltonian variety of the Langlands dual group introduced by Ben-Zvi, Sakellaridis, and Venkatesh, proving some structural conjectures for this variety in the symmetric setting.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 11F70; Secondary 11F55, 11F66

1.
Introduction

The primary goal of this paper is to develop a theory of endoscopy in the relative Langlands program of Jacquet and Sakellaridis–Venkatesh. That is, given a connected reductive group
{\mathrm{G}}
over a field
k
and a smooth symmetric variety
\mathrm{X}
, we develop a method of associating symmetric varieties
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
for endoscopic groups
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
in terms of the dual group
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
which is suitable for stabilizing the relative trace formula associated to many pairs
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{X})
. As discussed below, this requires proving the existence and uniqueness of the appropriate symmetric varieties of endoscopic groups, for which we introduce a new invariant (the geometric cocyle) to homogeneous spherical
k
-varieties to differentiate between possible
k
-forms of the variety
\mathrm{X}
with different arithmetic properties. We then establish the foundational properties required of a theory of endoscopy, including the matching of geometric semi-simple orbits necessary for comparison of relative trace formulae. In sufficiently nice settings, we place these results in the context of the relative duality conjectures of Ben-Zvi, Sakellaridis, and Venkatesh
[
BZSV23
]
.

In Section
10.2
, we show how our notion of endoscopic datum of the symmetric variety gives a pre-stabilization of the regular elliptic part of the relative trace formula for symmetric varieties. This relies on several cohomological preliminaries established in the companion paper
[
Les24
]
, where we also apply these results to formulate the local harmonic-analytic conjectures (smooth transfer, fundamental lemma, etc.) generalizing notions introduced in
[
Les19b
,
Les19a
,
Les22
]
and stabilize the elliptic part of a large class of relative trace formulae subject to these conjectures. Nevertheless, the main step in the prestabilization is demonstrated in Proposition
10.5
, in which all of our existence and uniqueness results become necessary. In a sense, the notion of endoscopic datum of a symmetric variety
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is formulated to prove Proposition
10.5
.

1.1.
Motivation for a relative theory of endoscopy

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a connected reductive group over a field
k
, which we assume for the moment is global. In this setting, one is interested in the study of period integrals of automorphic forms and their relations to functoriality and arithmetic quantities such as
L
-functions. While certainly not the most general setting, this typically involves a closed subgroup
\mathrm{H}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
and one seeks to analyze the period integral

\varphi\in L^{2}(Z_{\mathrm{H}}({\mathrm{G}})(\mathbb{A}){\mathrm{G}}(k)\backslash{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{A}))\longmapsto\displaystyle\int_{Z_{\mathrm{H}}({\mathrm{G}})(\mathbb{A})\mathrm{H}(k)\backslash\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{A})}^{\ast}\varphi(h)dh,

where
Z_{\mathrm{H}}({\mathrm{G}})=\mathrm{H}\cap Z({\mathrm{G}})
and the asterisk indicates the common need to regularize the integral.

In this context, one of the central tools is the relative trace formula (RTF). Introduced by Jacquet in the 1980’s
[
Jac86
]
, this gives a distribution designed to relate period questions on different groups via comparisons: by matching up the geometric terms (orbital integrals) of two RTFs associated to
({\mathrm{G}}_{1},\mathrm{H}_{1})
and
({\mathrm{G}}_{2},\mathrm{H}_{2})
, we can extract relations between the respective spectral terms (
L
-values and period integrals). A famous recent example of this method is the proof of the Gan–Gross–Prasad and Ichino–Ikeda conjectures for unitary groups, now a theorem by the combination of many works
[
Yun11
,
Zha14
,
BPLZZ21
,
BPCZ22
]
.

In general, many of the good properties present in the GGP setting (trivial generic stabilizers on the geometric side, local multiplicity one on the spectral side) fail for more general RTFs, and several new problems arise. As noted by Getz and Wambach in their twisted base change formalism
[
GW14
]
, many RTFs require a form of
endoscopy and stabilization
analogous to the Arthur–Selberg trace formula in order to be effectively compared. Spectrally, this is connected to questions of whether periods of automorphic forms (or more properly, the squares of their norms) are Eulerian or are sums of Euler products. See
[
BZSV23
, Section 14]
for a collection of conjectures of when such formulae are expected to hold; we expect the theory of endoscopy developed here will play a role in establishing cases of these conjectures, and discuss a specific example in Section
10
.

When
k
is a local field, one is interested in understanding the space of
\mathrm{H}(k)
-invariant functionals
\Hom_{\mathrm{H}(k)}(\pi,\mathbb{C})
for an irreducible admissible representation
\pi
of
{\mathrm{G}}(k)
. This is most naturally studied on the level of packets, as illustrated by the local Gan–Gross–Prasad conjectures and the conjectures of Sakellaridis and Venkatesh
[
SV17
]
. Indeed, questions about the distribution of
\mathrm{H}
-invariant functionals within packets, as well as general dimension formulae such as
[
Wan22
,
Pra20
]
often have interpretations in terms of endoscopic transfer. To give an example, the multiplicity formulae for unitary Shalika models in
[
BPW23
]
(see also
[
CG21
]
) are explicitly computed via a local trace formula technique combined with the endoscopic properties of
L
-packets for unitary groups.

1.1.1.
Prior work

As a first case, we developed a putative theory of endoscopy for the case of unitary Friedberg–Jacquet (FJ) periods where
{\mathrm{G}}=\U_{2n}
is a unitary group of even rank and
\mathrm{H}=\U_{n}\times\U_{n}

[
Les19b
,
Les19a
,
Les22
]
, establishing many of the necessary harmonic-analytic results such as the fundamental lemma and results toward smooth transfer. The stabilization of the elliptic part of this trace formula will appear in
[
Les24
]
. This case has important applications with arithmetic: in ongoing joint work with Jingwei Xiao and Wei Zhang, we relate these periods to central values of base change
L
-functions on associated general linear groups by comparing a relative trace formula to the
stabilized relative trace formula for unitary FJ periods
. Unfortunately, the notion of endoscopic symmetric variety used in
[
Les19b
]
relies on certain degenerations to relate relative orbital integrals to more classical objects in the setting of endoscopy for Lie algebras. As a result, it does not clearly extend to more general period questions.

The purpose of this paper is to develop a general framework of endoscopy for symmetric varieties which recovers the theory of
[
Les19b
,
Les19a
,
Les22
]
, allows for the (pre-)stabilization of relative trace formulas for more general symmetric varieties, and is compatible with the conjectures of Sakellaridis–Venkatesh and the more recent relative duality conjectures of
[
BZSV23
]
(see Section
1.6
below).

1.2.
The dual group and semi-simple descent

Suppose now that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a connected reductive group over a field
k
of either characteristic zero or large enough positive characteristic in a sense made precise later. Suppose also that
\mathrm{X}
is a homogeneous spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety with
x_{0}\in\mathrm{X}(k)\neq\emptyset
. Setting
\mathrm{H}:=\mathrm{Stab}_{{\mathrm{G}}}(x_{0})
, we obtain
\mathrm{X}\simeq\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. Assume also that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split, and let
\mathrm{A}\subset\B
denote a
k
-rational maximal torus and a Borel subgroup. Sphericity of
\mathrm{X}
is the statement that
\B
acts on
\mathrm{X}
with a Zariski-open orbit. As first observed by Brion
[
Bri90
]
, there exists a based root datum
\Psi_{\mathrm{X}}
associated to
\mathrm{X}
which controls the large scale geometry of
\mathrm{X}
.

Motivated by Langlands duality, one may associate a complex reductive group
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
by passing to the dual root datum. In
[
SV17
]
, Sakellaridis and Venkatesh refined the dual group to a hypothetical homomorphism

(1.1)

\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}:\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}\times\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow\check{{\mathrm{G}}}

to formulate a Plancherel theorem for spherical varieties over p-adic fields. The existence and uniqueness (up to conjugacy) of this morphism was established by Knop and Schalke
[
KS17
]
, who refer to such maps as distinguished morphisms. As clarified in
[
Kno19
]
, there is an essentially unique way to induce an action of the Galois group
\Gamma
of
k
on
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
so that
\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}
intertwines with the
L
-action on
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}
.

Now assume that
\mathrm{X}
is symmetric, in the sense that there is a
k
-rational involutive automorphism
\theta
of
{\mathrm{G}}
such that

({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta});

we always assume that
\mathrm{H}
is smooth (automatic if
\mathrm{H}
connected or if
\mathrm{char}(k)
is zero). We now assume that
\mathrm{char}(k)
is good for
{\mathrm{G}}
(cf. Section
2.1
).

The notion of endoscopic group is closely tied to semi-simple descent on
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}
. Our starting point for the relative setting is the construction of
\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}
by Knop–Schalke (which is compatible with ideas of Nadler
[
Nad05
]
): the image of
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{X}}:=\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}(\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}})
is (the connected component of the identity of) a symmetric subgroup of a Levi subgroup
\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}\subset\check{{\mathrm{G}}}
(Proposition
7.10
). We let

\hat{{\mathrm{X}}}:=\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{{\mathrm{X}}}^{\ast}\backslash\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}.

This is a complex symmetric variety equipped with a distinguished point
\hat{x}_{0}.
The equivariance of
\varphi_{{\mathrm{X}}}
implies that
\Gamma
acts on
\hat{{\mathrm{X}}}
. An important property of symmetric varieties is that they are
quasi-Hamiltonian
: they come with a group-valued moment map

\hat{s}:\hat{{\mathrm{X}}}\longrightarrow\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}\subset\check{{\mathrm{G}}},

which is
\Gamma
-equivariant. We refer to it as the symmetrization map for
\hat{\mathrm{X}}
.

In Section
8
, we isolate a closed subvariety of semi-simple points
x\in\hat{\mathrm{X}}^{\heartsuit}\subset\hat{{\mathrm{X}}}^{ss}
and let
(\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{x},\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},x},\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{{\mathrm{X}},x})
be the
descendant at
x;
that is,
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{x}
(resp.,
\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},x}
) is the centralizer of
\check{s}(x)
in
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}
(resp.,
\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
) and
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{{\mathrm{X}},x}
is the stabilizer of
x\in\hat{\mathrm{X}}
in
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
. The constraint on
x\in\hat{\mathrm{X}}^{ss}
is to ensure that the morphism from
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow\check{{\mathrm{G}}}
induced from (
1.1
) factors through
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{x}
. This restriction is motivated by harmonic-analytic considerations, but plays a role in our arguments (cf. Proposition
4.10
).

Set
\hat{{\mathrm{X}}}_{x}:=\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}(\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{{\mathrm{X}},x})\backslash\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},x}
. The symmetrization map induces a closed immersion

{\eta}_{x}:\hat{{\mathrm{X}}}_{x}\longrightarrow\hat{{\mathrm{X}}},

and a commutative diagram

(1.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{{\mathrm{X}},x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{{\mathrm{X}}}_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{{\mathrm{X}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{{\mathrm{X}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{{\mathrm{X}}}}

Note that if
\eta:\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{x}^{\circ}\subset\check{{\mathrm{G}}}
is the inclusion, the triple
\mathfrak{e}:=(\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{x}^{\circ},\hat{s}(x),\eta)
is an endoscopic datum for
{\mathrm{G}}
. Let
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
denote the corresponding quasi-split reductive group over
k
satisfying that
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}=\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{x}^{\circ}
.

It is natural to ask if the top row of (
1.2
) corresponds to a
k
-rational spherical
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
-variety
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
in the sense that
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}}=\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{{\mathrm{X}},x}
and the induced morphism

\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}|_{\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{{\mathrm{X}},x}}:\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},x}\times\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{x}

is the analogue of (
1.1
) for
({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}},\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}})
. This is essentially the content of Theorem
8.5
(which we state as Theorem
1.4
below), though more work is necessary to state this result. More precisely, we establish the existence and uniqueness (under mild assumptions) of the pair
({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}},\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}})
suitable for relative functoriality and for the stabilization of the RTF associated to
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{X})
.

Remark 1.1

(Why symmetric varieties?)
.

A natural question is why restrict to symmetric varieties among all spherical varieties. There are several components to this.

(1)

Semi-simple descent at
all
semi-simple points is central to the theory of elliptic endoscopy, both classically and in the relative setting. For symmetric varieties, there is a well-understood theory for descent via the symmetrization map, so that the existence of the dual involution in Proposition
7.10
seems to play a crucial role. It is not clear how to extend this to the general spherical setting, though certainly the existence of a group-valued moment map is a reasonable desideratum. Certain
ad hoc
variations of these techniques can be used to include affine spherical varieties such as
{\mathrm{G}}_{2}\backslash\Spin_{7}
or
\SL_{3}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}_{2}
, but it is not clear to us if this is the correct approach in these settings.

(2)

We make concrete use of the notion of
(\Gamma,\theta)
-indices due to
[
Hel00
]
as an enhancement of the spherical datum of a symmetric variety over a non-algebraically closed field in proving the existence of endoscopic symmetric varieties in Sections
4.4
and
8.2
. It is possible that a similar argument may be handled purely in terms of homogeneous spherical data.

(3)

Finally, there are issues of characteristic. While Section
3
discusses general spherical homogeneous spaces, it is essentially restricted to characteristic zero due to the reliance on
[
Los09
,
BG22
]
. It is likely that this restriction may be weakened to include fields of sufficiently large positive characteristic, but this is not clear. In the symmetric setting, the work of Helminck
[
Hel88
,
Hel00
]
and Levy
[
Lev07
]
allow us to handle positive characteristic settings with minor restrictions.

Remark 1.2

(What about hyperspherical varieties?)
.

Generalizing in a different direction, one may ask about the setting of
hyperspherical varieties
as studied in
[
BZSV23
]
as a good context for “relative Langlands duality.” As defined in Section 3.5 of
loc. cit.
, a hyperspherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety
\M
is a Hamiltonian
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety with a moment map
\mu:M\to\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
satisfying several assumptions including that
M
is smooth, affine, and that the stabilizer in
{\mathrm{G}}
of a generic point of
M
is connected. When
M=T^{\ast}\mathrm{X}
with
\mathrm{X}
spherical, then Proposition 3.7.4 of
[
BZSV23
]
implies that if
M
is hyperspherical,
\mathrm{X}
is affine and “has no spherical roots of type
N
;” see Section
3.1
. More generally,
M
is determined by the data

•

an injective morphism
\mathrm{H}\times\SL_{2}\to{\mathrm{G}}
with
\mathrm{H}
reductive,

•

a symplectic
\mathrm{H}
-representation
S
.

Our results and methods readily extend to spherical varieties obtained by parabolic induction from a symmetric variety on a Levi subgroup as well as Whittaker-type inductions from such varieties. These Whittaker inductions include several examples of hyperspherical varieties which are not cotangent bundles of symmetric varieties, such as generalized Shalika models and their unitary variants. For the sake of space, we omit a detailed account of these cases, as the core existence and uniqueness results ultimately reduce to the symmetric case. We plan to return to this more general setting in future work.

On the other hand, this article
does not
impose the restriction that generic stabilizers on
T^{\ast}\mathrm{X}
are connected: we allow
\mathrm{X}
to be a general (smooth) symmetric variety for the majority of the paper, and only impose a restriction on roots of type
N
when connecting to conjectures of
[
BZSV23
]
. Indeed, many very natural symmetric varieties have type
N
roots, such as
\mathrm{O}_{n}\backslash\GL_{n}
or
A_{1}\cdot A_{5}\backslash E_{6}
. While these cases are ruled out of
[
SV17
,
BZSV23
]
, there is good reason to expect that these theories extend in some fashion to include such case (see
[
CY23
]
for a geometric example and
[
PWZ21
]
for an arithmetic one). We hope that working in this generality will fit naturally into future extensions of the framework of
[
BZSV23
]
.

1.3.
Rationality questions

We first address the question of
uniqueness
of the variety
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
on
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
. The problem here is that the data of the dual group
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
and the morphism
\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}
in (
1.1
) – even with appropriate Galois actions incorporated – do not determine a unique spherical
k
-variety
\mathrm{X}
, even up to inner twist.

Example 1.3
.

An important motivating example is the unitary FJ variety

\mathrm{X}=\U_{n}\times\U_{n}\backslash\U_{2n},

where we take all the unitary groups to be quasi-split over
k
. In this case one calculates that
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}=\Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{C})
and the morphism
\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}
is trivial on the
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
factor and may be arranged to be the natural inclusion

\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}:\Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow\GL_{2n}(\mathbb{C});

we may arrange so that the
\Gamma
-action on
\GL_{2n}(\mathbb{C})
preserves the standard pinning and fixes
\Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{C})
point-wise.

On the other hand, we may consider the symmetric
\U_{2n}
-variety

\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\Res_{E/k}(\GL_{n})\backslash\U_{2n},

where
E/k
is the quadratic extension of
k
over which
{\mathrm{G}}
splits. One may then show that
\mathrm{X}_{E}\simeq\mathrm{X}_{E}^{\prime}
, where the subscript denotes the base change to
\Spec(E)
, and that
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
induces the same distinguished morphism (
1.1
). As explored in
[
CG21
]
, these two varieties give rise to deeply related but distinct arithmetic problems in the local and global relative Langlands program.∎

1.3.1.
Outer forms of
\mathrm{X}

Suppose now that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split over
k
and
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
is a spherical variety of
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
. We will say that
\overline{\mathrm{X}}

descends to
k
if there exists a
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety
\mathrm{X}
such that
\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}\simeq\overline{\mathrm{X}}
. When
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
descends to
k
the isomorphism classes of
{\mathrm{G}}
-forms of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
are a torsor for
H^{1}(k,\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
, where

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\simeq\mathrm{H}\backslash N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})

is the group of
{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariant automorphisms of
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
.

Recall that the Langlands
L
-group
{}^{L}{\mathrm{G}}
only determines the inner class of
{\mathrm{G}}
. Thus, our goal is to enhance (
1.1
) so as to determine
\mathrm{X}
up to “
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner twist,” in a sense to be clarified presently. For any such
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
there is a canonical morphism

\out_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{G}}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\Out(\mathrm{H});

we set
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
to be the image of this map
1
1

1

More canonically, we can work with the limit of such groups over
x\in\mathrm{X}(k)
, which is denoted
\Out(\mathrm{X})
. We say that two
{\mathrm{G}}
-varieties
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
are
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner twists if there is a
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
-equivariant isomorphism
\psi:\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{X}^{\prime}_{\overline{k}}
such that the
1
-cocycle
\sigma\in\Gamma\mapsto\psi^{-1}{}^{\sigma}\psi
takes values in

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}:=\ker[\out_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{G}}].

It is straightforward to see that if
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\mathrm{H}^{\prime}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is another
k
-form of
\mathrm{X}
and we consider the corresponding class
[X^{\prime}]\in H^{1}(k,\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
, then
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}
is an inner form of
\mathrm{H}
if and only if the image of
[\mathrm{X}^{\prime}]
in
H^{1}(k,\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H}))
is trivial. However, this result depends on the choice of
\mathrm{X}
as a
k
-forms of
\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}
.

To remedy this, we introduce a quotient
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\to\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
called the group of “doubling automorphisms” of
\mathrm{X}
. When
\mathrm{H}
is connected, this group is implicit in the arguments of
[
Los09
]
and essentially encodes the actions of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
on
\B
-orbits of
\mathrm{X}
; see Remark
3.20
and Example
3.21
. We develop a different definition when
\mathrm{H}
is disconnected that enjoys better functorial properties in Section
3.4.2
. In Section
3.4.3
, we associate to any
k
-rational spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety a
1
-cocycle
\mu_{\mathrm{X}}:\Gamma\longrightarrow\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})(\overline{k})
, referred to as the
geometric cocycle
for
\mathrm{X}
. Moreover, we show in Lemma
3.14
and Corollary
3.18
that
H^{1}(k,\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}})\to H^{1}(k,\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}))
is surjective most cases of interest (we conjecture this always holds in Conjecture
3.17
).

We say that
\mathrm{X}
is
well adapted
if the map
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\to\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
is an isomorphism. In this setting, two
k
-forms
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
are
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner twists of each other if and only if their geometric cocycles are cohomologous.

It turns out that not all spherical varieties are well adapted. Nevertheless, we completely determine which symmetric varieties are well adapted in Theorem
6.1
. A nice consequence is that if
\mathrm{X}
has no roots of type
N
, then it is well-adapted. Along the way, we also show that when a symmetric variety
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is well adapted, there exists a
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner twist
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
possessing a point
x\in\mathrm{X}^{\prime}(k)
such that
\mathrm{Stab}_{{\mathrm{G}}}(x)
is the
quasi-split
inner form of
\mathrm{H}
; see Theorem
6.4
. Proving these two statements involves reducing to the case where
{\mathrm{G}}
is
k
-simple and simply connected and appealing to the classification of symmetric varieties over
\overline{k}
. This argument depends on an extension of the notion of a
z
-extension to the case of symmetric varieties in Proposition
5.3
; in fact, this construction works more generally for any finite order automorphism of
{\mathrm{G}}
and may be of independent interest.

1.4.
Endoscopic symmetric varieties

We may now state our main existence theorem. We refer the reader to Section
8.2
for any undefined terms. Recall that to each spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety
\mathrm{X}
, there is a well-defined notion of “canonical torus”
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
, which encodes the possible Borel semiinvariant rational functions on
\mathrm{X}
; see Section
3.1
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Assume that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a symmetric space associated to a
k
-rational involution
\theta
of
{\mathrm{G}}
. Assume also that
\mathrm{X}
is well-adapted. Let
2
2

2

See Section
8.2
for the definition of
\hat{\mathrm{X}}^{\heartsuit}
. This condition essentially requires that the
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-factor of (
1.1
) factors through
{\mathrm{G}}_{x}
. In particular, endoscopic varieties are as “non-tempered” as
\mathrm{X}
.

x\in\mathrm{X}^{\heartsuit,\Gamma}
. Let
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
be the (quasi-split) endoscopic group associated to
\mathfrak{e}_{x}:=(\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{x}^{\circ},x,\eta)
. There exists an
k
-rational involution
\theta_{\mathfrak{e}}:{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
and a
k
-rational subgroup
({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\theta_{\mathfrak{e}}})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta_{\mathfrak{e}}}_{\mathfrak{e}})
such that if
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}:=\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is the associated symmetric variety,

(1)

\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}=\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}}
(up to a Galois
1
-cocycle valued in the little Weyl group
W_{\hat{\mathrm{X}}}
);

(2)

the dual sequence

\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}}\longrightarrow\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}}\longrightarrow\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathfrak{e}}

of the
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
-variety
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is conjugate by an element of
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
to the top row of (
8.3
);

(3)

there is a canonical morphism
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})\to\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}})
.

If Conjecture
3.17
holds for
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
, then
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
may be chosen so that its geometric
1
-cocycle
\mu_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}}:\Gamma\longrightarrow\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}})
is cohomologous to the cocycle obtained by

(1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathrm{X},\mathfrak{e}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathrm{X}}}

When
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is well-adapted, this determines it up to
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
-inner form.

Remark 1.5
.

We verify that Conjecture
3.17
holds if
\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is connected (Lemma
3.14
) and in many natural settings when
\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is not connected (cf. Corollary
3.18
).

Our proof of this relies heavily on our restriction to symmetric varieties, where we may consider first the existence of the involution over the algebraic closure and then consider questions pertaining to the fine structure of
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
over
k
as in the theorem. For this, we show in Section
4.4
that there is a good notion of endoscopic root systems with involution; this essentially shows the existence of
\theta_{\mathfrak{e}}
(equivalently, the symmetric variety
\overline{\mathrm{X}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
) over
\overline{k}
. In Proposition
4.10
, we utilize the more subtle notion of a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index from
[
Hel00
]
to descend this involution to
k
(see Remark
5.11
). This corresponds to the statement that the symmetric variety
\overline{\mathrm{X}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
descends to
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
over
k
. This is not sufficient for the purposes of endoscopy, and the additional constraint on the geometric cocycle isolates the appropriate inner class of
k
-forms. Finally, Theorem
6.4
shows that one may typically isolate a unique form in a given
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
-inner class possessing points with quasi-split stabilizers. We remark that all of these conditions are satisfied if
\mathrm{X}
has no spherical roots of type
N
.

With this theorem, we formulate the notion of an endoscopic datum for
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{X})
as a quintuple

{\mathfrak{e}}=({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}},\theta_{\mathfrak{e}},\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa,\eta_{\mathfrak{e}}),

where
({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa,,\eta_{\mathfrak{e}})
is a pure endoscopic triple for
{\mathrm{G}}
requiring the semi-simple element
\kappa\in\check{{\mathrm{G}}}^{ss}
to satisfy several constraints,
\theta_{\mathfrak{e}}
is the
k
-rational involution on
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
as in the theorem, and
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is the corresponding symmetric variety. The data of
\theta_{\mathfrak{e}}
is technically not unique up to isomorphism due to the issue of pure inner forms (see Definition
8.9
), but we include it to incorporate the existence and uniqueness of the quasi-split
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
-inner form (Theorem
6.4
).

Remark 1.6
.

We do not assume that
\mathrm{X}
not possess type
N
roots, but only that it be well adapted. As seen in Section
6
, these two notions are not unrelated, but the latter assumption is much weaker.

The presence of such roots can cause the distinguished morphism
\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}
to fail to be injective, and even if it is injective, the construction of
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
requires a certain renormalization which loses information. What saves us is our ability to keep track of the involution
\theta
induced on a maximal torus
\mathrm{A}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
which transfers to
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
.

Example 1.7
.

We show how to recover the notion of endoscopy in
[
Les19b
]
. Let
{\mathrm{G}}=\U_{2n}
be a quasi-split unitary group associated to a quadratic extension
E/k
of fields, and let
\mathrm{X}=\U_{n}\times\U_{n}\backslash\U_{2n}
as before. As noted,
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}=\Sp_{2n}
embeds into
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}=\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
in the obvious way.
This embedding identifies the image with the
\Gamma
-fixed points of
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}
so that
{}^{L}\mathrm{X}=\Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{C})\times\Gal(E/k)
. The dual symmetric variety is thus the variety of non-degenerate alternating forms on
\mathbb{C}^{2n}

\displaystyle\hat{\mathrm{X}}=\Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\GL_{2n}(\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Alt}(\mathbb{C}^{2n})

\displaystyle[g]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\check{\theta}(g)^{-1}g,

with distinguished base point
x_{0}:=J\in\hat{\mathrm{X}}^{\Gal(E/k)}
corresponding to the symplectic form cutting out
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
. In this setting
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})\cong\mu_{2}
and the geometric cocycle
\mu_{\mathrm{X}}:\Gal(E/k)\to\mu_{2}(\overline{k})
is the unique non-trivial character
\omega_{E/k}
.

For each pair
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
such that
a+b=n
, consider the element
x_{a,b}:=s_{a,b}J
with

s_{a,b}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}I_{a}&&\\
&-I_{2b}&\\
&&I_{a}\end{array}\right).

Passing to the descendant of
\hat{\mathrm{X}}
at this point, we obtain
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},a,b}\longrightarrow\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{a,b}
, where

\text{$\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},a,b}=\Sp_{2a}(\mathbb{C})\times\Sp_{2b}(\mathbb{C})$ and $\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{a,b}=\GL_{2a}(\mathbb{C})\times\GL_{2b}(\mathbb{C})$}.

By Theorem
1.4
we obtain the endoscopic datum

{\mathfrak{e}}_{a,b}=({\mathrm{G}}_{a,b},\theta_{a,b},\mathrm{X}_{a,b},x_{a,b},\eta_{a,b}).

where
\mathrm{X}_{a,b}=\mathrm{H}_{a,b}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}_{a,b}
such that
\mathrm{H}_{a,b}=\mathrm{H}_{a}\times\mathrm{H}_{b}
,
{\mathrm{G}}_{a,b}={\mathrm{G}}_{a}\times{\mathrm{G}}_{b}
with

\mathrm{H}_{a}=\U_{a}\times\U_{a}\text{ and }{\mathrm{G}}_{a}=\U_{2a}

and similarly for
\mathrm{H}_{b}
and
{\mathrm{G}}_{b}
. Noting that
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}_{a,b})\simeq\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2}
with
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}_{n})\to\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}_{a,b})
being the diagonal map, this is the unique outer class of
k
-forms satisfying (
1.3
).
Finally, we note that everything above is compatible with the Galois actions, so that we obtain the following Cartesian diagram of
L
-groups

(1.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\mathrm{X}_{a,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}{\mathrm{G}}_{a,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\mathrm{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}{\mathrm{G}}.}

It is not hard to show that all elliptic endoscopic data of
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{X})
are isomorphic to a form of this type. This is compatible with our goal of stabilizing the relative trace formula along transfer from these varieties as discussed in
[
Les19b
,
Les19a
,
Les22
]
.

1.5.
Point comparison and prestabilization

An important property of endoscopic groups
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
of
{\mathrm{G}}
is the point matching of stable semi-simple conjugacy classes of
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
with those of
{\mathrm{G}}
. This is foundational to the notion of smooth transfer of orbital integrals. As evidence for our theory, we establish the existence of such a matching of stable semi-simple orbits in Theorem
8.13
.

Theorem 1.8
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a reductive group over
k
. Let
\theta
be a
k
-rational involution and let
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
be an associated symmetric variety. Let
\mathfrak{e}=({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}},\theta_{\mathfrak{e}},\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa,\eta)
be an endoscopic datum for
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{X})
. There exists a canonical map of affine
k
-schemes

\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}:\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{X}\sslash\mathrm{H}

between the categorical quotients.

After a simple argument extending the Chevalley-style isomorphism for
{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
of
[
Ric82
]
to all
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset N_{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})
(Lemma
8.14
), the proof relies on this isomorphism as well as important structural properties of the dual symmetric variety
\hat{\mathrm{X}}
.

In particular, one may now formulate the notion of matching stable orbits necessary to formulate the local transfer and fundamental lemma conjectures. This requires enhancing the discussion to the stack-theoretic level via the morphism
\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{H}\to\mathrm{X}\sslash\mathrm{H}
, since it is not true in general that we may explicitly relate
k
-points of
\mathrm{X}\sslash\mathrm{H}
to stable semi-simple orbits of
\mathrm{X}(k)
alone. We discuss these notions in more detail in the companion paper
[
Les24
]
.

We expect the preceding theorem will play a role in establishing of analogues of Ngô’s work on geometric stabilization in this context, with a view toward establishing fundamental lemmas such as the one established in
[
Les19a
]
. Building on
[
Les21
,
GPPNR18
]
, forthcoming work of T. Hameister and B. Morrissey
[
HM23
]
completes the structural description of the Hitchin fibration for symmetric pairs and proves analogues of Ngô’s results on the gerbe structure of the Hitchin fibration with respect to a certain stabilizer group scheme when
\mathrm{X}
has abelian regular stabilizers, and one may ask whether there exists in that setting an interpretation of our notion of endoscopic data analogous to Ngô’s re-interpretation
[
Ngo06
]
. We also mention in this direction work in the context of Hitchin fibrations for symmetric pairs and more general Vinberg gradings of
[
GP23
,
GGP23
]
.

In Section
10.2
, we show how Theorem
1.8
allows for the pre-stabilization of the regular elliptic part of the relative trace formula for
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{X})
, subject to simplifying assumptions. The more general statement and proof of the cohomological preliminaries is handled in
[
Les24
]
, where we show that the (regular elliptic part of the) geometric side of the RTF may be written as

\sum_{a\in(\mathrm{X}^{re}\sslash\mathrm{H})(k)}\sum_{\kappa\in H^{1}_{ab}(k,{\mathrm{H}}_{a})^{D}}\Orb_{a}^{\kappa}({f}),

where
\mathrm{X}^{re}
denotes the regular elliptic locus and
{\mathrm{H}}_{a}
denotes the stabilizer of a representative in
\mathrm{X}(k)
of the class
a
(well-defined up to inner form, so that the abelianized cohomology group is well-defined), and
\Orb_{a}^{\kappa}({f})
is an adelic
\kappa
-orbital integral; see Section
10.2
. To illustrate the main application of Theorems
1.4
and
1.8
, we establish Proposition
10.5
which switches the order of summation to give

\sum_{\mathfrak{e}/\sim}\left(\sum_{b\in[\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{re}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}]^{\mathrm{X}}(k)}\Orb_{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}(b)}^{\kappa}({f})\right)

summing over isomorphism classes of elliptic endoscopic data for
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{X})
and the
\mathrm{X}
-regular locus of
[\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{re}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}](k)
. One may now formulate the relevant smooth transfer and fundamental lemma conjectures toward the stabilization of this formula; to reasons of space, we defer this to
[
Les24
]
.

1.6.
Relative Langlands duality and endoscopy

Let us now assume that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
satisfies the constraint that generic (semi-simple) stabilizers are connected; this is related to the notion of “simply connected” variety studied in
[
Les22
]
. In this context, the cotangent bundle
\mathcal{M}=T^{\ast}\mathrm{X}
is a hyperspherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety
3
3

3

For the purposes of this article, we ignore the more technical restrictions and grading discussed in
[
BZSV23
]
.
in the sense of
[
BZSV23
]
. Ben-Zvi, Sakellaridis, and Venkatesh have conjectured the existence of a
dual Hamiltonian
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}
-variety

\check{\mathcal{M}}
, the symplectic geometry of which encodes the arithmetic properties of the various distinction questions associated to the variety
\mathrm{X}
. Section 4 of
ibid.
constructs the putative variety
\check{\mathcal{M}}
for hyperspherical
T^{\ast}\mathrm{X}
, and conjectures its various properties.

A consequence of Theorem
6.1
is that
\mathrm{X}
is well adapted whenever
T^{\ast}\mathrm{X}
is hyperspherical. We show in Section
6.4
and Section
9
that the geometric cocyle
\mu_{\mathrm{X}}
encodes the data of a symplectic representation
S_{\mathrm{X}}
of
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}

together with a natural extension
of this representation to
{}^{L}\mathrm{X}=\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}\rtimes\Gamma
; see Theorem
9.8
. In fact, this holds for any well adapted symmetric variety.

Happily, our construction of the representation
S_{\mathrm{X}}
is identical to the conjectured formula of
[
BZSV23
, Section 4.3]
! Their formula was derived from analysis of the
L
-factors associated to spherical varieties and agrees precisely with the representation arising from our analysis of rational forms of
\mathrm{X}
. As a consequence, we establish some of the conjectures of these authors when
\mathrm{X}
is symmetric and
T^{\ast}\mathrm{X}
is hyperspherical (Section
9.3
). Moreover, we obtain a natural Galois action on
\check{\mathcal{M}}
via the action on
S_{\mathrm{X}}
; this appears vital for applications beyond the context of split groups (cf.
[
GW14
]
). We expect this connection (and the notion of well-adapted variety) will play a role in understanding their construction in greater generality.

One of the points of relative Langlands duality is to give a robust setting in which to study “relative functoriality”; that is, the relationship between distinction questions of various groups through Langlands functoriality. Our construction gives rise to such a setting: given a pair
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{X})
of quasi-split reductive group
{\mathrm{G}}
and symmetric variety, we construct a family of pairs
({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}},\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}})
where
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is an endoscopic group of
{\mathrm{G}}
. A basic assertion is that this construction is compatible with functoriality. For example, when
k
is local we expect that an
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
-distinguished
L
-packet
\Pi
of
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}(k)
lifts under endoscopic transfer to a
\mathrm{X}
-distinguished packet on
{\mathrm{G}}(k)
via functoriality along diagrams generalizing (
1.4
). The natural expectation is that if
\check{\mathcal{M}}
is dual to
T^{\ast}\mathrm{X}
and
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is dual to
T^{\ast}\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
, then there ought to be a (derived) Lagrangian correspondence

\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\longleftarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{e}}\longrightarrow\check{\mathcal{M}}

compatible with the moment maps and the restriction morphism
\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ast}\to\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ast}_{\mathfrak{e}}

Under the assumption that the generic stabilizer of
\mathrm{X}
is abelian and
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
is simple, we verify that this is the case in Proposition
9.14
. The restriction on the generic stabilizers is purely for convenience; we will return to the geometric and symplectic relationship between
\check{\mathcal{M}}
and
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
in future work.

Example 1.9
.

Continuing with the example
{\mathrm{G}}=\U_{2n}
and
\mathrm{X}=\U_{n}\times\U_{n}\backslash\U_{2n}
, we find that
S_{\mathrm{X}}=T^{\ast}(\mathbb{C}^{2n})
, where
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
denotes the standard representation of
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}=\Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{C})
. Thus,

\check{\mathcal{M}}_{n}=T^{\ast}(\Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\GL_{2n}v\times\mathbb{C}^{2n}).

For
\mathrm{X}
as above, the
\Gal(E/k)
action is non-trivial on
S_{\mathrm{X}}
, acting by swapping the two factors of
T^{\ast}(\mathbb{C}^{2n})
. On the other hand, this action is trivial for
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\Res_{E/k}(\GL_{n})\backslash\U_{2n}
. In either case, there is a natural closed immersion

\left[\Sp_{2a}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\GL_{2a}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C}^{2a}\right]\times\left[\Sp_{2b}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\GL_{2b}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C}^{2b}\right]\subset\Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\GL_{2n}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C}^{2n},

giving rise to the Lagrangian correspondence

\check{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\times\check{\mathcal{M}}_{b}\longleftarrow\mathcal{L}_{a,b}\longrightarrow\check{\mathcal{M}}_{n},

with
\mathcal{L}_{a,b}
the fiber product over this closed embedding.

In Section 14 of
[
BZSV23
]
, the authors formulate local and global conjectures relating
\mathrm{H}
-invariant forms (or
\mathrm{H}
-period integrals) to fixed points of Langlands parameters on
\check{\mathcal{M}}
. We hope the results of the present work will aid in establishing these conjectures, at least for varieties related to symmetric varieties. In the number field setting, this can only be a guiding principle at the moment. Nevertheless, Section
10.1
discusses the relation between the endoscopy for the Galois symmetric variety
\U_{n}\backslash\Res_{E/k}(\GL_{n})
, unitary periods of Eisenstein series, and the fundamental lemma proved in
[
Les19a
]
.

1.7.
Outline

We end with a brief summary of the papers contents. We begin with some notation and conventions in Section
2
. In Section
3
, we recall the necessary generalities of spherical varieties as well as the rationality results of Borovoi and Gagliardi. In Section
3.4
, we introduce the group of doubling automorphisms, the geometric cocycle, and the notion of well adaptedness. In Section
4
, we recall certain generalities on involutions on root systems, including the notions of
\theta
-index and
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index due to
[
Hel00
]
. The new material is our introduction of endoscopic root systems with involution in Section
4.4
, which is the combinatorial core of the existence result in Theorem
1.4
. Section
5
elucidates the spherical data of symmetric varieties at length. Particular attention is paid to the explicit calculation of the various root systems associated to symmetric varieties. We also show that the notion of
z
-extension generalizes quite naturally to the setting of symmetric varieties in Proposition
5.3
. Section
5.4
includes the necessary results regarding
(\Gamma,\theta)
-indices.

Section
6
proves Theorems
6.1
and
6.4
, calculating the group
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
for all symmetric varieties and showing that in all well-adapted cases the quasi-split
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner form exists over
k
. The argument proceeds via a reduction to the absolutely simple case, where we ultimately compute the various groups case-by-case.

In Section
7.1
, we recall the dual group of a spherical variety, its construction and properties. We also refine the functoriality of the dual group in Appendix
A
. We then introduce the dual symmetric variety
\hat{\mathrm{X}}
in Section
7.2
, and prove its basic properties in Section
7.3
. Section
8
defines and proves the existence of endoscopic symmetric varieties. Section
8.4
then establishes the foundational result on point matching for these varieties.

In Section
9
, we show that under the additional assumption that
\mathrm{H}
is geometrically connected the geometric cocycle constructed in Section
3.2
is completely encoded by a symplectic representation of
{}^{L}\mathrm{X}
, with the cocycle determining the Galois action. This is Theorem
9.8
. Restricting further to the case of so-called excellent symmetric varieties, we discuss the compatibility of our construction with the dual Hamiltonian variety of
[
BZSV23
]
. Finally, Section
10
discusses examples of unitary periods and relations to endoscopy, establishes the pre-stabilization of the regular elliptic part of the RTF in Section
10.2
, and ends with examples of endoscopic varieties when
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
is
k
-simple in Table
4
.

1.8.

[
Les19a
]
in the context of
[
BZSV23
]
, and Sol Friedberg for several helpful remarks. Part I
{\mathrm{G}}
-outer forms and symmetric varieties

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Groups and varieties

We use
k
for a field, which we always assume does not have characteristic
2
. We generally assume that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a connected reductive group over a field
k
. We will either assume that
k
has characteristic zero or good characteristic for
{\mathrm{G}}
. We let
\overline{k}
denote a fixed algebraic closure of
k
and
k^{sep}\subset\overline{k}
the separable closure. Throughout much of the paper,
\Gamma=\Gal(k^{sep}/k)
.

We let
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
denote the derive subgroup of
{\mathrm{G}}
,
{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}
its adjoint quotient, and
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
the simply connected cover of
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
. We write
Z({\mathrm{G}})
for the center, and
Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
for the centralizer of a subgroup
\mathrm{H}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
.

For a spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety
\mathrm{X}
, we set
\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})
to the group
k
-scheme of
{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariant automorphisms (cf.
[
Los09
,
Kno96
]
). In particular, if
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is homogeneous, then

\Aut^{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{X})=\mathrm{H}\backslash N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H}),

where
N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
denotes the normalizer of
\mathrm{H}
in
{\mathrm{G}}
.

If
\mathrm{H}
is acted on by a group of automorphisms
\Gamma_{\mathrm{H}}\subset\Gamma
, we denote by
\Ind_{\Gamma/\Gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}(\mathrm{H})
the induced group
[
Bor79
, Section 4]
.

Now assume that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is symmetric, in the sense that there is a
k
-rational involutive automorphism
\theta
of
{\mathrm{G}}
such that

({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta});

we always assume that
\mathrm{H}
is smooth (automatic if
\mathrm{H}
connected or if
\mathrm{char}(k)
is zero). When
\mathrm{char}(k)\neq 2
is positive, we always assume that it is good for
{\mathrm{G}}
. When
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
is absolutely simple, this is equivalent to

•

\mathrm{char}(k)\neq 2
if
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
is of Cartan types
A
,
B
,
C
, or
D
,

•

\mathrm{char}(k)\neq 2,3
if
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
is of Cartan types
G_{2}
,
F_{4}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
,

•

\mathrm{char}(k)\neq 2,3,5
if
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
is of Cartan type
E_{8}
,

In general,
p
is good for
{\mathrm{G}}
if it is good for each irreducible factor of
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
This will be our standard assumption throughout, though it is not optimal for much of the paper (for which it suffices to assume
\mathrm{char}(k)\neq 2
). It is made to ensure that our use of certain calculations regarding the nilpotent cone for symmetric varieties in positive characteristic (see
[
Lev07
]
) behave similarly to the characteristic zero setting.

If
\mathrm{A}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
is a torus that is
\theta
-stable, we abuse notation and use
\theta
to denote the induced involution on the character lattice
\theta:X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})\to X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
and
\check{\theta}
the involution on
X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
.

2.2.
Invariant theory

For any field
k
and any non-singular affine algebraic variety
\mathrm{X}
over
k
with
\mathrm{G}
a connected reductive algebraic group over
k
acting algebraically on
\mathrm{X}
, we set
\mathrm{X}^{rss}
to be the invariant-theoretic regular semi-simple locus. That is,
x\in\mathrm{X}^{rss}(\overline{k})
if and only if its
\mathrm{G}_{\overline{k}}
-orbit is of maximal possible dimension and Zariski-closed. We also recall the semi-simple locus
\mathrm{X}^{ss}
of points with Zariski-closed orbits.

Definition 2.1
.

Let an algebraic group
{\mathrm{G}}
act on an algebraic variety
\mathrm{X}
. A pair consisting of an algebraic variety
{\mathrm{Y}}
and a
{\mathrm{G}}
-invariant morphism
\pi:\mathrm{X}\to{\mathrm{Y}}
is called the
categorical quotient
of
\mathrm{X}
by the action of
{\mathrm{G}}
if for any other such pair
(\pi^{\prime},{\mathrm{Y}}^{\prime})
, there exists a unique morphism
\varphi:{\mathrm{Y}}\to{\mathrm{Y}}^{\prime}
such that
\pi^{\prime}=\varphi\circ\pi
. Clearly, if a categorical quotient exists, it is unique up to a unique isomorphism with respect to the obvious notion of isomorphism between pairs. We will denote it by
(\pi_{\mathrm{X}},\mathrm{X}\sslash{\mathrm{G}})
; by a common abuse of terminology, we often drop reference to the morphism
\pi_{\mathrm{X}}
and call
\mathrm{X}\sslash{\mathrm{G}}
the categorical quotient.

Proposition 2.2
.

[
AG09
, Theorem 2.2.2]

Suppose
{\mathrm{G}}
is (not-necessarily connected) reductive and
\mathrm{X}
affine. Then the categorical quotient
\mathrm{X}\sslash{\mathrm{G}}
always exists and is given by
\Spec(k[\mathrm{X}]^{{\mathrm{G}}})
. Moreover, every fiber of
\pi_{\mathrm{X}}
contains a unique Zariski-closed orbit.

Suppose now that
{\mathrm{G}}
is reductive over a field
k
and
\mathrm{X}
is an affine
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety with categorical quotient
\mathrm{X}\sslash{\mathrm{G}}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is another (possibly disconnected, but smooth) reductive group with an algebraic action on
\mathrm{X}
commuting with
{\mathrm{G}}
. Then one obtains an algebraic action

(2.1)

m^{\prime}\sslash{\mathrm{G}}:\mathcal{A}\times\mathrm{X}\sslash{\mathrm{G}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{X}\sslash{\mathrm{G}},

by noting that
\mathcal{A}\times\mathrm{X}\sslash{\mathrm{G}}
is the categorical quotient of
\mathcal{A}\times\mathrm{X}
by
{\mathrm{G}}
, where
{\mathrm{G}}
acts trivially on
\mathcal{A}
. With this, one applies the universal property to the action map
\mathcal{A}\times\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{X}
composed with
\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{X}\sslash{\mathrm{G}}
to obtain the morphism (
2.1
), and verifies that the appropriate diagrams commute. This is straightforward.

Lemma 2.3
.

Setting
{\mathrm{Y}}=\mathrm{X}\sslash{\mathrm{G}}
, the categorical quotients
\mathrm{X}\sslash\mathcal{A}
and
{\mathrm{Y}}\sslash\mathcal{A}
exist and sit in a commutative diagram of
k
-schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{X}\sslash\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Y}}\sslash\mathcal{A},}

where each arrow is the corresponding quotient map.

Proof.

If the two claimed quotients exist, the diagram exists by properties of the categorical quotient and repeated applications of the existence of the embedding (
2.1
). The quotients exist by Proposition
2.2
since by assumption
\mathcal{A}
is reductive.
∎

2.3.
The
\ast
-action and the
L
-group

Assume now that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a reductive group over
k
and let
\mathrm{A}
denote a maximal torus. If
\mathrm{SAut}({\mathrm{G}})
denotes the set of semi-linear automorphisms of
{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
(cf.
[
Bor20
]
), the
k
-rational structure of
{\mathrm{G}}
induces a morphism

\mu:\Gamma\longrightarrow\mathrm{SAut}({\mathrm{G}}),

which splits the sequence

1\longrightarrow\Aut({\mathrm{G}})(\overline{k})\longrightarrow\mathrm{SAut}({{\mathrm{G}}})\longrightarrow\Gamma.

As explained in
[
Bor20
]
, this induces a
\Gamma
-action on
{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
, which we refer to as
\mu_{\sigma}
for
\sigma\in\Gamma
.

Over the algebraic closure
\overline{k}
, there exists a Borel subgroup
\B\supset\mathrm{A}
, unique up to
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{A})
-conjugacy. Fixing one gives rise to a based root datum
\Psi=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Delta,X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\check{\Delta})
.

For any
\sigma\in\Gamma
, there exists
g_{\sigma}\in{\mathrm{G}}(k^{sep})
such that
\sigma(\B,\mathrm{A})=\Ad(g_{\sigma})(\B,\mathrm{A})
. This induces a
\Gamma
-action on
\Psi
.
Fixing a pinning
\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Delta},
where
x_{\alpha}\in U_{\alpha}(k^{sep})
is a root vector for each simple root realizes

\Aut(\Psi({\mathrm{G}}))\simeq\Aut({\mathrm{G}},\B,\mathrm{A},\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha}),

as a subgroup of
\Aut({\mathrm{G}})
is a manner that splits the short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\mathrm{Inn}({\mathrm{G}})\longrightarrow\Aut({\mathrm{G}})\overset{\psi}{\longrightarrow}\Aut(\Psi({\mathrm{G}}))\longrightarrow 1.

Augmenting
\sigma\mapsto\Ad(g_{\sigma})
by a cocycle valued in
\mathrm{A}
if necessary to ensure that

\mu_{\sigma}(\B,\mathrm{A},\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha})=\Ad(g_{\sigma})(\B,\mathrm{A},\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha}),

the assignment
\mu_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\sigma)=\Ad(g_{\sigma})^{-1}\circ\mu_{\sigma}
thus gives a homomorphism
\mu_{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\ast}:\Gamma\longrightarrow\mathrm{SAut}({{\mathrm{G}}})
such that the corresponding
k
-form
{\mathrm{G}}^{\ast}
is quasi-split. The induced
\Gamma
-action on
\Psi
is known as the
\ast
-action
.

Passing to the dual based root datum
\check{\Psi}:=(X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\check{\Delta},X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Delta)
, let
{\check{{\mathrm{G}}}}
be the Langlands dual complex reductive group associated to this datum. Since there is a canonical isomorphism

\Aut(\Psi({\mathrm{G}}))\simeq\Aut(\check{\Psi}({\check{{\mathrm{G}}}})),

we obtain a homomorphism
\mu^{\vee}_{{\mathrm{G}}}:\Gamma\longrightarrow\Aut(\check{\Psi}({\check{{\mathrm{G}}}}))
, and upon fixing a pinning
\{x_{\check{\alpha}}\}
for
(\check{B},\check{T})
, we obtain an action of
\Gamma
on
(\check{{\mathrm{G}}},\check{B},\check{T},\{x_{\check{\alpha}}\})
. The (Galois form of the)
L
-group

{}^{L}{\mathrm{G}}:=\check{{\mathrm{G}}}\rtimes\Gamma

is the corresponding semi-direct product.

2.4.
Galois actions on groups and varieties

With
{\mathrm{G}}
as before, assume
\mathrm{X}
is a
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety over
k
. These
k
-rational structures induce morphisms

\mu:\Gamma\longrightarrow\mathrm{SAut}({\mathrm{G}}),\qquad\mu_{\mathrm{X}}:\Gamma\longrightarrow\mathrm{SAut}^{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{X})

with the first morphism as above and the second morphism satisfies
\mu
-equivariance and
\mathrm{SAut}^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})
denotes the group of
\mu
-equivariant semi-linear automorphisms of
\mathrm{X}
as in
[
BG22
, Section 2.10]
. That is, for each
\sigma\in\Gamma
,

(2.2)

\mu_{\mathrm{X},\sigma}(g\cdot x)=\mu_{\sigma}(g)\cdot\mu_{\mathrm{X},\sigma}(x).

The
k
-rational structure of
{\mathrm{G}}
induces an exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})(\overline{k})\longrightarrow\mathrm{SAut}^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\Gamma,

which
\mu_{\mathrm{X}}
splits. These morphisms induce
\Gamma
-actions on
{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
and
\mathrm{X}(\overline{k})
, such that for each
\sigma\in\Gamma
, (
2.2
) holds.

2.4.1.
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner vs.
{\mathrm{G}}
-outer forms

Assume now that
\mathrm{X}
is
{\mathrm{G}}
-homogeneous with
\mathrm{X}(k)\neq\emptyset
. Fix a base point
x_{0}\in\mathrm{X}(k)
, and let
\Stab_{{\mathrm{G}}}(x_{0})=\mathrm{H}
. This induces an isomorphism
\mathrm{X}\simeq\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
identifying
\mathrm{H}\cdot 1=x_{0}
. A standard calculation shows that

\mathrm{X}(k)=\bigsqcup_{s\in\ker^{1}(\mathrm{H},{\mathrm{G}};k)}x_{s}\cdot{\mathrm{G}}(k),

where
\ker^{1}(\mathrm{H},{\mathrm{G}};k)=\ker[H^{1}(k,\mathrm{H})\to H^{1}(k,{\mathrm{G}})]
and
x_{s}\in\mathrm{X}(k)
satisfying that
\mathrm{H}_{s}=\mathrm{Stab}_{{\mathrm{G}}}(x_{s})
is obtaining by twisting
\mathrm{H}
by a
1
-cocycle in the class
s
. In particular, all stabilizers are pure inner twists of
\mathrm{H}
.

Having fixed
x_{0}
, the normalizer
N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
of
\mathrm{H}
has a natural map to
\Aut(\mathrm{H})
via the conjugation action, which we may compose with the canonical quotient to obtain a
k
-morphism

\mathrm{out}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\mathrm{G}}}:\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\Out(\mathrm{H});

here we rely on the
k
-isomorphism
\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})\simeq\mathrm{H}\backslash N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
. We claim that this morphism does not depend on the choice of
x_{0}\in\mathrm{X}(k)
. Indeed, for any other
x\in\mathrm{X}(k)
with
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}=\Stab_{{\mathrm{G}}}(x)
, there exists
g\in{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
satisfying
x_{0}=x\cdot g^{-1}
so that
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}=g\mathrm{H}g^{-1}
. Then
\Ad(g)
induces in a isomorphism
\Aut(\mathrm{H})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Aut(\mathrm{H}^{\prime})
such that changing
g\mapsto hg
composes
\Ad(g)
with an inner automorphism of
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}
, so that the induced isomorphism
\Out(\mathrm{H})\to\Out(\mathrm{H}^{\prime})
is independent of the choice of
g
. Combining with
N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H}^{\prime})=gN_{G}(\mathrm{H})g^{-1}
, we find that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}\backslash N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{\prime}\backslash N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out(\mathrm{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out(\mathrm{H}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ad(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ad(g)}

commutes. Let
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
denote the image. One may thus define

\Out(\mathrm{X}):=\lim_{x}\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H}_{x})

and
\out:\Aut^{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{X})\to\Out(\mathrm{X})
is independent of the choice of base point. Denote by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}\subset\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})
the kernel of this map.

We will often fix
x_{0}\in\mathrm{X}(k)
and work with
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
below. Then both
\mathcal{A}^{\flat}_{\mathrm{X}}
and
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
are defined over
k
with the natural inclusion
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\subset\mathrm{Out}(\mathrm{H})
being defined over
k
. The following lemma is straightforward.

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
and
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
as above. The map
\mathrm{out}:=\mathrm{out}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\mathrm{G}}}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant for the natural action on
\Out(\mathrm{H})
. Finally,
\mathcal{A}^{\flat}_{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})
is normal inside
\mathrm{SAut}^{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{X})
.

In particular, if we define
\mathrm{S}\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\subset\mathrm{SOut}(\mathrm{H})
as
\mathrm{S}\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})=\mathrm{SAut}(\mathrm{X})/\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}(\overline{k})
, there is a morphism

\tilde{\mu}_{\mathrm{X}}:\Gamma\longrightarrow\mathrm{SOut}_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})

fitting into a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})(\overline{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SAut}^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})(\overline{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{S}\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mu}_{\mathrm{X}}}

Suppose now that
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
is another
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety such that there exists a
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
-equivariant isomorphism

\psi:\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{X}^{\prime}_{\overline{k}}.

satisfying that for each
\sigma\in\Gamma

\psi(\mu_{\sigma}(g\cdot x))=\nu_{\sigma}(g)\cdot\psi(\mu_{\sigma}(x)).

We say that
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
is a
{\mathrm{G}}
-form
of
\mathrm{X}
. If
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}(k)\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}\simeq\mathrm{H}^{\prime}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
for some subgroup
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
.

Remark 2.5
.

Note that
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
being
{\mathrm{G}}
-forms is more restrictive than
\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}\simeq\mathrm{X}^{\prime}_{\overline{k}}
. They are required to be isomorphic in a
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
equivariant way. Indeed, for
\mathrm{H}_{1}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
and
\mathrm{H}_{2}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
to be
{\mathrm{G}}
-forms, it is necessary that
\mathrm{H}_{1}=g\mathrm{H}_{2}g^{-1}
for some
g\in{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})

[
Bor20
, Lemma 4.1]
.

Fix a
{\mathrm{G}}
-form
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
and isomorphism
\psi
as above. For any
\sigma\in\Gamma
, the automorphism
\psi^{-1}\circ{}^{\sigma}\psi:\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}
is
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
-equivariant. Here
{}^{\sigma}\psi(x):=\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{\sigma}\circ\psi(\mu_{\sigma^{-1}}(x))
, where
\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}
denotes the corresponding semi-linear automorphism of
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
. We thus obtain a cocycle

c_{\mathrm{X},\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}:=[\sigma\longrightarrow\psi^{-1}\circ{}^{\sigma}\psi]\in Z^{1}(k,\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})).

Augmenting the choice of isomorphism
\psi
via automorphisms corresponds to twisting the cocycle by a coboundary, so we obtain a cohomology class
[c_{\mathrm{X},\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}]\in H^{1}(k,\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X}))
which depends only on
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
.

Definition 2.6
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
and let
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\mathrm{H}^{\prime}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
be a
{\mathrm{G}}
-form of
\mathrm{X}
. We say
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
is a
{\mathrm{G}}
-
inner form
if there exists a choice of
\psi
such that the cocycle
c_{\mathrm{X},\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}\in Z^{1}(k,\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X}))
takes values in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}(\overline{k}).

A
{\mathrm{G}}
-form of
\mathrm{X}
which is not a
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner form is called a
{\mathrm{G}}
-outer form
of
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a reductive
k
-group and suppose that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a homogeneous
k
-variety and let
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
be a
{\mathrm{G}}
-form of
\mathrm{X}
. The cocycle
c_{\mathrm{X},\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}
induces the trivial cohomology class in
H^{1}(k,\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H}))
if and only if
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
are
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner forms.

Proof.

This is immediate by considering the long exact sequence in Galois cohomology associated to the commutative diagram with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{ad}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut(\mathrm{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out(\mathrm{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.\qed}

For any
k
-subgroup
\mathrm{H}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
, recall the notation

\ker^{1}(\mathrm{H},{\mathrm{G}};k):=\ker[H^{1}(k,\mathrm{H})\to H^{1}(k,{\mathrm{G}})].

The following is an easy exercise.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
{\mathrm{G}}
be a reductive group over
k
,
\mathrm{H}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
as
k
-rational reductive subgroup, and
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. Set
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}=\mathrm{H}\backslash N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
.

(1)

There is a natural exact sequence

\ker^{1}(\mathrm{H},{\mathrm{G}};k)\to\ker^{1}(N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{H}),{\mathrm{G}};k)\to H^{1}(k,\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}).

(2)

If
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}=\ker[\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\to\Out(\mathrm{H})]
, and
\mathrm{H}\subset\mathrm{H}^{\flat}\subset N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{H})
is the subgroup corresponding to
\mathcal{A}^{\flat}_{\mathrm{X}}
, then there is a natural exact sequence
sequence

\ker^{1}(\mathrm{H},{\mathrm{G}};k)\to\ker^{1}(\mathrm{H}^{\flat},{\mathrm{G}};k)\to H^{1}(k,\mathcal{A}^{\flat}_{\mathrm{X}}).

In particular, two
{\mathrm{G}}
-forms
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\mathrm{H}^{\prime}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
and
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
are
{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariantly isomorphic if and only if
\mathrm{H}
to
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}
are
pure inner forms
with
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}
corresponding to a class in
\ker^{1}(\mathrm{H},{\mathrm{G}};k)
.

We remark that the preceding claim relies on the notion of
{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariant isomorphism
\psi:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
, which requires the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{G}}\times\mathrm{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}

to commute. If we allow more general automorphisms of
{\mathrm{G}}
in the preceding diagram, certain
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner forms may be shown to be isomorphic to
\mathrm{X}
.

3.
Spherical data and automorphisms

In this section, we recall generalities of spherical varieties, their combinatorial data, and rationality properties. We then analyze certain quotients of the automorphism group of a homogeneous spherical variety in Section
3.4
. The main tool developed is the notion of geometric cocycle in Definition
3.19
.

3.1.
Generalities

Let
{\mathrm{G}}
be a connected reductive group over a field
k
, which for this section we assume has characteristic zero. This is essentially for convenience as our main references
[
Los09
,
BG22
]
work in this setting. However, most of these results hold with mild assumptions on the residue characteristic. In any case, we discuss the positive characteristic setting for symmetric varieties in Section
5
.

Fix a Borel subgroup
\B
with
\mathrm{A}=\B/[\B,\B]
the canonical Cartan of
{\mathrm{G}}
. The canonicity of the quotient implies that
\mathrm{A}
inherits a unique
k
-structure, and we use
\mathrm{A}
to denote the resulting
k
-torus. In particular, for any maximally
k
-split maximal torus
T\subset{\mathrm{G}}
there is a
k
-isomorphism
T\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{A}
. Let
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})=X^{\ast}(\B)
denote the character lattice, with
\Delta\subset\Phi^{+}\subset\Phi
be the associated simple and positive roots. Then we have the based root datum
\Psi:=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Delta,X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\check{\Delta})
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a homogeneous spherical variety over
k
. Let
\mathring{\mathrm{X}}
denote the open
B
-orbit. Then
\mathrm{A}
acts on
\mathring{\mathrm{X}}\sslash[B,B]
through a quotient
\mathrm{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
; we call the torus
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
the (canonical) torus of
\mathrm{X}
.

We let
\mathfrak{X}:=X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
denote the weight lattice of
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
. It corresponds to the lattice of
B
-semiinvariants

0\longrightarrow\overline{k}^{\times}\longrightarrow\overline{k}(\mathrm{X})^{(B)}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{X}\longrightarrow 0.

We note that since we are not assuming that
k
is algebraically closed, this should be viewed as a sequence of
\Gamma
-modules. We also have the
spherical weights
of
\mathrm{X}

\mathfrak{X}^{+}:=\{\lambda\in X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})^{+}:V(\lambda)_{\overline{k}}^{\mathrm{H}_{\overline{k}}}\neq 0\},

which satisfies

\overline{k}[\mathrm{X}]=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathfrak{X}^{+}}V(\lambda).

One considers the set
\mathcal{V}:=\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{X})
of
central discrete valuations
on
\overline{k}(\mathrm{X})
; that is, valuations
\nu:\overline{k}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
that are
{\mathrm{G}}
-invariant and trivial on
\B
-invariants
\overline{k}(\mathrm{X})^{\B}=\overline{k}
. On any
\B
-semiinvariant, such a valuation depends only on the corresponding character, so that there is a map

\mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X},\mathbb{Q}}:=\Hom(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{Q}).

It is known that this map is injective and that the image realizes
\mathcal{V}
as a finitely-generated convex cone which is not contained in any hyperplane. Indeed, it gives a chamber for the so-called little Weyl group
W_{\mathrm{X}}
, which acts on
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X},\mathbb{Q}}
.

Let
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}:=\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{s}\}\subset\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}
be a minimal set of “outward normal vectors” such that

\mathcal{V}:=\{a\in\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X},\mathbb{Q}}:a(\gamma_{i})\leq 0,\>\>i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}\};

these are the
spherical roots
of
\mathrm{X}
. At the moment, these roots are only defined up to a rational scalar.

3.1.1.
Normalizations of spherical roots

For our purposes, there are
3
natural root systems associated to
\mathrm{X}
, each of which plays a distinct role in the structure theory. Let
W_{\mathrm{X}}
denote the little Weyl group of
\mathrm{X}
; all root systems have this as their Weyl group, and differ only in replacing simple roots with scalar multiples.

(1)

The
n
-spherical roots:
Suppose that
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in\mathfrak{X}^{+}
such that
V(\nu)\subset V(\lambda)\cdot V(\mu)
in
\overline{k}[\mathrm{X}]
. Denote

\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}^{+}:=\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\{\lambda+\mu-\nu:V(\nu)\subset V(\lambda)\cdot V(\mu)\}.

A theorem of Knop states that this is a free monoid; let
\Delta^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}
denote its (unique) set of free generators. This is the set of
n
-spherical roots of
\mathrm{X}
, and we let
\Phi^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}=W_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot\Delta^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}
.

Remark 3.1
.

Knop shows in
[
Kno96
]
that the
root lattice

\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}:=\mathbb{Z}\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}^{+}
of
\mathrm{X}
is precisely the kernel of

X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})\longrightarrow X^{\ast}(\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})),

at least when
\mathrm{X}
is quasi-affine.

(2)

The minimal root system:
By construction, we have inclusion

\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}\subset\mathfrak{X}.

However,
\Delta^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}
need not be primitive in
\mathfrak{X}
. Knop defines
\Delta^{min}_{\mathrm{X}}
as the set of primitive elements in
\mathfrak{X}
associated to the extremal rays of the cone generated by
\Delta^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}
in
\mathfrak{X}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
. Set
\Phi^{min}_{\mathrm{X}}=W_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot\Delta^{min}_{\mathrm{X}}
.

Remark 3.2
.

We take this to be the definition of
spherical roots
of
\mathrm{X}
, and set
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}:=\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{min}
.

(3)

The normalized root system:
Motivated by integrality constraints imposed by Langlands functoriality (see Section
7.1
), Sakellaridis–Venkatesh introduce an additional normalization in
[
SV17
]
. The
normalized spherical roots
of
\mathrm{X}
, denoted as
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{sv}
, are obtained by replacing
\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
with the corresponding primitive elements in the root lattice of
{\mathrm{G}}
,
\mathbb{Z}\Phi\subset X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
.

With this definition, it may happen that
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{sv}\not\subset\mathfrak{X}
, so one must also re-normalize

{\mathfrak{X}}^{sv}:=\mathfrak{X}+\mathbb{Z}\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{sv}.

Relying on work of Brion, there is a natural trichotomy of spherical roots.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{min}
be an unnormalized spherical root. Then
\gamma
is either

(1)

proportional to a root
\alpha\in\Phi
of
{\mathrm{G}}
such that
\alpha\in\mathfrak{X}
. We say
\gamma
is a root of type
T
,

(2)

proportional to a root
\alpha\in\Phi
of
{\mathrm{G}}
that does not lie in
\mathfrak{X}
. We say
\gamma
is a root of type
N
, or

(3)

proportional to a sum
\alpha+\beta
where
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
are strongly orthogonal roots of
{\mathrm{G}}
. We say
\gamma
is a root of type
G
.

A standard assumption in the harmonic analysis of spherical varieties is to assume that
\mathrm{X}
has no spherical roots of type
N
. This implies that
\mathfrak{X}^{sv}=\mathfrak{X}

[
SV17
, Proposition 3.1.6]
. This is a very restrictive assumption in the context of symmetric varieties, so we will not impose this constraint until Section
9.2
when we connect with the ideas of
[
BZSV23
]
.

Let
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}:=\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{s}\}\subset\mathfrak{X}
denote the spherical roots.

Definition 3.4
.

For each
i
,
\gamma^{sv}_{i}=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}n_{\alpha}\alpha
with
n_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\gcd(n_{\alpha})=1
. For a given spherical root
\gamma
, we define its
support

|\gamma|
to be
\{\alpha\in\Delta:n_{\alpha}>0\}
. More generally, we set
|\Sigma_{0}|=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Sigma_{0}}|\gamma|
for any subset
\Sigma_{0}\subset\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
.

3.1.2.
Associated coroots

There is an obvious bijection
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{sv}
, sending a spherical root
\gamma
to its normalization
\gamma^{sv}
. For any spherical root
\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
, either
\gamma^{sv}\in\Phi^{+}
(types
T
and
N
roots) or
\gamma^{sv}=\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}
is a sum of two strongly orthogonal roots
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Phi^{+}
(type
G
). For any root
\gamma
of type
{\mathrm{G}}
, there is a unique pair of such roots
\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\}
such that

\check{\gamma}_{1}-\check{\gamma}_{2}=\check{\delta}_{1}-\check{\delta}_{2},\text{ for appropriate simple roots}\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in\Delta.

This constraint implies that the restrictions of
\check{\gamma}_{i}
to
{\mathfrak{X}}^{sv}
coincide and define a unique element
\check{\gamma}\in\check{\mathfrak{X}}^{sv}=\Hom({\mathfrak{X}}^{sv},\mathbb{Z}).

For spherical roots
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
of type
T
or
N
, we set
\check{\gamma}
for the corresponding coroot in the usual sense. Let
\check{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}:=\{\check{\gamma}:\gamma\in\Delta^{sv}_{\mathrm{X}}\}
and

(3.1)

\displaystyle\hat{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}=\{\check{\gamma}:\gamma\text{ of type $T$ or $N$}\}\sqcup\{\check{\gamma}_{1},\check{\gamma}_{2}:\gamma\text{ of type $G$}\}.

This latter set is called the associated coroots of
\mathrm{X}
; the corresponding roots
\hat{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{X}}
is the set of associated roots. The table below gives the possible set of associated roots for spherical roots of type
{\mathrm{G}}
. The first column refers to the underlying root system of the rank
1
boundary degeneration of
\mathrm{X}
associated to the spherical root
\gamma
; see
[
KS17
]
for details.

Table 1.
Associated roots

|\gamma|

\gamma_{1},\>\gamma_{2}

\gamma_{1}^{\vee},\gamma_{2}^{\vee}

\delta_{1}^{\vee},\>\delta_{2}^{\vee}

\mathbf{D_{2}}

\alpha_{1},\>\alpha_{2}

\alpha_{1}^{\vee},\alpha_{2}^{\vee}

\alpha_{1}^{\vee},\alpha_{2}^{\vee}

\mathbf{D_{l}\>(l\geq 3)}

(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{l-2})+\alpha_{l-1}

(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{l-2})+\alpha_{l}

(\alpha^{\vee}_{1}+\cdots+\alpha^{\vee}_{l-2})+\alpha^{\vee}_{l-1}

(\alpha^{\vee}_{1}+\cdots+\alpha^{\vee}_{l-2})+\alpha^{\vee}_{l}

\alpha_{l-1}^{\vee},\alpha_{l}^{\vee}

\mathbf{B_{3}}

\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2\alpha_{3},\>\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}

\alpha^{\vee}_{1}+\alpha^{\vee}_{2}+\alpha^{\vee}_{3},\>2\alpha^{\vee}_{2}+\alpha^{\vee}_{3}

\alpha_{1}^{\vee},\alpha_{2}^{\vee}

3.2.
Combinatorial homogeneous spherical data

We say a simple root
\alpha\in\Delta
is
parabolic
for
\mathrm{X}
if
x\cdot P_{\alpha}=x\cdot\B
for any
x\in\mathrm{X}
in the (unique) open
\B
-orbit
\mathring{\mathrm{X}}\subset\mathrm{X}
; here,
P_{\alpha}
is the minimal non-solvable parabolic subgroup associated to
\alpha
. We set
\Delta^{p}_{\mathrm{X}}\subset\Delta
to be the set of parabolic simple roots of
\mathrm{X}
.

Let
\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{X})={\mathcal{D}}^{B}(\mathrm{X})
denote the set of
\B
-stable prime
divisors of
\mathrm{X}
, called the
colors of
\mathrm{X}
. Each color
D\in{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})
defines a
\B
-invariant valuation on
\overline{k}(\mathrm{X})^{(\B)}
. We thus obtain a map

\rho:{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X},\mathbb{Q}}

sending
D
to the associated valuation
\nu_{D}
. For
D\in{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})
, let
P_{D}
denote the stabilizer of
D
in
{\mathrm{G}}
. Clearly,
P_{D}\supset B
is a parabolic subgroup. For
\alpha\in\Delta
, let
P_{\alpha}
denote the corresponding minimal parabolic
subgroup of
{\mathrm{G}}
containing
\B
. Let
\Delta(D)
denote the set of
\alpha\in\Delta
such that
P_{\alpha}\not\subset P_{D}
. We obtain a map

\varsigma:{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\Delta),

where
\mathcal{P}(\Delta)
denotes the power set of
\Delta
.

Consider the map

(3.2)

\rho\times\varsigma:{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X},\mathbb{Q}}\times\mathcal{P}(\Delta),

and let
\Omega
denote its image. The fibers of this map over
\Omega
contain either one or
2
colors
[
Bor20
, Appendix B]
. Let
\Omega^{(1)},\>\Omega^{(2)}
denote the corresponding subsets determined by the size of the fiber.
By a variant Losev’s Uniqueness Theorem
[
Los09
, Theorem 1]
, the invariants

(3.3)

\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}:=(\mathfrak{X},\Delta_{\mathrm{X}},\Omega^{(1)},\Omega^{(2)})

determine the spherical homogeneous space
\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}=\mathrm{H}_{\overline{k}}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
of the reductive
\overline{k}
-group
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
up to a
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
-equivariant isomorphism (the spherical roots
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
encode the valuation cone
\mathcal{V}
).

On the other hand, our
k
-group
{\mathrm{G}}
induces a
\ast
-action
\{\sigma_{\ast}\}_{\sigma\in\Gamma}
of
\Gamma
on
\mathrm{X}^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
and on
\Delta
. As explained in
[
BG22
, Section 2]
, the
\ast
-action induces for each
\sigma\in\Gamma
a homogeneous spherical datum
\sigma_{\ast}\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
. A necessary condition for the existence of a
k
-model of
\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}
is that
\sigma_{\ast}\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}=\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
for each
\sigma\in\Gamma
.

Proposition 3.5
.

[
BG22
, Proposition 2.17]

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a connected reductive
k
-group and
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a spherical homogeneous
k
-variety. The induced
\ast
-action on
\mathrm{X}^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
and
\Delta
preserves the invariants
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
.

When
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split, Borovoi and Gagliardi prove the following converse of this result (see
[
BG22
]
