for their more general results). Fix also a
k
-rational Borel subgroup
\B\subset{\mathrm{G}}
, and let
{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})
denote the colors of
\mathrm{X}
. This comes equipped with map
\rho\times\varsigma
, defined in (
3.2
). Note that the
k
-rationality of
\B
implies that the Galois action on
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
induced by
\mu_{\mathrm{X}}
preserves the set
\B
-orbits and
\rho\times\varsigma
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.

When
\sigma_{\ast}\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}=\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
for each
\sigma\in\Gamma
, we obtain a continuous
\Gamma
-action on
\Omega
, and one may always choose a continuous lift

\alpha_{{\mathcal{D}}}:\Gamma\times{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X}),

which amounts to giving an action of
\Gamma
on the fibers over
\Omega^{(2)}
.

Proposition 3.6
.

[
BG22
, Theorem 6.13 and 6.15]

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split and that
\overline{\mathrm{X}}=\overline{\mathrm{H}}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
is a spherical homogeneous space of
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
. Assume that the corresponding
\ast
-action
\{\sigma_{\ast}\}_{\sigma\in\Gamma}
determined by
{\mathrm{G}}
preserves
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
. Then for any lift
\alpha_{{\mathcal{D}}}
of the
\Gamma
-action from
\Omega
to a continuous action on
{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})
, there exists a
{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariant
k
-model
\mathrm{X}
of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
inducing
\alpha_{{\mathcal{D}}}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{X}(k)\neq\emptyset
, so that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
for a
k
-rational subgroup
\mathrm{H}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
.

The following is a simple example where distinct lifts
\alpha_{{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})}
of the Galois action exist. It illustrates the issue of determining what possible
{\mathrm{G}}
-outer forms may exists.

Example 3.7
.

Let
E/k
be a quadratic extension of fields and let
(V,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)
be a
2
-dimensional Hermitian
E
-vector space containing an isotropic line. Let
{\mathrm{G}}=\U(V)
denote the corresponding quasi-split unitary group. We assume that the Hermitian form is represented by the matrix

J=\epsilon\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&1\\
-1&\end{smallmatrix}\right),\qquad\epsilon\in E_{tr=0}.

Thus,
{\mathrm{G}}=\{g\in\GL(V):J{}^{T}\overline{g}^{-1}J^{-1}=g\}.
Now we consider the two involutions

\theta_{t}=\Ad\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&t^{-1}\\
t&\end{smallmatrix}\right),\qquad t\in\{1,\epsilon\}.

We compute

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{t}:={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta_{t}}=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
t^{2}b&a\end{smallmatrix}\right):\mathrm{Nm}(a)-t^{2}\mathrm{Nm}(b)=1,\quad a\overline{b}=\overline{a}b\right\};

so that
\mathrm{H}_{1}\simeq\Res_{E/k}(\mathbb{G}_{m})
while
\mathrm{H}_{\epsilon}\simeq\mathrm{Nm}^{1}_{E/k}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}
.
We have

\mathrm{X}_{t}=\mathrm{H}_{t}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&y\\
-t^{2}{y}&z\end{smallmatrix}\right):x,y,z\in k,\>xz+t^{2}y^{2}=1\right\}.

Then
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\simeq\mathrm{A}
with the canonical map
\mathrm{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
being the squaring map. In particular,
{\Delta}^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}=\{2\alpha\}
. Since
-I\in\mathrm{X}(k)
, we see that
\alpha\in\mathfrak{X}
. The simple calculation now shows that the divisor
\{z=0\}
is stable under the upper triangular Borel subgroup with cocharacter
\check{\omega}=\frac{\check{\alpha}}{2}\in X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
, geometrically with two irreducible components
\{D_{1},{\mathcal{D}}_{2}\}=\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{X}_{t})
.

The two symmetric
k
-varieties
\mathrm{X}_{t}
become isomorphic upon base changing to
E
, so that they have isomorphic homogeneous spherical data

\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}=(2X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\{\alpha\},\emptyset,\{(\check{\omega},\{\alpha\})\})

and the fiber
(\rho\times\varsigma)^{-1}(\check{\omega},\{\alpha\})=\{D_{1},D_{2}\}
consists of the two colors in
\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}
. Note that

\displaystyle X_{t,\{z=0\}}(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left\{\pm\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&1\\
-1&\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}&:t=1,\\
\qquad\emptyset&:t=\alpha,\text{ since }\epsilon^{2}\notin(k^{\times})^{2}.\end{cases}

Thus, the
\Gamma
-action on
\{D_{1},D_{2}\}
is trivial when
t=1
and acts non-trivially through the quotient
\Gal(E/k)
when
t=\epsilon
. ∎

3.3.
Automorphisms and forms of varieties

For this section, we let
\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}
be a reductive group over
\overline{k}
and
\overline{\mathrm{X}}=\overline{\mathrm{H}}\backslash\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}
be a homogeneous spherical
\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}
-variety, and let
{\mathrm{G}}
(respectively,
\mathrm{X}
) denote a given
k
-form of
\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
).

Recall from
[
Kno96
]
that the group
\Aut^{\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}}(\overline{\mathrm{X}})=\overline{\mathrm{H}}\backslash N_{\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}}(\overline{\mathrm{H}})
of
\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}
-automorphisms is diagonalizable and comes equipped with a canonical inclusion
\Aut^{\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}}(\overline{\mathrm{X}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\overline{\mathrm{X}}}
. The following result considers the
k
-rational structure on this group determined by the quasi-split form.

Proposition 3.8
.

[
BG22
, Appendix B]

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a quasi-split
k
-form of
\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}
and suppose that
\mathrm{X}
is a
{\mathrm{G}}
-model of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
. The model
\mathrm{X}
induces a
\Gamma
-action on the automorphism group
\Aut^{\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}}(\overline{\mathrm{X}})
, with respect to which the embedding

(3.4)

\Aut^{\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}}(\overline{\mathrm{X}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\overline{\mathrm{X}}}

is equivariant. In particular,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}:=\Aut^{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{X})
is a
k
-form of
\Aut^{\overline{{\mathrm{G}}}}(\overline{\mathrm{X}})
with a
k
-rational structure and the embedding (
3.4
) descends to an embedding of diagonalizable group
k
-schemes
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
.

We note that the
\Gamma
-action on
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
depends on the
k
-form
\mathrm{X}
, but the
\Gamma
-action on
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}
depends only on the
\ast
-action associated to
{\mathrm{G}}
.

We now assume that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split over
k
for the remainder of this section and fix a
k
-rational Borel
\B\subset{\mathrm{G}}
. Assume that the
\ast
-action preserves the combinatorial data of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
. Proposition
3.6
implies the existence of a
{\mathrm{G}}
-form
\mathrm{X}
for any choice
\alpha_{\mathcal{D}}
, and we set
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}:=\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})
for the canonical
k
-group described above.
The
k
-form
{\mathrm{G}}
induces a homomorphism
\mu:\Gamma\longrightarrow\rS\Aut(\overline{{\mathrm{G}}})
(see Section
2.4
) and an exact sequence

(3.5)

1\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})\longrightarrow\rS\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\Gamma,

where
\rS\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
is the group of
\mu
-semi-linear automorphisms of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
.

3.3.1.
Doubled and distinguished roots

One of the main results of
[
Los09
]
is to clarify the distinction between
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}
, which effectively calculates the root lattice
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}=\mathbb{Z}\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}
. This requires the introduction of so-called
distinguished spherical roots
.

Lemma 3.9
.

If
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a homogeneous spherical variety, then
{\Delta}^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}
is obtained from
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
by replacing any
\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
satisfying (
A
), (
B
), (
C
) or (
D
) below by
{2}\gamma

(A)

(Type
A
, or doubled root)
\gamma=\alpha\in\Delta
such that there exists
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
with
\rho(D)=\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{\vee}|_{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X}}}
;

(B)

(type
B
) there is a subset
\Sigma\subset\Delta
of type
B_{k}
with
k\geq 2
such that

\gamma=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{k},

and
\alpha_{i}\in\Delta^{p}_{\mathrm{X}}
for
i>1
;

(C)

(type
{\mathrm{G}}_{2})
there is a subset
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}\subset\Delta
of type
{\mathrm{G}}_{2}
with
\alpha_{1}
short such that

\gamma=2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2};

(D)

\gamma\in X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
but
\gamma\notin\mathbb{Z}\Phi
,

Proof.

This is
[
Los09
, Theorem 2]
.
∎

More precisely, Losev identifies roots of type (
A
), (
B
), and (
C
) as
distinguished roots
. Note that roots of type (
C
) do not appear as spherical roots of symmetric varieties. We thus refer only to type (
A
) or (
B
) distinguished roots in Section
6
.

Remark 3.10
.

Roots of type (
D
) correspond to automorphisms arising from
Z({\mathrm{G}})
, and so do not play the same role.

Distinguished roots of type (
A
) play a special role in the general theory. Knop defines a natural subgroup

\mathcal{A}^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{X}}:=\{\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}:\phi\text{ stabilizes each }D\in{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})\},

which may be identified with the subgroup of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
inducing the trivial automorphism all the data
(\mathfrak{X},\Delta_{\mathrm{X}},{\mathcal{D}}({\mathrm{X}}))
. Since our Borel subgroup
\B
is
k
-rational, this subgroup is defined over
k
.

Set
\Aut_{\Omega}({\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X}))\subset\Aut(\mathfrak{X},\Delta_{\mathrm{X}},{\mathcal{D}}({\mathrm{X}}))
to be the subgroup of automorphisms acting trivially on
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
, but acting on the fibers of
\rho\times\varsigma
over
\Omega^{(2)}
. Then
\Aut_{\Omega}({\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X}))
consists of automorphisms that swap two
undetermined
colors
\{D^{+},D^{-}\}
that lie over the same
\check{\gamma}
. This is a finite étale group scheme, and we have a short exact sequence of diagonalizable
k
-group schemes
[
BG22
, (7.7)]

(3.6)

1\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}\longrightarrow\Aut^{G}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\Aut_{\Omega}({\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X}))\longrightarrow 1.

This quotient is well understood: we have the dual sequence of
\Gamma
-modules

0\longrightarrow X^{\ast}(\Aut_{\Omega}({\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})))\longrightarrow X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}\longrightarrow 0,

where
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}
is the sublattice spanned by the set
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}
obtained from
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}
by replacing
2\alpha
with
\alpha
for every distinguished root of type (
A
).

Remark 3.11
.

It turns out that
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}
is the set of spherical roots of the
spherical closure

\mathrm{H}^{\sharp}
of
\mathrm{H}
. More generally,
\mathrm{X}
is said to be
spherically closed
when
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}=\{1\}
.

3.4.
The group of doubling automorphisms

We generalize this by introducing a quotient
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
which plays an important role in the rational theory of spherical varieties. For functoriality reasons, our definition will proceed in two steps: first the case when
\mathrm{H}
is connected, and then the general case. Section
3.4.3
, discusses the relationship between this group and the issue of
{\mathrm{G}}
-outer forms to the geometry of
\mathrm{X}
.

3.4.1.
The connected case

We first assume that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
with
\mathrm{H}
connected. Let
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}\subset\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
denote the subset of distinguished spherical roots, and consider the set
\Sigma^{d}
obtained from the normalized spherical roots
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}
by replacing
2\alpha
with
\alpha
for every
\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}
(so that only those roots of type (
D
) are doubled), and set
\Lambda^{d}:=\mathbb{Z}\Sigma^{d}
. This is clearly
\Gamma
-stable, so that we obtain a short exact sequence of
\Gamma
-modules

0\longrightarrow\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{X}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{X}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}\longrightarrow 0

and a dual sequence of diagonalizable
k
-schemes

(3.7)

1\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow 1;

we refer to the quotient group
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
as the group of
doubling automorphisms
. To motivate the definition, recall that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\subset\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
so that for any
\gamma\in\mathfrak{X}=X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
, we set
\langle\gamma,a\rangle\in\overline{k}^{\times}
for
a\in\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})
.

Definition 3.12
.

[
Los09
, Definition 4.1.6]

For
\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}
, an automorphism
a\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})
is said to
double

\alpha
if
\langle\alpha,a\rangle=-1
.

In particular, each
\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}
determines a canonical character
\mu_{\alpha}=\langle\alpha,-\rangle:\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})\longrightarrow\mu_{2}(\overline{k})
via this pairing. In particular,

\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})_{\overline{k}}\overset{\prod_{\alpha}\mu_{\alpha}}{\longrightarrow}\prod_{\alpha}\mu_{2}

is a
\overline{k}
-isomorphism. The
k
-structure on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
induces a
k
-form on the quotient. Set
I_{\mathrm{X}}
be a finite index set for the
\Gamma
-orbits on
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}
. We have the
\Gamma
-orbit decomposition

(3.8)

\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}=\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{\mathrm{X}}}\mathcal{O}_{i}=\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{\mathrm{X}}}\Gamma/\Gamma_{i}\cdot\gamma_{i},

where
\gamma_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{i}
and
\Gamma_{i}=\Gal(\overline{k}/k_{i})
is the stabilizer of
\gamma_{i}
. This orbit structure completely determines the
\Gamma
-action on

\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}/2\mathbb{Z}\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}.

More precisely,

(3.9)

X^{\ast}(\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}))=\bigoplus_{i\in I_{\mathrm{X}}}\Ind_{\Gamma_{i}}^{\Gamma}(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\gamma_{i})\simeq(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}}.

This induces a product formula

(3.10)

\Aut_{d}({\mathrm{X}})=\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{X}}}\Res_{k_{i}/k}(\mu_{2}).

The next lemma asserts that
\Aut_{d}({\mathrm{X}})
depends only on
{\mathrm{G}}
, and not on the specific
k
-form
\mathrm{X}
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split and that
\overline{\mathrm{X}}=\overline{\mathrm{H}}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
is a spherical homogeneous space of
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
. Assume that the corresponding
\ast
-action
\{\sigma_{\ast}\}_{\sigma\in\Gamma}
determined by
{\mathrm{G}}
preserves
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
. The
k
-structure on the group
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
is independent of the choice of
{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariant
k
-model
\mathrm{X}
of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
.

Proof.

The assumption that
\Gamma
stabilizes
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
induces a
\Gamma
-action on
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}
independent of the choice of
k
-form. This determines the
\Gamma
-actions on both
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}=\mathbb{Z}\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}\subset\Delta\mathrm{X}
. In particular, this determines the action on
\Lambda_{X}^{d}
, hence on

\Lambda^{d}_{\mathrm{X}}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}.

By duality, this determines the diagonalizable
k
-group
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
up to isomorphism.
∎

Moreover, we have the following surjectivity result.

Lemma 3.14
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is spherical and that
\mathrm{H}
is geometrically connected. In the long exact sequence on cohomology induced by (
3.7
), the map

H^{1}(k,\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(k,\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}))

is surjective.

Proof.

We show that

i:H^{2}(k,\mathcal{A}_{X}^{d})\to H^{2}(k,\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}})

is injective. This proves the claim via the long exact sequence in cohomology. For this, let recall that
\mathfrak{X}=X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
, and let
\mathrm{A}^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}
(resp.,
\mathrm{A}^{d}_{\mathrm{X}}
) be the
k
-torus with character group
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}
(resp.
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}
). We have the commutative diagram (with exact rows)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\ast}(\mathcal{A}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{d}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\ast}(\mathcal{A}^{d}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

which is dual to the commutative diagram (with exact rows)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{A}^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{A}^{d}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Passing to cohomology, we have the following commutative diagram (with exact rows)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(k,\mathrm{A}^{d}_{\mathrm{X}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(k,\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(k,\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(k,\mathrm{A}^{n}_{\mathrm{X}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(k,\mathcal{A}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(k,\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Since each of the lattices
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}
possess a
\Gamma
-stable
\mathbb{Z}
-basis (
\Delta^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}
, respectively), the first cohomology groups of the tori
\mathrm{A}^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}
and
\mathrm{A}^{d}_{\mathrm{X}}
vanish by Shapiro’s lemma and Hilbert’s theorem 90. This forces
i
to be injective, proving the claim.
∎

3.4.2.
The general case

Now suppose that
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H})
is not the trivial group
k
-scheme, set
\mathrm{H}^{\circ}
to be the connected component of the identity and set
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}=\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
, so that the map
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}\to\mathrm{X}
is finite étale (recall our assumption that
\mathrm{H}
is smooth in the positive characteristic setting). The definition given above works just as well for
\mathrm{H}
, but the group it produces is generally “too small” in the sense to be made precise below (see Remark
3.16
). The following example illustrates one of the deficiencies of such an approach.

Example 3.15
.

Let
{\mathrm{G}}=\SL_{2}\times\SL_{2}
and let
\mathrm{H}=(T_{0}\times T_{0})\sqcup(T_{0}w\times T_{0}w)
, where
T_{0}
is the maximal torus fixed pointwise by
\theta(g)={}^{T}g^{-1}
and
w\in N_{\SL_{2}}(T_{0})\setminus{T_{0}}
. Then
\mathrm{H}^{\circ}=T_{0}\times T_{0}
is the connected component of the identity and the induced map

\mathrm{X}^{\circ}=\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}

is an étale double cover. Let
\mathrm{A}=T\times T
be the diagonal torus, so we have the two canonical tori
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\circ}
and
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
with surjective morphisms

\mathrm{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}.

If
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})=\mathbb{Z}\omega_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\omega_{2}
, then
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}})=\mathbb{Z}(2\omega_{1})\oplus\mathbb{Z}(2\omega_{2})
and

X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})=\mathbb{Z}(4\omega_{1})+\mathbb{Z}(4\omega_{2})+\mathbb{Z}(2\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2}).

In particular, we see that
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{n}=\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}=\{4\omega_{1},4\omega_{2}\}
; this implies

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}\simeq\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2},\text{ and }\mathcal{A}_{{\mathrm{X}}}\simeq\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2}/\Delta\mu_{2}\simeq\mu_{2}.

While both spherical roots are distinguished for
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}
, they are both of type
N
for
\mathrm{X}
. This implies that
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})\simeq\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2}
, while
\Aut^{naive}_{d}(\mathrm{X})=1
if we use the naïve definition of mirroring the definition of
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})
for
\mathrm{X}
. ∎

The issue in this example is that while
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})
encodes a great deal of arithmetic information about
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}
, this definition of
\Aut^{naive}_{d}(\mathrm{X})
forgets too much information and fails to be functorial with respect to the natural map
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}\to\mathrm{X}
. More specifically,
\Out_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}(\mathrm{H}^{\circ})\simeq\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})
while
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\simeq\mu_{2}
is bigger than
\Aut^{naive}_{d}(\mathrm{X})
.

To remedy this, we define

(3.11)

\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}):=\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})/\Aut_{d}(\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H})),

where we slightly abuse notation to mean that we quotient out by the image of
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H})
under the morphism to
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})
. This corresponds to setting
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}:=\mathfrak{X}\cap\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{d}
, so that
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
is dual to

(\mathfrak{X}\cap\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{d})/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}\subset\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{d}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}};

the same argument as Lemma
3.13
applies in this case. This definition ensures that there exists a commutative diagram of diagonalizable
k
-groups with exact rows

(3.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}).}

Note that
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})\simeq\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
if
\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}})
.

Remark 3.16
.

This will be our main application of the functorial properties of
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
. It may be enhanced to give a functor from the category of quasi-affine homogeneous spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-varieties with dominant
{\mathrm{G}}
-equviariant morphisms to the category of diagonalizable group
k
-schemes, but we do not need this.

If
H^{1}(k,\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ}))\to H^{1}(k,\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}))
is surjective, then certainly the map
H^{1}(k,\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}})\to H^{1}(k,\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}))
is also surjective so that Lemma
3.14
extends to this case. This surjectivity need not occur.

Nevertheless, we expect the following to hold.

Conjecture 3.17
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a quasi-split reductive
k
-group and
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a homogeneous spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety. The map

H^{1}(k,\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(k,\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}))

is surjective.

It is not hard to show that it holds in the following cases.

Corollary 3.18
.

Conjecture
3.17
holds in the following contexts:

(1)

When
\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}})
;

(2)

if
\mathrm{X}
is spherically closed (this includes the case
\mathrm{X}
is automorphism-free);

(3)

when the
\Gamma
-action on
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{dist}
is trivial.

3.4.3.
Geometric cocycles

Now let
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
be a homogeneous spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety. We first assume that
\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}})
, so that
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
is of the form (
3.10
). Recall the exact sequence (
3.5
). Quotienting out by the
\Gamma
-stable normal subgroup
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}(\overline{k})
, we obtain the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rS\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})(\overline{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rS\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

where
\rS\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})=\rS\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}/\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}(\overline{k})
. The
k
-structure of
\mathrm{X}
gives a splitting
\mu_{\mathrm{X}}:\Gamma\to\rS\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
, which descends to a splitting
\Gamma\to\rS\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
. Recalling (
3.10
) and Lemma
3.13
, we have an isomorphism
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})(\overline{k})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}}
independently of the choice of
k
-form. This gives rise to a canonical extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}}\rtimes\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma,}

where the group law on
\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}}\rtimes\Gamma
is given by

[(\epsilon^{\sigma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha},\sigma][(\epsilon^{\tau}_{\alpha})_{\alpha},\tau]=[(\epsilon^{\sigma}_{\alpha}\epsilon^{\tau}_{\sigma(\alpha)})_{\alpha},\sigma\tau].

On the other hand, when acting on
\overline{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})
by a semi-automorphism, the doubling automorphisms
\mu_{\alpha}(s)\in\mu_{2}(\overline{k})=\{\pm 1\}
are still defined, but the semi-linearity forces
\mu_{\alpha}(st)=\mu_{\alpha}(s)\mu_{\sigma(\alpha)}(t)
where
s
lies over
\sigma\in\Gamma
. We obtain the isomorphism
\epsilon:\rS\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}}\rtimes\Gamma
denote the morphism

\epsilon(s)=[(\mu_{\alpha}(s))_{\alpha},\sigma],

where
s
lies over
\sigma\in\Gamma
.

Definition 3.19
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{X}
is a
{\mathrm{G}}
-form of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
, inducing a section
\mu_{\mathrm{X}}:\Gamma\longrightarrow\rS\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
, defining a
\nu
-equivariant
\Gamma
-action on
\overline{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})
. Composing with
\epsilon
, we obtain a section

\widetilde{\mu}_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}:\Gamma\overset{\mu_{\mathrm{X}}}{\longrightarrow}\rS\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\overset{\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}}\rtimes\Gamma.

Comparing with the trivial section
\sigma\mapsto[(1)_{\alpha},\sigma]
, we thus obtain a
canonical

1
-cocycle

(3.13)

\mu_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}:\Gamma\longrightarrow\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}}=\prod_{\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}}\{\pm 1\}.

We refer to this as the
geometric cocycle
of
\mathrm{X}
.

Remark 3.20
.

The name “geometric” is motivated by how this cocycle records the actions of
\Gamma
on the geometric structures of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
, at least when
\mathrm{H}\subset\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}})
. For example, if
\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{(2)}\subset\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}
is a doubled root, we have
\mu_{\alpha}(\sigma)=-1
if and only if the automorphism by which
\sigma
acts on
\overline{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})
swaps the two colors
D^{+}
and
D^{-}
associated to
\alpha

[
Los09
, Remark 4.1.7]
(see also
[
BG22
, Proposition C.5]
).

For a distinguished root
\gamma
of type (
B
) or(
C
), there is a unique color
D_{\gamma}
associated to
\gamma
so that the automorphism acts trivially on
(\mathfrak{X},\Delta_{\mathrm{X}},{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X}))
. Nevertheless, in this case there exist
\B
-orbits of
higher codimension
in the closure of
D_{\gamma}
which are permuted by automorphisms. The doubling character
\mu_{\gamma}
encodes whether the an automorphism permutes these orbits or stabilizes them. See also the discussion in
[
BZSV23
, Section 4.8]
, where the authors use the language of “colors of even sphere type.” The next example illustrates this point geometric nature of a doubling automorphism of type (
B
).

Example 3.21

(Doubling automorphism for
\Sp_{2n}\times\Sp_{2n}\backslash\Sp_{4n}
)
.

Let
(V,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)
be a
4n
-dimensional symplectic space and let
{\mathrm{G}}=\Sp(V)
. Let
\mathrm{X}
be the variety of decompositions
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
into
2n
-dimensional non-degenerate symplectic subspaces of
V
, so that the sum is orthogonal. Then after fixing a base point, we identify

\mathrm{X}\simeq\Sp(V_{1})\times\Sp(V_{2})\backslash\Sp(V).

This symmetric variety possesses a wonderful compactification

\mathrm{X}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{2n}(V)

into the Grassmannian of
2n
-planes in
V
, with open orbit
\mathrm{X}\subset\mathrm{Gr}_{2n}(V)
corresponds to those partial flags

0\subset W\subset V

such that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle|_{W\otimes W}
is non-degenerate. The unique
\Sp(V)
-equivariant automorphism
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
doubling the distinguished spherical root corresponds to sending
W
to its annihilator

\displaystyle\varphi:\mathrm{X}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{X}

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle\longmapsto W^{\perp}.

Fix a Borel
\B\subset\Sp(V)
, which determines a full symplectic flag

0\subset U_{1}\subset\cdots\subset U_{2n}\subset\cdots\subset U_{4n-1}\subset V,

where
U_{2n}
is a Lagrangian and
U_{4n-i+1}/U_{4n-i}\simeq(U_{i+1}/U_{i})^{\ast}
.
Let
\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}
denote the unique distinguished root (which is of type (
B
)). The unique color
D\in{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})
associated to
\gamma
may be described as

D=\{W\subset V:\dim(U_{2n}\cap W)=1\}.

Note that if
W\in D
, then
W^{\perp}\in D
as well since
V=W\oplus W^{\perp}
, reflecting that
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}(\overline{k})
stabilizes the color.

One now considers the
B
-stable subvariety
D^{\prime\prime}=\{W\subset V:U_{1}\subset W\}\subset D
of codimension
2
, and notes

\varphi(D^{\prime\prime})=\{W\subset V:U_{1}\subset W^{\perp}\}\neq D^{\prime\prime}.

Thus,
\varphi
acts non-trivially on Borel orbits of higher codimension which lie in the closure of
D
.
∎

If we now allow more general
\pi_{o}(\mathrm{H})
, let
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
and
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}=\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. Consider the quotient
m_{\mathrm{H}}
defined by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})(\overline{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})(\overline{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{dist}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{dist}}/\Aut_{d}(\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}))}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{\mathrm{H}}}

The
k
-rationality of
\Aut_{d}(\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}))\subset\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})
implies that the subgroup

\Aut_{d}(\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}))(\overline{k})=\Aut_{d}(\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}))(\overline{k})\rtimes\{1\}\subset\mathrm{S}\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})(\overline{k})\simeq\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{dist}}\rtimes\Gamma

is normal so that we obtain the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rS\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})(\overline{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rS\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{dist}}/\Aut_{d}(\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mu_{2}(\overline{k})^{\Delta_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{dist}}/\Aut_{d}(\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}))\right)\rtimes\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

We may similarly obtain a
canonical
cocycle
\mu_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}
valued in the above quotient group from the section of
\rS\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\to\Gamma
as in the preceding case.

If
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
are two
{\mathrm{G}}
-forms of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
4
4

4

Recall that this requires that
\mathrm{X}_{\overline{k}}\simeq\mathrm{X}^{\prime}_{\overline{k}}
in a
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
-equivariant way (cf. Remark
2.5
).
, we say they are in the same
geometric class
if the geometric cocycles
\mu_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}^{d}
are cohomologous in
H^{1}(k,\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}))
. It is an easy check that this is the case if
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
are
{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariantly isomorphic. When
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H})
is trivial, Lemma
3.14
implies that there exist
{\mathrm{G}}
-forms of
\mathrm{X}
associated to each geometric class as soon as any
{\mathrm{G}}
-form exists, which occurs as soon as the
\ast
-action of
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A})
preserves the combinatorial data
\Omega_{\overline{\mathrm{X}}}
by Proposition
3.6
. Conjecture
3.17
asserts this should hold for general
\mathrm{H}
(cf. Corollary
3.18
).

3.4.4.
{\mathrm{G}}
-outer forms and Well-adapted varieties

We continue to assume that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split and that
\mathrm{X}
is a homogeneous spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety. Fix
x\in\mathrm{X}(k)
and let
\mathrm{H}=\Stab_{{\mathrm{G}}}(x)
. Recall from Section
2.4
that there is a canonical morphism

{\mathrm{out}}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\Out(\mathrm{X}),

which is compatible with
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\simeq\mathrm{H}\backslash N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})\to\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\subset\Out(\mathrm{H})
,
inducing a short exact sequence of diagonalizable groups

1\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\Out(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow 1.

Since
\Out(\mathrm{H})
is a discrete group, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\circ}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}
, though
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}
is generally larger. Since
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\cong\mathrm{H}\backslash N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
, it is easy to see that
Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})=Z(\mathrm{H})\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}})
, so that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}
is the image of
\mathrm{H}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}})\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
.

Lemma 3.22
.

We have a natural inclusions

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}.

In particular, there is a surjective morphisms

\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\longrightarrow\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\Aut_{\Omega}({\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})).

Proof.

The inclusion
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}
is a tautology. As noted above,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}\simeq\mathrm{H}\backslash(\mathrm{H}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}}))\simeq Z({\mathrm{G}})\cap\mathrm{H}\backslash Z({\mathrm{G}})
consists of automorphisms arising from central elements of
{\mathrm{G}}
. But any distinguished root
\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{dist}
is also an element of the root lattice
\Lambda
of
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A})
, hence is trivial on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{\flat}
. This shows the first inclusion for
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}
, but immediately implies the same for general
\mathrm{X}
since

X^{\ast}(\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}))=(\mathfrak{X}\cap\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{d})/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}\subset(\mathfrak{X}\cap\Lambda_{{\mathrm{G}}})/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}.\qed

Definition 3.23
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a quasi-split reductive
k
-group and
\mathrm{X}
is a homogeneous spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety. We say that
\mathrm{X}
is
well adapted
if the natural morphism

\Out(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})

is an isomorphism.

Remark 3.24
.

We note that well-adaptedness is a geometric property of
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
. For example, the preceding lemma implies that spherically-closed spherical varieties are automatically well adapted.

Lemma 3.25
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a homogeneous spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety, and set
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}=\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. If
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}
is well adapted, then so is
\mathrm{X}
.

Proof.

Fix a
k
-rational Borel
\B
and let
\mathrm{A}
denote the canonical torus quotient. Let
\mathring{\mathrm{X}}\subset\mathrm{X}
(resp.,
\mathring{\mathrm{X}}^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{X}^{\circ}
) denote the (geometric) open
\B
-orbit. Then
\mathrm{A}
acts on
\mathring{\mathrm{X}}\sslash[\B,\B]
and
\mathring{\mathrm{X}}^{\circ}\sslash[\B,\B]
via the quotient tori
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
and
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}
, respectively. This induces inclusions

X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})\subset X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}})\subset X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}).

Since the map
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}\to\mathrm{X}
is finite, we have equality of root lattices
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}

[
Los09
, Lemma 3.1.5 (3)]
. This implies an equality
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}=\Delta_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}^{n}
of normalized spherical roots. On the level of automorphism groups, the inclusion
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}\subset X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}})/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}
induces a short exact sequence of diagonalizable
k
-groups

1\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H})\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow 1;

Another application of
[
Los09
, Lemma 3.1.5 (3)]
applied to the spherical subgroup
\mathrm{H}^{\circ}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}})
shows that this morphism descends to a surjection
\Out_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}(\mathrm{H}^{\circ})\to\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
. Finally, diagram (
3.12
) descends to a commutative diagram with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\out(\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}}(\mathrm{H}^{\circ})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}^{\circ})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1;}

here the kernels are denoted to indicate the image of
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H})
under the appropriate quotient map.
By assumption the middle vertical arrow is an isomorphism. Standard arguments now show the other two vertical arrows are isomorphisms, proving the claim for the right most arrow.

∎

In particular, when
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
is well-adapted and
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split, the data

(\Omega_{\mathrm{X}},[\mu_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}])=(\mathfrak{X},\Delta_{\mathrm{X}},\Omega^{(1)},\Omega^{(2)},[\mu_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}])

with
[\mu_{\mathrm{X}}^{d}]\in H^{1}(k,\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}))
determines a
k
-form
\mathrm{X}
up to
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner form. The notion of well adaptedness is motivated by our applications to endoscopy for symmetric varieties and our calculation of
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
in Section
6
. In particular, we show that most symmetric varieties (including all symmetric varieties with no type
N
spherical roots) are well adapted.

4.
Involutions on groups and root systems

In this section, we review the combinatorial theory of involutions, first on root systems and then also on reductive algebraic groups. The important concept of admissibility of the index of an involution is reviewed. Sections
4.2
and
4.3.3
recall the definitions and properties associated to an arithmetic enhancement of an index due to Helminck. The new material comes in Section
4.4
, where we introduce and study the notion of an endoscopic root system with involution. We return to the geometric properties of the associated symmetric varieties in Section
5
.

4.1.
Abstract root systems

Let
{\mathrm{Y}}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice with dual lattice
\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}
.
Suppose that
\Psi=({\mathrm{Y}},\Phi,\check{{\mathrm{Y}}},\check{\Phi})
is a root datum with
\Phi\neq\emptyset
. Let
\theta\in\Aut(\Psi)
be an involution. Set

{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\theta)=\{\lambda\in{\mathrm{Y}}:\lambda=\theta(\lambda)\}\text{ and }{\mathrm{Y}}_{1}(\theta)=\{\lambda-\theta(\lambda)\in{\mathrm{Y}}:\lambda\in Y\},

and set
\Phi_{0}(\theta)=\Phi\cap{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\theta)
. This gives an additively-closed sub-root system.

Suppose that
\Delta
is a basis for
\Phi
chosen so that the induced ordering on
{\mathrm{Y}}
satisfies

(4.1)

If
\lambda>0
and
\lambda\notin{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\theta)
then
\theta(\lambda)<0
;

such a basis is called a basis as
\theta
-basis
for
{\mathrm{Y}}
. We shall see that such bases correspond to maximally
\theta
-split Borel subgroups containing our fixed maximally
\theta
-split maximal torus. The next lemma is well known.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose that
\Delta
is a
\theta
-basis for
\Phi
. Then
\theta(-\Delta)
is also a
\theta
-basis. In particular, there exists
w_{\theta}\in W_{0}
such that
w_{\theta}\theta(\Delta)=-\Delta
. In fact,
w_{\theta}
is the longest element of
W_{0}
with respect to
\Delta_{0}(\theta):=\Delta\cap\Phi_{0}(\theta)
.

With the notation as above, set
\theta^{\ast}:=-w_{\theta}\theta
, so that
\theta^{\ast}\in\Aut({\mathrm{Y}},\Phi,\Delta)
and
\theta^{\ast}(\Delta_{0}(\theta))=\Delta_{0}(\theta)
. The involution
\theta^{\ast}|_{\Delta_{0}(\theta)}=-w_{0}(\ast)
is the opposition involution of
\Delta_{0}(\theta)
, hence is completely determined by
\Phi_{0}(\theta)
.

4.1.1.
The index of
\theta

Suppose now that
\Psi
is semi-simple (equivalently,
\Phi
spans
{\mathrm{Y}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
), and let
\theta\in\Aut(\Psi)
be an involution. Set
\Delta
to be a fixed
\theta
-basis.

Lemma 4.2
.

[
Hel88
, 2.11]

\theta
is determined by the quadruple
\I=({\mathrm{Y}},\Delta,\Delta_{0}(\theta),\theta^{\ast})
.

This quadruple is known as the
index of
\theta
. It naturally encodes the Satake diagram of
\theta
: consider the Dynkin diagram of
({\mathrm{Y}},\Delta)
, color in black those vertices in
\Delta
associated to elements of
\Delta_{0}(\theta)
, then indicate via arrows the diagram action of
\theta^{\ast}
. When
\Psi
is semi-simple, this diagram encodes all the information of the index.

4.2.
(\Gamma,\theta)
-indices

Let
k
be a field with
\mathrm{char}(k)\neq 2
and set
\Gamma=\Gal(k^{sep}/k)
. As we will see below,
\theta
-indices give a combinatorial object designed to classify involutions of reductive groups over an algebraically closed field. Here we recall the enhancement – called a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index – introduced in
[
Hel00
]
to classify involutions over more general fields. As we explain in Lemma
5.10
below, the
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index records the homogeneous spherical datum of the variety
{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. This framework is useful in verifying that the proposed endoscopic spherical data (see Section
8.2
) satisfy Luna’s axioms and descend to a
k
-rational symmetric variety.

Suppose that
\Psi
is a root datum endowed with a
\Gamma
-action and an involution
\theta
such that
\sigma\circ\theta=\theta\circ\sigma
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
.
If
\mathcal{E}\subset\Aut(\Psi)
denotes the finite subgroup of automorphisms through which
\Gamma
acts and we set
\Gamma_{\theta}:=\mathcal{E}\cdot\{1,-\theta\}
to be the group generated by these two (commuting) groups of automorphisms, consider the lattices

{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\Gamma):=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathrm{Y}}:\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{E}}\sigma(\lambda)=0\right\}\>\>\text{and}\>\>{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\Gamma_{\theta}):=\left\{\alpha\in{\mathrm{Y}}:\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{E}}\sigma(\alpha)=\theta\left(\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{E}}\sigma(\alpha)\right)\right\}.

When
({\mathrm{Y}},\Phi)
comes from a reductive group
{\mathrm{G}}
over
k
(see discussion below) and
\theta
comes from a
k
-rational involution on
{\mathrm{G}}
restricted to an appropriate maximal torus
\mathrm{A}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
(cf. Section
4.3.1
),
{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\Gamma)
encodes the anisotropic kernel of
{\mathrm{G}}
and
{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\Gamma_{\theta})
encodes the semi-simple part of the centralizer of a maximal
k
-split torus
\mathrm{A}_{spl}^{-}
on which
\theta
acts by inversion.

Assume that a
\theta
-basis
\Delta\subset\Phi
may be chosen satisfying the following two conditions:

(1)

If
\lambda>0
and
\lambda\notin{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\Gamma)
then
\sigma(\lambda)>0
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
;

(2)

If
\lambda>0
and
\lambda\notin{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\Gamma_{\theta})
then
\sigma(\lambda)>0
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma_{\theta}
.

Such a basis is called a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-basis
for
(\Psi,\theta)
. In general, this notion can be quite subtle. They always exist when
({\mathrm{Y}},\theta)
is induced from a
k
-rational involution on a reductive group
{\mathrm{G}}
with the maximal torus
\mathrm{A}
chosen appropriately; see
[
Hel00
, Proposition 5.26]
.

Since we largely focus on quasi-split groups, we will impose the following assumption:

(4.2)

{{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap\mathbb{Z}\Phi=0;}

this is true, for example, for the
\ast
-action induced on the root datum of a
k
-rational pair
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A})
(equivalently, if
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split). Under this assumption, if
\lambda\in{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\Gamma_{\theta})\cap\mathbb{Z}\Phi
, then
\lambda\in{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\theta)
. In particular,
[
Hel00
, Proposition 5.26]
implies that
({\mathrm{Y}},\Phi,\theta)
possesses a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index whenever the
\Gamma
-action satisfies (
4.2
).

Given this data, we associate a sextuple

\I=({\mathrm{Y}},\Delta,\Delta_{0}(\Gamma),\Delta_{0}(\theta),\sigma_{\ast},\theta^{\ast}),

called a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index
, where

(1)

\I_{k}:=({\mathrm{Y}},\Delta,\Delta_{0}(\Gamma),\sigma_{\ast})
is a (Tits)
\Gamma
-index. Recall that Tits introduced such indices to classify reductive groups over
k

[
Tit66
]
. Here,
\sigma_{\ast}
denotes the
\ast
-action of
\Gamma
on
{\mathrm{Y}}
stabilizing
\Delta
;

(2)

\I_{\theta}:=(Y,\Delta,\Delta_{0}(\theta),\theta^{\ast})
is a
\theta
-index, where
\theta^{\ast}=-w_{\theta}\theta
is the induced diagram automorphism.

We note that if (
4.2
) holds, then
\Delta_{0}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
and
\sigma=\sigma_{\ast}
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
.

4.3.
Involutions from a reductive group

Let
k
be any field of characteristic
\neq 2
and let
{\mathrm{G}}
is a connected reductive
k
-group. Suppose that
\theta
is a
k
-rational involution on
{\mathrm{G}}
. A torus
\mathrm{T}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
is called
\theta
-split
if
\theta(t)=t^{-1}
for all
t\in\mathrm{T}(\overline{k})
. It is well known that any two maximal
\theta
-split tori are geometrically conjugate by an element of
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}

[
Ric82
, Theorem 7.5]
. Let
\mathrm{A}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
be a maximal
k
-torus which is stable under
\theta
. This induces an involution on the root datum

\widetilde{\theta}:\Psi({{\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A}})\to\Psi({{\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A}}),

where
\Psi({{\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A}})=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Phi(\mathrm{A}),X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\check{\Phi}(\mathrm{A}))
, in the obvious manner.

4.3.1.
Admissible tori

For any
\theta
-stable torus
T\subset{\mathrm{G}}
, we let

T^{+}=(T\cap{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}
and
T^{-}=(\{t\in T:\theta(t)=t^{-1}\})^{\circ}
.

Set
\mathrm{X}_{\theta}:={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. We assume in this section that
{\mathrm{G}}
is isotropic over
k
. As recalled in Section
5.1
, there is a symmetrization map
s:\mathrm{X}_{\theta}\hookrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}
embedding
\mathrm{X}_{\theta}
as a closed subvariety of
{\mathrm{G}}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose
x\in\mathrm{X}_{\theta}^{ss}(k)
. There exists a choice of a
\theta
-stable maximal
k
-torus
T\subset{\mathrm{G}}
such that
s(x)\in T(k)
and
T^{-}
is a maximal
\theta
-split torus.

Proof.

To begin, Proposition 2.3 of
[
HW93
]
applied to the symmetric pair
({\mathrm{G}},{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})
implies that there exists a
\theta
-stable maximal
k
-torus
T\subset{\mathrm{G}}
. On the other hand, Theorem 7.5 of
[
Ric82
]
implies that
s(x)\in{\mathrm{G}}(k)
lies in a maximal
\theta
-split torus; in particular,
s(x)
lies in a
\theta
-split
k
-torus
A
. With out loss of generality, we may assume that
A
is a maximal
\theta
-split
k
-torus. Lemma 11.1 of
[
HW93
]
implies that
A
is, in fact, a maximal
\theta
-split torus of
{\mathrm{G}}
. In particular, any choice of maximal
k
-torus
T\supset A
is
\theta
-stable
[
Vus74
, Section 1]
.
∎

For any algebraic
k
-torus
\mathrm{A}
, we let
\mathrm{A}_{spl}\subset\mathrm{A}
denote the maximal
k
-split sub-torus in
\mathrm{A}
.

Lemma/Definition 4.4
.

There exist
\theta
-stable maximal
k
-tori
\mathrm{A}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
such that

(1)

\mathrm{A}_{spl}
is a maximal
k
-split torus,

(2)

\mathrm{A}^{-}
is a maximal
\theta
-split torus, and

(3)

\mathrm{A}^{-}_{spl}
is a maximal
(\theta,k)
-split torus.

We call such a torus
\mathrm{A}
a
(\theta,k)
-admissible torus
.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{A}_{spl}^{-}
be a maximal
(\theta,k)
-split torus of
{\mathrm{G}}
, which exists by Proposition 4.3 of
[
HW93
]
. Let
Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A}_{spl}^{-})_{ani}
denote the anisotropic factor of
Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A}_{spl}^{-})
. Let
\mathrm{A}_{spl}
be a maximal
k
-split torus containing
\mathrm{A}_{spl}^{-}
and let
\mathrm{A}^{-}
denote a maximal
\theta
-split
k
-torus containing
\mathrm{A}_{spl}^{-}
. Lemma 4.5 (iii) of
[
HW93
]
implies that
\mathrm{A}_{spl}
is
\theta
-stable and that
Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A}_{spl}^{-})_{ani}\subset Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A}_{spl})
.

Consider the decomposition
\mathrm{A}^{-}=\mathrm{A}^{-}_{spl}\cdot\mathrm{A}^{-}_{ani}
, where
\mathrm{A}_{ani}^{-}
is anisotropic. Then
\mathrm{A}_{ani}^{-}\subset Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A}_{spl}^{-})_{ani}\subset Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A}_{spl})
, so that

\mathrm{A}^{-}\cdot\mathrm{A}_{spl}=\mathrm{A}_{spl}\cdot\mathrm{A}^{-}

is a
\theta
-stable
k
-torus. Let
\mathrm{A}
be a maximal torus in
Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A}^{-}\cdot\mathrm{A}_{spl})
. Since
\mathrm{A}^{-}\subset\mathrm{A}
is maximal
\theta
-split,
\mathrm{A}
is
\theta
-stable
[
Vus74
, Section 1]
which satisfies the constraints of the lemma.
∎

Now assume that our maximal torus
\mathrm{A}
is
(\theta,k)
-admissible. There exists a minimal
\theta
-split parabolic
k
-subgroup
P
containing
\mathrm{A}
; by Proposition 4.7 of
[
HW93
]
, it satisfies

P\cap\theta(P)=Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A}_{spl}^{-}).

Let
\B
be a Borel subgroup defined over
\overline{k}
, and assume it is chosen so that
\mathrm{A}\subset\B\subset P
; it is then maximally
\theta
-split among Borel subgroups of
{\mathrm{G}}
.

Lemma/Definition 4.5
.

A
(\theta,k)
-admissible maximal torus
\mathrm{A}
and a maximally
\theta
-split Borel
\B
gives a
\theta
-admissible Borel pair

(\mathrm{A},\B)
. If
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split,
k
-rational
\theta
-admissible Borel pairs exist.

Suppose that
\mathrm{A}
is a maximally
k
-split maximal torus of
{\mathrm{G}}
. We say that an involution
\theta
is
normally related to
\mathrm{A}
if
\mathrm{A}
is a
(\theta,k)
-admissible torus.

Lemma 4.6
.

[
Hel00
, Lemma 8.3]

Suppose that
\mathrm{A}
is a maximally
k
-split maximal torus of
{\mathrm{G}}
and
\theta
is a
k
-rational involution on
{\mathrm{G}}
. There exists a conjugate of
\theta
by an element
g\in{\mathrm{G}}(k)
such that
\theta^{\prime}=\Ad(g)\circ\theta\circ\Ad(g)^{-1}
is normally related to
\mathrm{A}
.

4.3.2.
Tori and indices

Suppose that
\Psi
is an abstract root datum as above. Recalling
[
Hel88
, Definition 3.9]
, we say that an involution
\theta\in\Aut(\Psi)
is
admissible
if there exists a reductive algebraic group
{\mathrm{G}}
over
\overline{k}
, a maximal torus
\mathrm{A}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
, and an involution
\widetilde{\theta}\in\Aut({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A})
such that

(1)

\Psi
is isomorphic to
\Psi({{\mathrm{G}},\T})=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Phi(\mathrm{A}),X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\check{\Phi}(\mathrm{A}))
,

(2)

\widetilde{\theta}
induces
\theta
on
\Psi
, and

(3)

\mathrm{A}
is a maximally
\theta
-split maximal torus of
{\mathrm{G}}

An index
\I
of an involution
\theta
on
\Psi
is called
admissible
if it is associated to an admissible involution. When
\Psi
is not semi-simple, we call an index admissible if the corresponding index on

\Psi_{ad}:=({\mathrm{Y}}_{ad},\Phi,\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}_{ad},\check{\Phi})

is admissible; here
{\mathrm{Y}}_{ad}\cap{\mathrm{Y}}
is the saturation of
\mathbb{Z}\Phi
in
{\mathrm{Y}}
. Importantly, the property of
\I
being admissible is independent of the algebraically closed field
\overline{k}

[
Hel88
]
.

Proposition 4.7
.

[
Hel88
, Theorem 3.11]

Assume that
{\mathrm{G}}
is semi-simple and
\mathrm{A}
is a maximal torus of
{\mathrm{G}}
. There is a bijection between the set of
{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
-conjugacy classes of involutory automorphisms of
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
and the isomorphism classes of admissible indices of
\Psi({{\mathrm{G}},\T})
.

Let us briefly explain how to extract an index from
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A},\theta)
. Up to conjugating
\theta
, we may assume that
\mathrm{A}
is a
(\theta,k)
-admissible torus. As we discuss in Section
4.3.1
, we may choose a Borel
\B
over
\overline{k}
such that there exists a parabolic subgroup
P
over
\overline{k}
satisfying
\B\subset P
and

(4.3)

L=P\cap\theta(P)\supset\mathrm{A}

is a
\theta
-stable Levi subgroup of
{\mathrm{G}}
. Such a parabolic subgroup is called
\theta
-split, and we assume that
P
is minimal among
\theta
-split parabolics, so that
\B
is maximally
\theta
-split among Borel subgroups in the sense that
\B\cap\theta(\B)
has minimal dimension.

Let
\Delta\subset\Phi^{+}\subset\Phi
denote the simple and positive roots associated to
(\mathrm{A},\B)
.Let
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{p}
denote those simple roots
\alpha
such that
\theta(\alpha)=\alpha
; we have that
\Delta^{p}_{\mathrm{X}}=\Delta_{L}

[
HH98
, Theorem 2.9]
.
It follows from (
4.3
) that for any
\alpha\in\Delta\setminus{\Delta^{p}_{\mathrm{X}}},

\theta(\alpha)\in\Phi^{-}
. In particular,
\Delta
is a
\theta
-basis.With notation as above,

(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Delta,\Delta^{p}_{\mathrm{X}},\theta^{\ast})

is an admissible
\theta
-index.

Generalizing Proposition
4.7
, suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is reductive and
\Psi\simeq\Psi({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A})
. Suppose that we have an index
\I
such that

(1)

the induced index
\I_{ad}
for
\Psi_{ad}
is admissible, and

(2)

there is a compatible involution
\theta_{\mathrm{A}}
on the torus
\mathrm{A}
.

We claim that there exists an involution
\theta
on
{\mathrm{G}}
inducing the index
\I
. To see this, let
\theta_{ad}
denote a lift of the involution
\theta
to
{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}
. Let
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
be the simply connected cover of
{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}
and let
\theta_{sc}
be the unique lift of
\theta_{ad}

[
Ste68
]
. Our compatibility assumption implies that
\theta_{sc}|_{Z({\mathrm{G}}_{sc})}
intertwines with
\theta_{\mathrm{A}}
. Since
{\mathrm{G}}=Z({\mathrm{G}})\times^{Z({\mathrm{G}}_{sc})}{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}

[
Del79
, 2.0.1]
, we obtain a well-defined automorphism via pushout.

4.3.3.
Rational involutions

The preceding discussion ignored the
k
-rational structure on
({\mathrm{G}},\theta)
. We now remark on the action of
\Gamma
.
Suppose that
(\mathrm{A},\B)
are chosen as above. Since
\B
need not be
k
-rational (as
{\mathrm{G}}
may not be quasi-split), the Galois action on
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
need not preserve the simple roots
\Delta
determined by
\B
. Recall from Section
2.3
the
\ast
-action
\sigma\mapsto\sigma_{\ast}
obtained by twisting the
\Gamma
-action on
(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Phi)
as follows:

\chi^{\sigma_{\ast}}=w_{\sigma}^{-1}\chi^{\sigma},

where
w_{\sigma}\in W
is the
unique
element such that
\Delta^{\sigma}=w_{\sigma}\Delta
. Then
\sigma_{\ast}\Delta=\Delta
. As noted above, if (
4.2
) holds, then
\sigma=\sigma_{\ast}
; this occurs when
\B
is
k
-rational.

Lemma 4.8
.

The involution
\theta
commutes with the
\ast
-action. In particular, the subset
\Delta^{p}_{\mathrm{X}}
is stable under the
\ast
-action of
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Note that
\theta\sigma=\sigma\theta
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
by the
k
-rationality of
\theta
. In particular, if
\chi\in X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
, then
\theta(\chi^{\sigma})=(\theta(\chi))^{\sigma}
. On the other hand

\theta(\chi^{\sigma_{\ast}})=\Ad(\theta(w_{\sigma}))^{-1}\cdot(\theta(\chi))^{\sigma}.

But
[
Hel00
, Proposition 10.39]
shows that
\theta(w_{\sigma})=w_{\sigma}
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
, proving the claim.
∎

Given this, we may associate to
({\mathrm{G}},\theta,\mathrm{A},\B)
a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index

\I=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Delta,\Delta_{0}(\Gamma),\Delta_{0}(\theta),\sigma_{\ast},\theta^{\ast}),

where
\Delta_{0}(\Gamma)
gives the simple roots in the anisotropic kernel of
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A})
(recall that
\mathrm{A}
is maximally
k
-split). A
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index is called
admissible
if it is isomorphic (in the sense of
[
Hel00
, Section 5]
) to one associated to a
k
-rational symmetric pair
({\mathrm{G}},\theta)
in this way.

One of the main results of
[
Hel00
]
is a characterization of such admissible indices.
More precisely,
[
Hel00
, Theorem 10.45]
states that a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index is admissible if and only if

(a)

\I
is a basic
\Gamma_{\theta}
-index,

(b)

(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Delta,\Delta_{0}(\Gamma),\sigma_{\ast})
is an admissible
\Gamma
-index,

(c)

(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Delta,\Delta_{0}(\theta),\theta^{\ast})
is an admissible
\theta
-index.

(d)

the index
(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})_{0},\Delta_{0}(\Gamma),\Delta_{0}(\theta),\sigma_{\ast}|_{\Delta_{0}}\theta^{\ast}|_{\Delta_{0}(\theta)})
is an admissible
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index.

The fourth criterion captures the notion that the involution must also be admissible “on the anisotropic kernel.” If we assume that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split and
\mathrm{A}\subset\B
are
k
-rational, (
d
) is trivially satisfied. Criteria (
b
) and (
c
) are natural, so we focus on the (
a
).

Recall from
[
Hel00
, Section 10]
, that
\I
is a basic
\Gamma_{\theta}
-index if the
\Gamma_{\theta}
-basis
\Delta
determined by
\B
(so that it satisfies (
2
)) satisfies

(i)

if
\Phi^{\prime}\subset\Phi_{0}(\Gamma_{\theta})
is an irreducible component then,
\Phi^{\prime}\subset\Phi_{0}(\theta)
or
\Phi^{\prime}\subset\Phi_{0}(\Gamma)
,

(ii)

\Delta_{0}(\Gamma)
is stabilized by
-w_{0}
where
w_{0}
is the long element of
W
determined by
\Delta_{0}(\Gamma)\subset\Delta
, and

(iii)

\Delta_{0}(\Gamma)
is
\theta^{\ast}
-stable, and

(iv)

\Delta_{0}(\theta)
is
\sigma_{\ast}
-stable for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
.

Here,

\Phi_{0}(\Gamma_{\theta}):=\left\{\alpha\in\Phi:\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{E}}\sigma(\alpha)=\theta\left(\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{E}}\sigma(\alpha)\right)\right\}=\Phi\cap{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\Gamma_{\theta}).

In particular, the
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index associated to
({\mathrm{G}},\theta,\mathrm{A},\B)
above satisfy these properties
[
Hel00
, Section 5]
.

A fundamental complication is that admissibility of a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index
\I
determines
if there exists
an involution
\theta
on
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A})
inducing
\I
, but falls short of a classification of such involutions. The main result of
[
Hel00
]
is to characterize those involutions which induce the same index, which is accomplished up to enumerating elements in a certain cohomology group. This approach does not naturally encode the geometric cocycle (cf. Definition
3.19
) of
{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
, so we do not make use of these more refined results in this paper except in the proof of Lemma
5.10
(cf. Remark
5.11
).

4.4.
Endoscopic root systems with involution

Suppose now that
\Psi=({\mathrm{Y}},\Phi,\check{{\mathrm{Y}}},\check{\Phi})
is a
root datum. A (not-necessarily additively-closed) sub-root system
\Psi_{s}
is called
endoscopic
if it is of the form

\Psi_{s}=({\mathrm{Y}},\Phi_{s},\check{{\mathrm{Y}}},\check{\Phi}_{s}),

where
s\in\Hom(\check{{\mathrm{Y}}},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, and

\check{\Phi}_{s}=\{\check{\lambda}\in\check{\Phi}:s(\check{\lambda})=1\}.

Note that there is an inclusion
W_{s}\leq W
of the Weyl group of
\Psi_{s}
into the Weyl group of
\Psi
.

We equip
\Psi
with a
\Gamma
-action which we assume agrees with the
\ast
-action associated with some choice of base
\Delta\subset\Phi
; in particular, this satisfies (
4.2
). We additionally equip
\Psi_{s}
with a basis
\Delta_{s}
and a
\Gamma
-action
\sigma_{s}
(
\sigma\in\Gamma
) agreeing with the
\ast
-action associated to
\Delta_{s}
. This implies that for each
\lambda\in{\mathrm{Y}}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma
,

\sigma_{s}(\lambda)=w(\sigma)\sigma(\lambda),\quad w(\sigma)\in N_{W}(\Psi_{s})\subset W;

here,
\mathrm{Stab}_{W}(\Psi_{s})
is the subgroup of
W
preserving
\Psi_{s}
.

Now suppose that
\Psi
is equipped with an involution
\theta
such that
\sigma\circ\theta=\theta\circ\sigma
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
. Let
\underline{\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}}\subset\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}
denote the sublattice dual to
{\mathrm{Y}}/{\mathrm{Y}}_{1}(\theta)
; that is,

\underline{\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}}=\{\chi\in\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}:\chi|_{Y_{1}(\theta)}\equiv 0\}.

Note that if
\check{\theta}
denotes the induced involution on
\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}
, then
\underline{\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}}=\{\chi\in\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}:\check{\theta}(\chi)=\chi\}
. Clearly, the
\Gamma
-action stabilizes
\underline{{\mathrm{Y}}}
.

We now assume that

(1)

s\in\Hom(\underline{\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
satisfies
\theta(s)=s
and
\sigma_{s}(s)=s
for each
\sigma\in\Gamma
;

(2)

for every
\sigma\in\Gamma
,
w(\sigma)\circ\theta=\theta\circ w(\sigma)

(3)

\Phi_{0}(\theta)\subset\Phi_{s}
.

With these assumptions, we say that
(\Psi_{s},\theta)
is an
endoscopic root system with involution
. By our discussion in Section
4.2
,
({\mathrm{Y}},\Psi_{s},\theta)
possesses a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-basis, so we assume that
\Delta_{s}
gives such a basis.

Remark 4.9
.

The third assumption is motivated by the theory in Section
8
, and is used several times in the argument below.

Proposition 4.10
.

Suppose that the root datum
\Psi=({\mathrm{Y}},\Phi,\check{{\mathrm{Y}}},\check{\Phi})
is equipped with an involution
\theta
and a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-basis
\Delta
such that the
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index
\I=({\mathrm{Y}},\Delta,\emptyset,\Delta_{0}(\theta),\sigma^{\ast},\theta^{\ast})
is admissible. Suppose that
(\Psi_{s},\theta)
is an endoscopic root datum with involution and assume that
\Delta_{s}\subset\Phi_{s}
is a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-basis satisfying
\Delta_{0}(\theta)\subset\Delta_{s}
. The induced
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index
\I_{s}=({\mathrm{Y}},\Delta_{s},\emptyset,\Delta_{0}(\theta),\sigma_{s}^{\ast},\theta^{\ast})
is admissible.

Proof.

We use
[
Hel00
, Theorem 10.45]
to reduce this to the claims that

(1)

\I_{s}
is a basic
\Gamma_{\theta}
-index,

(2)

(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Delta_{s},\Delta_{0}(\Gamma),\sigma)
is an admissible
\Gamma
-index,

(3)

(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Delta_{s},\Delta_{0}(\theta),\theta^{\ast})
is an admissible
\theta
-index.

Note that the fourth criterion (
d
) is vacuous in our setting. Criteria (
2
) is classical, since if
{\mathrm{G}}
is the quasi-split reductive
k
-group associated to
\Psi
, this gives the Tits index of the quasi-split endoscopic group
{\mathrm{G}}_{s}
. We therefore need only to consider (
1
) and (
3
).

We claim that (
1
) is immediate by considering the definition of a basic
\Gamma_{\theta}
-index given in Section
4.3.3
. There are four properties. We verify the first by noting that since (
4.2
) holds

\Phi_{s,0}(\Gamma_{\theta})=\Phi_{s,0}(\theta)=\Phi_{0}(\theta)=\Phi_{0}(\Gamma_{\theta}),

so that any irreducible component
\Phi^{\prime}\subset\Phi_{s,0}(\Gamma_{\theta})
is contained in
\Phi_{s,0}(\theta)
. Now the next two properties are vacuous as
\Delta_{s,0}(\Gamma)=\Delta_{0}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
, and the final property is the same as that of
\I
since
\Phi_{0}(\theta)=\Phi_{s,0}(\theta)
.

To verify (
c
), we need to recall some more notation from
[
Hel88
]
. Recall the sublattice
{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\theta)\subset{\mathrm{Y}}
. Set
\overline{{\mathrm{Y}}}_{0}:=Y/{\mathrm{Y}}_{0}(\theta)
and let
\pi:{\mathrm{Y}}\longrightarrow\overline{{\mathrm{Y}}}_{0}
denote the natural projection. The image
\overline{\Phi}:=\pi(\Phi-\Phi_{0})
is the set of restricted roots of
\Phi
relative to
\theta
. This is a root system with set of simple roots
\overline{\Delta}:=\pi(\Delta-\Delta_{0})
; its cardinality is the
relative rank
of
(\Psi,\theta)
.

For any
\lambda\in\overline{\Phi}
such that
\frac{1}{2}\lambda\notin\overline{\Phi}
, consider the set
\Phi(\lambda)
consisting of roots that project to an integral multiple of
\lambda
. Note
\Phi_{0}\subset\Phi(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in\overline{\Phi}
. Now define
{\mathrm{Y}}(\lambda)
to be the projection of
{\mathrm{Y}}
to
\mathbb{Z}\Phi(\lambda)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
. Setting
\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}(\lambda)\subset\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}
to be the dual with
\check{\Phi}(\lambda)\subset\check{\Phi}
, we obtain a root datum

\Psi(\lambda):=({\mathrm{Y}}(\lambda),\Phi(\lambda),\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}(\lambda),\check{\Phi}(\lambda));

this system is additively closed, symmetric, and stable under
\theta

[
BT65
, pg.71]
. Moreover, the system
(\Phi(\lambda),\theta)
has relative rank
1
. By
[
Hel88
, Proposition 4.9]
,
(\Psi,\theta)
is admissible if and only if
(\Phi(\lambda),\theta)
is admissible for each such
\lambda\in\overline{\Phi}
if and only if it is admissible for each
\lambda\in\overline{\Delta}
.

Now let
\lambda\in\overline{\Delta}_{s}
; we may likewise consider

\Psi_{s}(\lambda):=({\mathrm{Y}}(\lambda),\Phi_{s}(\lambda),\check{{\mathrm{Y}}}(\lambda),\check{\Phi}_{s}(\lambda));

this gives a
\theta
-stable sub-root system of
\Psi(\lambda)
, also with relative rank
1
. It is immediate that
(\Psi_{s}(\lambda),\theta)
is admissible if and only if the projection to
\mathbb{Z}\Phi_{s}(\lambda)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
is, since
\Psi(\lambda)
is admissible. Noting that
\Psi_{s}(\lambda)
is an endoscopic root datum with involution for
\Psi(\lambda)
, the claim follows once we verify it for the relative rank
1
case.

We thus need to consider admissible rank
1
root data with involution, and verify that those endoscopic data with involution satisfying (
1
) and (
3
) are also admissible.

Recall from
[
Hel88
, Section 4]
that there are
6
types of admissible absolutely irreducible indices of restricted rank
1
(types
A_{1}
,
A_{l}\;(l\geq 3)
,
B_{l}\;(l\geq 2)
,
C_{l}\;(l\geq 3)
,
D_{l}\;(l\geq 3)
, and type
F_{4}
), and an additional irreducible one that is not absolutely irreducible (type
D_{2}
). As the only proper
\theta
-stable root systems in types
A_{1}
and
D_{l}

(l\geq 2)
contain no roots (corresponding to an involution on a torus, and so admissible), we need only consider the remaining
4
types, allowing us to assume that
\Psi(\lambda)
is absolutely irreducible.

Table 2.
Rank-
1
Satake diagrams and endoscopy

Type

(\Psi(\lambda),\theta)

(\Psi_{s}(\lambda),\theta)

\mathbf{A_{l}\>(l\geq 3)}

(\SL_{l},\GL_{l-1})

(S(\GL_{l-2}\times\GL_{2}),\GL_{l-2}\times\mathbb{G}_{m})

\mathbf{B_{l}\>(l\geq 3)}

(\Spin_{2l+1},\Spin_{2l})

(\SL_{2}\times\Spin_{2l-1},\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\Spin_{2l-1})
