(\Sp_{2l},\Sp_{2}\times\Sp_{2l-2})

(\SO_{4}\times\Sp_{2l-4},\GL_{2}\times\Sp_{2l-4})

\mathbf{F_{4}}

(F_{4},\Spin_{9})

(\Spin_{7}\times^{\mu_{2}}\SL_{2},\Spin_{7}\times^{\mu_{2}}\mathbb{G}_{m})

An endoscopic datum corresponds to a subset
S\subset\check{\Delta}\cup\{-\check{\alpha}_{long}\}
, where
\check{\alpha}_{long}
denotes the highest long coroot. If
-\check{\alpha}_{long}\notin S
, then
\Psi_{s}(\lambda)
is a Levi sub-root system and
\Delta_{s}(\lambda)\subset\Delta(\lambda)
. However since
\Delta_{0}\subset\Delta_{s}(\lambda)
, the restricted rank
1
assumption forces
\Psi_{s}(\lambda)=\Psi(\lambda)
. We thus assume that
-\check{\alpha}_{long}\in S
.

We may now handle the cases in turn: given an admissible relative rank-
1
system
(\Psi(\lambda),\theta)
, we may read off the type of
(\Psi_{s}(\lambda),\theta)
and verify that the Satake diagram of
(\Psi(\lambda),\theta)
is again admissible. Indeed, it is shown in
[
Rub22
]
that passing to descendants corresponds to removing nodes from the Satake diagram. We compute this as follows. Given the Satake diagram of
(\Psi(\lambda),\theta)
, we dualize while keeping the coloring for the vertices as this data gives the appropriate Levi sub-root system, the dual of which is a Levi sub-root system. This gives the Dynkin diagram of the dual root system
\check{\Psi}(\lambda)
and encodes the dual Levi. We then pass to the affine Dynkin diagram of
\check{\Psi}(\lambda)
, delete a subset of the white vertices of the non-affine diagram, and dualize the resulting (possibly disconnected) diagram. We may check each case, all of which result are easily checked to produce an admissible diagram. We reproduce the diagrams which correspond to reductive involutions along with representative symmetric pairs inducing these diagram in Table
2
.

Appealing to
[
Hel88
, Theorem 4.16]
, this proves the claim.
∎

5.
Symmetric varieties and indices

In this section, we review the geometric properties of symmetric varieties, as well as establish useful rationality properties. This includes generalizing the notion of a
z
-extension from
[
Kot82
]
to the symmetric setting. We also completely explicate the spherical data for a symmetric
k
-variety. In Section
5.4
, we relate this spherical data to the associated a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index, which is a useful variant of
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
for a symmetric variety. In Section
5.3
, we relate the spherical roots systems of a symmetric variety to symmetric varieties for
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
,
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
, and
z
-extensions; this is important for the arguments of Section
6
.

5.1.
Generalities on symmetric pairs and varieties

A symmetric pair denotes a triple
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{H},\theta)
where
\mathrm{H}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
are reductive groups over
k
and
\theta
is an involution of
{\mathrm{G}}
such that

({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}).

We do not require that
\mathrm{H}
is connected, but always assume it is smooth. The quotient variety
{\mathrm{X}}:=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is called the
symmetric variety,
which we say is associated to the involution
\theta
.

Fix a symmetric pair
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{H},\theta)
. When it will not lead to confusion, we frequently refer to
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{H})
or
({\mathrm{G}},\theta)
as a symmetric pair. Define the
symmetrization map

\displaystyle\tau:{\mathrm{G}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\longmapsto g^{-1}\theta(g).

It is well known
[
Ric82
, Lemma 2.4]
that
\tau
induces an embedding (also referred to as the
symmetrization map
) of affine
{\mathrm{G}}
-varieties

s:{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}.

In particular, when
\mathrm{X}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
we may identify
\mathrm{X}
as a closed subvariety of
{\mathrm{G}}
. More generally, we set
\mathrm{X}_{\theta}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. If
\mathrm{H}\subset{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
, we obtain a finite morphism
s=s_{\mathrm{X}}:\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}\to{\mathrm{G}}
. In all cases, since
N_{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})/Z({\mathrm{G}})=[{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}]^{\theta_{ad}}
surjects onto the fixed point subgroup of the induced involution
\theta_{ad}
on
{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}
, there exists a morphism

s_{ad}:\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{X}_{ad}\subset{\mathrm{G}}_{ad};

we refer to this as the
adjoint symmetrization map
.

Viewing
{\mathrm{X}}
as an
\mathrm{H}
-variety, let
{\mathrm{X}}^{ss}
(resp.
{\mathrm{X}}^{rss}
) denote the semi-simple locus (resp. regular semi-simple locus) of
{\mathrm{X}}
. The symmetrization map allows us to relate these notions to semi-simplicity of elements in
{\mathrm{G}}

[
Ric82
, Theorem 7.5]
.

We will have occasion to consider multiple involutions, so we introduce some notation. We say that two involutions
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are conjugate if there exists
g\in{\mathrm{G}}(k)
such that
\theta^{\prime}=\Ad(g)^{-1}\circ\theta\circ\Ad(g)
. We say
\theta^{\prime}
is a
pure inner (
{\mathrm{G}}
-)twist of
\theta
if there exists
g\in{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
such that
\theta^{\prime}=\Ad(g)^{-1}\circ\theta\circ\Ad(g)
satisfying
\tau(g)=g^{-1}\theta(g)\in{\mathrm{G}}(k)
and that the
1
-cocycle

\sigma\mapsto g^{-1}\sigma(g)\in{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}(\overline{k}).

Clearly, conjugacy classes of pure inner twists of
\theta
are parameterized by
\ker^{1}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta},{\mathrm{G}};k)
.
The following is an easy Galois cohomology exercise.

Lemma 5.1
.

There is a natural bijection

{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}(k)=\bigsqcup_{\theta^{\prime}\in\ker^{1}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta},{\mathrm{G}};k)}\tau(g^{\prime})\cdot{\mathrm{G}}(k),

where
\theta^{\prime}=\Ad(\tau(g^{\prime}))\circ\theta
. Moreover, there is a
{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariant isomorphism
{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}\to{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta^{\prime}}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
if and only if
\theta^{\prime}
is a pure inner twist of
\theta
by a class in
\ker^{1}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta},{\mathrm{G}};k)
.

5.1.1.
Descendants

For
x\in{\mathrm{X}}^{ss}(k)
, let
{\mathrm{G}}_{x}
denote the connected component of the identity of the centralizer in
{\mathrm{G}}
of the element
s_{ad}(x)\in{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}(k)
and
\mathrm{H}_{x}
its stabilizer in
\mathrm{H}\cap{\mathrm{G}}_{x}
. Then
({\mathrm{G}}_{x},\mathrm{H}_{x},\theta|_{{\mathrm{G}}_{x}})
is a symmetric pair, which we refer to as as the
descendant
of
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{H})
at
x
(this is a slight generalization of the concept in
[
AG09
, Definition 7.2.2]
). We will also refer to the symmetric variety
{\mathrm{X}}_{x}:=\mathrm{H}_{x}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}_{x}
as the descendant of
{\mathrm{X}}
at
x.

Remark 5.2
.

There are two notions of degenerating a symmetric variety in the literature: semi-simple descent as just described and the notion of boundary degeneration as described in
[
SV17
, Proposition 2.4.3]
. A crucial aspect of descent for symmetric varieties is the ability to degenerate
at any semi-simple element
; boundary degeneration only applies to degeneration with respect to tori. The ability to descend with respect to torsion elements is fundamental for elliptic endoscopy.

5.2.
Compatible
z
-extensions

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a connected reductive group over a field
k
and let
({\mathrm{G}},\theta)
be a symmetric pair. Let
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
denote the derived subgroup and
\pi:{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}\to{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
the simply connected cover of
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
. By a result of Steinberg
[
Ste68
, 9.16]
, there exists a unique involution
\theta_{sc}:{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}\to{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{G}}}_{sc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{G}}}_{der}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{G}}_{der}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{sc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

commutes. The uniqueness of this involution implies that it is defined over
k
.

Let
L/k
be a finite Galois extension over which
{\mathrm{G}}
splits. Recall that a
z
-extension associated to
L/k
is a homomorphism
\alpha:\tilde{{\mathrm{G}}}\to{\mathrm{G}}
such that

(1)

\tilde{{\mathrm{G}}}
is a connected reductive group over
k
whose derived group is simply connected,

(2)

\alpha
is surjective, and

(3)

\ker(\alpha)
is central in
\tilde{{\mathrm{G}}}
and isomorphic to a product of tori of the form
\mathrm{Res}_{L/k}(\mathbb{G}_{m})
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
L/k
be a finite Galois extension over which
{\mathrm{G}}
splits. There exists a
z
-extension
\alpha:\tilde{{\mathrm{G}}}\to{\mathrm{G}}
associated to
L/k
and an involution
\tilde{\theta}:\tilde{{\mathrm{G}}}\to\tilde{{\mathrm{G}}}
such that

(1)

the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{{\mathrm{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{{\mathrm{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

commutes, and

(2)

\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\widetilde{\theta}}
is a
z
-extension of
{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
. In particular,
\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\widetilde{\theta}}(k)
surjects onto
{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}(k)
.

We refer to the
z
-extensions satisfying the claims of the proposition as (
\theta
-)compatible. We moreover note that the construction works for any finite order
k
-automorphism of
{\mathrm{G}}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}\to{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
denote the non-trivial covering map of the simply connected cover of
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
. Let
Z_{sc}
denote the center of
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
and
Z\subset{\mathrm{G}}
denote the center of
{\mathrm{G}}
. Then we may write
{\mathrm{G}}
as the fiber sum
{\mathrm{G}}={\mathrm{G}}_{sc}\times^{Z_{sc}}Z

[
Del79
, 2.0.1]
. We recall the following useful lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

[
MS82
, 3.1]

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a finite group and let
M
be a finitely generated
\mathcal{E}
-module. There exists an exact sequence of
\mathcal{E}
-modules

0\longrightarrow P_{1}\longrightarrow P_{0}\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow 0

such that
P_{0}
is free and finitely generated as a
\mathbb{Z}
-modules and
P_{1}
is free as a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{E}]
-modules.

Now let
\mathrm{A}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
be a
\theta
-stable maximal
k
-torus of
{\mathrm{G}}
that splits over
L/k
, and let
\mathrm{A}_{sc}
be the inverse image of
\mathrm{A}
in
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
; it is clearly a
\theta_{sc}
-stable maximal
k
-torus of
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
. By assumption, both lattices
X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
and
X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{sc})
are finitely generated
\mathcal{E}:=\Gal(L/k)\times\langle\theta\rangle
-modules.

Set
M=X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A})/X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{sc})
. Applying Lemma
5.4
to this module, we obtain the commutative diagram of
\mathcal{E}
-modules

(5.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{sc})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{sc})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

By construction, there is a natural involution
\theta
on each term in this diagram, and each arrow is
\theta
-equivariant. Moreover, the central row induces the exact sequence of tori

1\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}\longrightarrow 1,

where
N
is an induced torus. Note that the automorphism
\theta
induces a unique involution
\widetilde{\theta}
on
\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}
lifting the involution
\theta
. Pulling back along the inclusion
Z\hookrightarrow\mathrm{A}
, we obtain the short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow\widetilde{Z}\longrightarrow Z\longrightarrow 1.

Set
\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}={\mathrm{G}}_{sc}\times^{Z_{sc}}{\widetilde{Z}}
; this comes equipped with a surjective morphism

1\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}\overset{\alpha}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{G}}\longrightarrow 1,

as well as an involution
\widetilde{\theta}
satisfying the first claim of the proposition.

We verify the final claim on rational points. By construction,
N=\ker(\alpha)
is a torus such that
X^{\ast}(N)
possesses a free
\Gal(L/k)\times\langle\theta\rangle
-action. Letting
N^{\theta}\subset N
be the fixed-point subgroup, if follows that
N^{\theta}
is connected, since
X^{\ast}(N)_{\theta=\pm 1}
are saturated sub-lattices. Moreover, since
X^{\ast}(N)
is a free
\Gal(L/k)\times\langle\theta\rangle
-module,
X^{\ast}(N^{\theta})
is a free
\Gal(L/k)
-module. This implies that

1\longrightarrow N^{\theta}(k)\longrightarrow\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\widetilde{\theta}}(k)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}(k)\longrightarrow 1.\qed

Remark 5.5
.

Suppose
\theta
is a
k
-rational inner involution; that is, assume there is
a\in\mathrm{Inn}({\mathrm{G}})(k)
such that
\theta(g)=\Ad(a)(g)
is an involution. There is a canonical identification

\mathrm{Inn}(\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}})\simeq\mathrm{Inn}({\mathrm{G}}),

for any
z
-extension
\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}
of
{\mathrm{G}}
, so there is a canonical lift of
\theta
to
\tilde{{\mathrm{G}}}
such that
\tilde{\theta}|_{\widetilde{Z}}\equiv id
. This does
not
correspond to the construction above, as the action of
\theta
on
X^{\ast}(N)
is not free. Such a lift will not necessary recall the connected components of
\mathrm{H}
, as the following example illustrates.

Example 5.6
.

When
{\mathrm{G}}=\PGL_{2}
and

\theta\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&-b\\
-c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right),

we have
\mathrm{H}=N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A})
, where
\mathrm{A}
is the diagonal torus. The natural lift as an inner automorphism to the
z
-extension
\GL_{2}
has fixed-point subgroup the diagonal torus
\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}
, which does not surject onto
\mathrm{H}
. On the other hand, Proposition
5.3
gives

\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}=\SL_{2}\times^{\{\pm I_{2}\}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2},

with

\widetilde{\theta}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&y\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&-b\\
-c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y&\\
&x\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right),

so that

\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}=\left\{\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&\\
&a^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&x\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right):a,x\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\right\}\bigsqcup\left\{\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&b\\
-b^{-1}&\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&-x\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right):b,x\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\right\}.\hfill\qed

5.3.
Spherical data, derived subgroups, and
z
-extension

Suppose that
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{H},\theta)
is a symmetric pair over
k
and let
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. In Appendix
B
, we compute the homogeneous spherical data

\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}),\Delta_{\mathrm{X}},\Omega^{(1)},\Omega^{(2)})

of
\mathrm{X}
in terms of the involution
\theta
. On the other hand, one also obtains involutions
\theta_{ad}:{\mathrm{G}}_{\ad}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}
and
\theta_{sc}:{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
, or may consider compatible
z
-extensions of
({\mathrm{G}},\theta)
such that the natural diagrams commute. Our present goal is to clarify the relations between the corresponding spherical data.

Let
\mathrm{H}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
be a symmetric subgroup and consider
\mathrm{H}_{der}:=\mathrm{H}\cap{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

The pair
({\mathrm{G}}_{der},\mathrm{H}_{der})
is a symmetric pair. There is a surjection

X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})\longrightarrow X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}_{der}}),

inducing a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection of spherical roots

\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Delta_{\mathrm{X}_{der}},

sending a spherical root
\alpha\in X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
to the positive multiple that is primitive in
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}_{der}})
.

Proof.

The first claim is obvious. The second is contained in Appendix
A
, where it is shown that the root systems associated to
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{X}_{der}
agree after re-normalization.∎

Let
(\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}},\widetilde{\theta})
denote a
\theta
-compatible
z
-extension in the sense of Proposition
5.3
; in particular,
\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}_{der}={\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
. Since we have a short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow N^{\widetilde{\theta}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\widetilde{\theta}}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\longrightarrow 1,

we obtain

(5.2)

1\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow N_{\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}}(\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\widetilde{\theta}})\longrightarrow N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})\longrightarrow 1,

and
\pi_{0}(\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\widetilde{\theta}})\simeq\pi_{0}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})
. In particular, there is a bijection of intermediate groups

\{({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})\}\leftrightarrow\{(\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}^{\widetilde{\theta}})^{\circ}\subset\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}\subset N_{\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}^{\widetilde{\theta}}):N^{\widetilde{\theta}}\subset\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}\}.

Thus, we obtain a
k
-rational subgroup
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}\subset\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}
with a fibration

N^{\widetilde{\theta}}\backslash N\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}:=\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}\backslash\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{X},

and a short exact sequence

(5.3)

1\longrightarrow N^{\widetilde{\theta}}\backslash N\longrightarrow\Aut^{\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{X}})\longrightarrow\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow 1.

Lemma 5.8
.

With the notation as above, there is a natural (e.g.
\Gamma
-equivariant) identification

\text{$\Lambda_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}$ and $\Delta_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}=\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}$}.

Moreover, the quotient map
\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{X}
induces a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection of colors
{\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{X}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})
.

Proof.

We begin by choosing a Borel
N\subset\widetilde{\B}
of
\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}
and letting
\B\subset{\mathrm{G}}
be the image. The first claim follows from the short exact sequence (
5.3
) and the definition of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}
. Indeed, there is a short exact sequence on canonical tori

1\longrightarrow N^{\widetilde{\theta}}\backslash N\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow 1,

corresponding to a dual sequence

0\longrightarrow X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})\longrightarrow X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}})\longrightarrow X^{\ast}(N^{\widetilde{\theta}}\backslash N)\longrightarrow 0.

Since
X^{\ast}(\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X}))=X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})/\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}
, the identification of root lattices follows. For the spherical roots, since
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})\subset X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}})
is a saturated sublattice, the minimal ray generators in
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
are also minimal in
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}})
, giving the identification of spherical roots.

Finally, it is immediate that the map induces an order-preserving (in terms of the adherence relation) bijection between
\widetilde{B}
-orbits on
\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}
and
B=\widetilde{B}/N
-orbits on
\mathrm{X}
.
∎

The upshot is that if
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}),\Delta_{\mathrm{X}},\Omega^{(1)},\Omega^{(2)})
is the spherical data for
\mathrm{X}
, then
\Omega_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}),\Delta_{\mathrm{X}},\Omega^{(1)},\Omega^{(2)})
. Replacing
{\mathrm{G}}
with
\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}
if necessary, we now suppose
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}={\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
. There is an isomorphism
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}\cong\prod_{i}{\mathrm{G}}_{sc,i}\times\prod_{j}({\mathrm{G}}_{sc,j}\times{\mathrm{G}}_{sc,j})
, where each factor is
k
-simple and simply-connected and
\theta
stabilizes each
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc,i}
in the first product and acts as the swap automorphism on each factor in the second product. If we use the notation
\mathrm{H}_{sc}=\prod_{i}\mathrm{H}_{sc,i}\times\prod_{j}\mathrm{H}_{sc,j}
for the induced product on
\mathrm{H}\cap{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
, then
\mathrm{X}_{sc}=\prod_{i}\mathrm{X}_{i}\times\prod_{j}\mathrm{X}_{j}
and there is a product decomposition of the spherical data

\Omega_{\mathrm{X},sc}=\prod_{i}\Omega_{\mathrm{X}_{i}}\times\prod_{j}\Omega_{\mathrm{X}_{j}},\qquad\Omega_{\mathrm{X}_{i}}=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{X,i}),\Delta_{\mathrm{X}_{i}},\Omega_{i}^{(1)},\Omega_{i}^{(2)}).

Combining Lemmas
5.8
and
5.7
with the preceding discussion, for any symmetric
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
and any spherical root
\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
, there is a well-defined
k
-simple component

\mathrm{X}_{\gamma}
of
\mathrm{X}_{sc}
such that under the bijections

(5.4)

\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}\to\Delta_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}\to\Delta_{\mathrm{X}_{sc}}=\bigsqcup_{i}\Delta_{\mathrm{X}_{i}}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{j}\Delta_{\mathrm{X}_{j}},

\Gamma\cdot\gamma
is sent to
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}_{\gamma}}
.

5.4.
Rational involutions and
(\Gamma,\theta)
-indices

Suppose now that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a quasi-split connected reductive
k
-group. Let
\theta
be a
k
-rational involution of
{\mathrm{G}}
and fix a
(\theta,k)
-admissible pair
(\mathrm{A},\B)
. Recall the notion of
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index

\I=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Delta,\emptyset,\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{p},\sigma_{\ast},\theta^{\ast}).

from Section
4.3.3
, where we note that
\Delta_{0}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
since
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split and we have exchanged the notation
\Delta_{0}(\theta)
for
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{p}
in reference to
\mathrm{X}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. This provides a useful variant of the combinatorial spherical datum
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}=(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}),\Delta_{\mathrm{X}},\Omega^{(1)},\Omega^{(2)})
in the symmetric setting.

Remark 5.9
.

Aside from Proposition
4.10
, there are other benefits of utilizing
(\Gamma,\theta)
-indices. Indeed, when
\overline{\mathrm{X}}
is a symmetric
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
-variety in Proposition
3.6
, it is not immediate clear from the proof of
[
BG22
]
that the resulting homogeneous spherical
k
-variety
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is symmetric: that is, we do not obtain an involution
\theta
satisfying
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})
in this way. Beyond this, Borovoi and Gagliardi restrict to characteristic zero. This is likely not too serious for large enough positive characteristic, but working with
(\Gamma,\theta)
-indices resolves both of these concerns.

We first explain how to recover the combinatorial spherical datum
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
for
\mathrm{X}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
from the information of
\I
and the involution
\theta|_{\mathrm{A}}
, using the calculations in Appendix
B
. From this data, we set
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}=(\mathfrak{X},\Delta_{\mathrm{X}},\Omega^{(1)},\Omega^{(2)})
as follows:

(1)

Set
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})=\{\lambda-\theta(\lambda):\lambda\in X^{\ast}(\T)\}\subset X^{\ast}(\T)
;

(2)

set
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}:=\{\alpha-\theta(\alpha):\alpha\in\Delta,\theta(\alpha)\neq\alpha\}
. We then re-normalize through generators in
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
to obtain
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
;

(3)

set
{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}}{\mathcal{D}}(\alpha^{\vee})
, where the sets
{\mathcal{D}}(\alpha^{\vee})
are color data determined over
\overline{k}
as in Section
B.2
. More precisely, the index
\I
determines a symmetric pair
({\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}},\theta)
uniquely (up to isomorphism) over
\overline{k}
. Setting
\mathrm{H}_{\overline{k}}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}_{\overline{k}}
, the equivalence relation defined in Section
B.2
depends on the connected component group of
\mathrm{H}_{\overline{k}}
.
From this, we obtain the map

\rho\times\varsigma:{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X},\mathbb{Q}}\times\mathcal{P}(\Delta),

and we let
(\Omega^{(1)},\Omega^{(2)})
denote the corresponding sets, with the natural
\Gamma
-action induced from that on
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X}}\times\mathcal{P}(\Delta)
.

By Appendix
B
, this recovers
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
. It is now a straightforward check that the admissibility properties of the
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index imply that the
\Gamma
-action
\sigma_{\ast}
preserves
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}
. Since
{\mathrm{G}}
is assumed to be quasi-split, this agrees with the action on the combinatorial data of
\mathrm{X}
.

Note that this does not recover the
\Gamma
-action on
{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})
as it relies on passing to
\overline{k}
to obtain a uniquely determined (up to isomorphism) involution
\theta
. This is related to the fact that the
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index
\I
does not uniquely determine the involution
\theta
, reflecting the problem of
{\mathrm{G}}
-outer forms from Sections
2.4.1
and
3.4.4
. The results of
[
Hel00
]
toward classifying involutions with a fixed
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index unfortunately do not encode the group
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})
or the geometric cocycle of
\mathrm{X}
. We address this in Section
6
by computing
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
for all symmetric subgroups.

We end this section with the following lemma regarding the failure of the
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index
\I
to uniquely determine the involution
\theta
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split,
\mathrm{A}
is a maximally
k
-split maximal torus,
\B
is a Borel subgroup containing
\mathrm{A}
. Assume that
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are two involutions normally related to
A
and such that
\B
is maximally split for both involutions. Assume also that

\theta|_{\mathrm{A}}=\theta^{\prime}|_{\mathrm{A}}.

Then
({\mathrm{G}},\theta,\mathrm{A},\B)
and
({\mathrm{G}},\theta^{\prime},\mathrm{A},\B)
induce the same
(\Gamma,\theta)
-indices
\mathrm{I}
. Moreover, there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection
\Omega_{\mathrm{X}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Omega_{\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}
between the combinatorial data for the varieties
\mathrm{X}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
and
\mathrm{X}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta^{\prime}}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
.

Proof.

As argued in
[
Hel00
, Section 8]
, the association of the involutions to indices factors through the restriction to
\mathrm{A}
, implying the indices are congruent. More specifically, there exists
g\in{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
such that

\theta^{\prime}=\Ad(g)\circ\theta\circ\Ad(g)^{-1}=\Ad(s(g))\circ\theta.

Theorem 8.9 of
[
Hel00
]
proves the first claim, up to congruence.

In fact, the actual proof of
loc. cit.
implies that our assumption that the Borel subgroup
B
is maximally split for both involutions forces the indices to agree on the nose. Indeed, the actions agree on
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
and if
\Delta
denotes the simple roots associated to
B
, then
\Delta
gives a
\Gamma_{\theta}
-fundamental order for both involutions
[
Hel00
, Definition 5.25]
.

That
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
induce the same combinatorial invariants now follows from the previous discussion.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

This lemma fits naturally into the classification schema of
[
Hel00
]
, and illustrates the extent to which it determines the variety
{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. Sticking with the assumption that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split, let

\Ind(\Gamma,\theta):=\{\text{admissible $(\Gamma,\theta)$-indices on }(X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\Phi(\mathrm{A}))\}/\sim,

where
\sim
denotes the natural notion of congruence of indices
[
Hel00
, Section 5]
. If we set
\mathrm{Inv}_{k}({\mathrm{G}})
to be the set of
{\mathrm{G}}(k)
-conjugacy classes of
k
-rational involutions on
{\mathrm{G}}
, there is a natural surjective map

\rho_{{\mathrm{G}}}:\mathrm{Inv}_{k}({\mathrm{G}})\longrightarrow\Ind(\Gamma,\theta),

given by conjugating any involution so that
(\mathrm{A},\B)
is an admissible pair and passing to the induced
(\Gamma,\theta)
-index. A central result of
[
Hel00
]
is to classify the image of this map (cf. Section
4.3.3
).

To characterize the fibers of
\rho_{\mathrm{G}}
, Helminck shows that this map factors

\mathrm{Inv}_{k}({\mathrm{G}})\overset{\mu}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Inv}({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A}){\longrightarrow}\Ind(\Gamma,\theta),

where
\mathrm{Inv}({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A})
is obtained from
\mathrm{Inv}_{k}({\mathrm{G}})
by identifying involutions
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
if
\theta|_{\mathrm{A}}=\theta^{\prime}|_{\mathrm{A}}
, and the map
\mu
is the natural quotient map. One then considers the fibers of each factor. Lemma
5.10
states that involutions identified in
\mathrm{Inv}({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{A})
induce
\Gamma
-equivariantly isomorphic homogeneous spherical data. However, as Example
3.7
shows, two such involutions can possess distinct geometric cocycles resulting in
{\mathrm{G}}
-outer forms.

6.
Outer data for symmetric varieties

We continue to assume that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split over
k
and that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a symmetric variety. We compute the group
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
for symmetric varieties in Theorem
6.1
below. In particular, we isolate a particular class of symmetric varieties (referred to as Chevalley varieties; see Section
6.2
) such that every symmetric variety not in this class is well-adapted (cf. Definition
3.23
). Along the way, we also establish the existence of quasi-split forms in each
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner class for
\mathrm{X}
.
In the later Sections
9.2
and
9.3
, we interpret these results for symmetric varieties without type
N
roots in terms of the dual Hamiltonian
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}
-variety introduced in some generality by Ben-Zvi, Sakellaridis, and Venkatesh
[
BZSV23
]
.

Let
\theta
be the involution such that
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}).
Let
\mathrm{A}
be a
(\theta,k)
-admissible torus and let
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
denote the quotient by which
\mathrm{A}
acts on
\mathrm{X}
. We have the embedding
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
and the natural quotients
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
and
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
. Recall that a spherical variety is called well adapted if the natural surjective morphism

\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\longrightarrow\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})

is an isomorphism.

Theorem 6.1
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split over
k
and that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a symmetric
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety that has no factors of Chevalley type (see Definition
6.7
). Then
\mathrm{X}
is well adapted.

Remark 6.2
.

This statement is not as general as possible. For example, the calculation in Lemma
6.16
shows that those symmetric varieties of Chevalley type for
G_{2}
,
F_{4}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{8}
, as well as involutions of
\Spin_{2n}
arising from outer automorphisms are also well-adapted. We opt for this formulation as we will later adopt the assumption that
\mathrm{X}
has no factors of Chevalley type (cf. Assumption
6.21
).

Remark 6.3
.

The case of varieties of Chevalley type is handled in Proposition
6.13
, which remarks on a striking relationship between
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
in the Chevalley setting and the action of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
on the irreducible components of the regular part of the nilpotent cone of
\mathrm{X}
.

While not directly related to the preceding theorem, we establish the following result along the way.

Theorem 6.4
.

Suppose that
k
is perfect,
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split over
k
, and that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a symmetric
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety. There exists a
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner form
\mathrm{X}_{qs}
of
\mathrm{X}
and
x\in\mathrm{X}_{qs}(k)
such that the connected component of the identity of
\mathrm{Stab}_{{\mathrm{G}}}(x)=\mathrm{H}_{qs}
is quasi-split over
k
.

The assumption that
k
is perfect arises in the proof of Lemma
6.10
which relies on a lemma of Kottwitz, and can be dropped in most cases.

6.1.
Reduction to absolutely simple case

In this section, we reduce the proofs of Theorems
6.1
and
6.4
to the case where
{\mathrm{G}}
is simply connected and semi-simple. Note that Lemma
3.25
implies that it suffices to prove Theorem
6.1
with the assumption
\mathrm{H}
is connected; additionally, the statement of Theorem
6.4
only requires consideration of this setting. We therefore may assume
\mathrm{H}
is connected in the following.

Let
(\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}},\widetilde{\theta})
denote a
\theta
-compatible
z
-extension in the sense of Proposition
5.3
. If
p:\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}\to{\mathrm{G}}
is the covering map, and set
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}=p^{-1}(\mathrm{H})
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}=\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}\backslash\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

With the notation as above, there is a natural bijection

\Out_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H}).

Moreover,
\mathrm{X}
is well-adapted if and only if
\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}
is. Finally, a quasi-split
\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}
-inner form
\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}
exists over
k
if and only if a quasi-split
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner form
\mathrm{X}_{qs}
exists over
k
.

Proof.

It follows from the short exact sequence (
5.3
) that there is an isomorphism of group schemes

\pi_{0}(\Aut^{\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}))\simeq\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}),

Recalling that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\circ}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}
, we obtain a surjective map
\pi_{0}(\Aut^{\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}))\longrightarrow\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
.

Since
N^{\widetilde{\theta}}\subset Z(\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}})
, the image of

N_{\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}}(\widetilde{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\widetilde{\theta}})\to\Aut(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})

lies in the subgroup
\Aut(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},N^{\widetilde{\theta}})
of automorphisms preserving the subgroup
N^{\widetilde{\theta}}
, and factors through the quotient group
N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
. Finally, since
\mathrm{Inn}(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})=\mathrm{Inn}(\mathrm{H})
, we obtain a isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut^{\widetilde{\mathrm{G}}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{X}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathrm{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{out}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Recalling Lemma
5.8
, there is a canonical
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}=\Delta_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}^{dist}
which induces a natural isomorphism

\Aut_{d}(\widetilde{\mathrm{X}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}).

Using the commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\widetilde{\mathrm{X}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}),}

where both horizontal arrows are isomorphisms, we see that
\mathrm{X}
is well-adapted if and only if
\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}
is.

The final claim regarding quasi-split forms is obvious as
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
is a
z
-extension of
\mathrm{H}
.
∎

In particular, we are free to replace
({\mathrm{G}},\theta)
with
(\widetilde{\mathrm{G}},\widetilde{\theta})
and assume that
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}={\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
.

Let
({\mathrm{G}}_{sc},\theta)
denote the induced symmetric pair on the derived subgroup. Then
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}^{\theta}={\mathrm{G}}_{sc}\cap({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}
is connected, and we set
\mathrm{X}_{sc}={\mathrm{G}}_{sc}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
. Recall that
N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})=N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ})

[
Los09
, Lemma 3.1.1]
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Assume that
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}={\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
is simply connected and
\mathrm{H}
is connected. The induced map

(6.1)

\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}}(\mathrm{X}_{sc})\longrightarrow\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})

is surjective and induces an isomorphism
\Out_{\mathrm{X}_{sc}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}_{sc})\simeq\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
. Moreover,
\mathrm{X}
is well-adapted if
\mathrm{X}_{sc}
is. Finally, a quasi-split
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
-inner form
\mathrm{X}_{sc}
exists over
k
if and only if a quasi-split
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner form
\mathrm{X}_{qs}
exists over
k
.

Proof.

The surjectivity of (
6.1
) follows from the fact that
{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}
is a surjective map of reductive group schemes over
k
. More precisely, for any
n\in N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})(\overline{k})
, there exists
n^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}(\overline{k})
satisfying
\Ad(n)=\Ad(n^{\prime})
. But then it is clear that
n^{\prime}\in N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})(\overline{k})\cap{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}(\overline{k})\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}}({\mathrm{G}}_{sc}^{\theta})(\overline{k})
, so that the images of the further restriction to the adjoint action on
\mathrm{H}
agree.

Note that the surjective map (
6.1
) factors through
\Out_{{\mathrm{X}_{sc}}}({\mathrm{G}}_{sc}^{\theta})
. Setting
\mathrm{H}^{\flat}=\mathrm{H}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}})
and
\mathrm{H}^{\flat}_{sc}=\mathrm{H}^{\flat}\cap{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}
, this gives a surjective map

\Out_{{\mathrm{X}_{sc}}}({\mathrm{G}}_{sc}^{\theta})\simeq({\mathrm{G}}_{sc}^{\theta}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}}_{sc}))\backslash N_{{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}}({\mathrm{G}}_{sc}^{\theta})\to\mathrm{H}_{sc}^{\flat}\backslash N_{{\mathrm{G}}_{sc}}({\mathrm{G}}_{sc}^{\theta})\simeq\Out_{{\mathrm{X}}}(\mathrm{H}),

so that the claim holds if
\mathrm{H}^{\flat}_{sc}={\mathrm{G}}_{sc}^{\theta}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}}_{sc})
. Since we have assumed that
\mathrm{H}
is connected, it follows that
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}}))^{\circ}
, which gives the equality. There is thus a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}_{sc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out_{\mathrm{X}_{sc}}({\mathrm{G}}_{sc}^{\theta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H}),}

where the bottom horizontal arrow is an isomorphism. Since
\mathrm{H}
is connected,
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}
is in natural bijection with
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}_{sc}}^{dist}
via Lemma
5.7
, and an argument mirroring the one in the proof of Lemma
6.5
now shows that
\mathrm{X}
is well-adapted if and only if
\mathrm{X}_{sc}
is.

The final claim about quasi-split forms is, again, straightforward.
∎

In particular, to prove Theorems
6.1
and
6.4
it suffices to assume that
{\mathrm{G}}
is semi-simple and simply connected and that
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
is connected, relying on the preceding Lemmas to reduce to this case.

6.2.
Varieties of Chevalley type

We now describe the notion of Chevalley type as in the statement of Theorems
6.1
and
6.4
. Recall that an involution
\theta
of
{\mathrm{G}}
is called a
Chevalley involution
if there exists a
k
-rational maximal torus
\mathrm{A}
such that
\theta|_{\mathrm{A}}
is the inversion automorphism. It is natural to call a symmetric variety
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a Chevalley symmetric variety if the associated involution is a Chevalley involution. We introduce a slightly broader notion in order to isolate a family of symmetric varieties for which
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
does not have a relation to distinguished roots.

Definition 6.7
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is
k
-simple, simply-connected group over
k
and that
\theta
is an involution on
{\mathrm{G}}
. We say that
\theta
is an
involution of Chevalley type
if the spherical root system satisfying that
\rk(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})>1
and there is at most one Galois orbit of spherical roots
\Gamma\cdot\alpha_{\mathrm{X}}\subset\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
not of type
N
.

More generally, suppose
{\mathrm{G}}
is a connected reductive group,
\theta
an involution, and
\mathrm{X}
a symmetric variety associated to
\theta
. We say that
\mathrm{X}
has
a factor of Chevalley type
if
\mathrm{X}_{sc}
has a
k
-simple factor of Chevalley type.

The next lemma explains the somewhat unnatural terminology.

Lemma 6.8
.

Suppose
{\mathrm{G}}
is
k
-simple, simply-connected group. If
\theta
is an involution of Chevalley type, then there is a finite extension
K/k
and an absolutely simple
K
-group
{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
with an involution of Chevalley type
\theta^{\prime}
such that
({\mathrm{G}},\theta)=(\Res_{K/k}({\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}),\Res_{K/k}(\theta^{\prime}))
. The pair
({\mathrm{G}}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
fits into one of the following two cases:

(1)

\theta^{\prime}
is a Chevalley involution on
{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
, and

(2)

If
\theta^{\prime}
is not a Chevalley involution, the
{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
is a
K
-form of
\Spin(V)
for some quadratic space
V
over
K
, and
{{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}}^{\theta^{\prime}}=\Spin(V_{1})\times^{\mu_{2}}\Spin(V_{2})
for some decomposition
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
such that

\begin{cases}n>\min\{\dim(V_{1}),\dim(V_{2})\}\geq 2&:\dim(V)=2n+1,\\
n>\min\{\dim(V_{1}),\dim(V_{2})\}\geq 3&:\dim(V)=2n.\end{cases}

Proof.

Over
\overline{k}
, there exists a single conjugacy class of Chevalley involutions. Suppose that
({\mathrm{G}},\theta_{Ch})
is such an involution, and let
\mathrm{A}
be a maximally
\theta_{Ch}
-split torus. There exists a
\theta_{Ch}
-split Borel subgroup containing
\mathrm{A}
; with respect to this Borel, we compute that

\mathrm{X}^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})=2X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}),\quad\text{ and }\quad\Delta^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}=2\Delta.

In particular, the spherical roots are obtained by sending
2\alpha
to
\alpha
only if
\alpha\in 2X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
. This occurs only for the long simple root of type
C_{n}
, giving the first claim.

The second claim may be shown by a case-by-case analysis, checking the root systems of symmetric pairs
({\mathrm{G}},\theta)
with
{\mathrm{G}}
absolutely simple and simply connected. We simply show that the claimed varieties are of Chevalley type. Suppose that
V
is a quadratic space of dimension
d
, and let
{\mathrm{G}}=\Spin(V)
. Suppose that
\theta
is an involution such that
\mathrm{H}=\Spin(V_{1})\times_{\mu_{2}}\Spin(V_{2})
with
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
denoting an orthogonal decomposition; we may assume that
\dim(V_{1})=m\leq[d/2]
. Note that
\theta
is inner unless
\dim(V)
is even and
m
is odd. Let
\mathrm{X}
denote the associated symmetric variety.

A simple computation shows that if
\Delta=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
are the simple roots (aligning with the numbering of Bourbaki), then

\Delta^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}=\{2\alpha_{1},\ldots,2\alpha_{m-1},\gamma_{m}\},

where

\gamma_{m}=\begin{cases}2(\alpha_{m}+\cdots+\alpha_{n})&:d=2n+1,\\
2(\alpha_{m}+\cdots+\alpha_{n-2})+\alpha_{n-1}+\alpha_{n}&:d=2n.\end{cases}

In both case,
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})=\sspan_{\mathbb{Z}}\{2e_{1},\ldots,2e_{m},e_{1}+\cdots+e_{m}\}
, so that
\alpha_{i}\notin X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
except when
m\leq 2
. When
m>2
, this implies that
\{2\alpha_{1},\ldots,2\alpha_{m-1}\}
are roots of type
N
(
A
).

When
m=2
, then
\alpha_{1}=(e_{1}+e_{2})-2e_{2}\in X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
so that
\alpha_{1}
is a distinguished root of type (
A
). When
\dim(V)
is even,
\mathrm{X}
has no roots of type
N
, while when
\dim(V)
is odd,
\gamma_{2}
is of type
N
(
B
).

When
d=2n+1
and
m=1
(so that
\mathrm{X}
has rank
1
),
\gamma_{1}=2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
with
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}
a distinguished root of type (
B
), while
\gamma_{m}^{\prime}\notin X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
as soon as
m>1
; in this range,
\gamma_{m}
is of type
N
(
B
).

When
d=2n
,
\gamma_{m}
is always a root of type
G
. When
m=1
(so that
\mathrm{X}
has rank
1
),
\gamma_{m}=2\gamma_{m}^{\prime}
with
\gamma_{m}^{\prime}\in X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})\setminus\mathbb{Z}\Phi
is of type (
D
). Otherwise
\gamma_{m}^{\prime}\notin X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
.

∎

6.3.
The simply-connected, semi-simple case

Returning to the proofs of Theorems
6.1
and
6.4
, we may assume
{\mathrm{G}}
is simply connected and semi-simple. Write
{\mathrm{G}}=\prod_{i}{\mathrm{G}}_{i}\times\prod_{j}({\mathrm{G}}_{j}\times{\mathrm{G}}_{j})
, where each factor is
k
-simple, simply-connected, and
\theta
stabilizes each
{\mathrm{G}}_{i}
in the first product and acts as the swap automorphism on each factor in the second product. Then
\mathrm{X}=\prod_{i}\mathrm{X}_{i}\times\prod_{j}\mathrm{X}_{j}
and we have decompositions

\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})=\prod_{i}\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}_{i}}(\mathrm{X}_{i})\times\prod_{j}\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}_{j}\times{\mathrm{G}}_{j}}(\mathrm{X}_{j});

the morphism
\mathrm{out}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\mathrm{G}}}
similarly factors to give

\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})=\prod_{i}\Out_{\mathrm{X}_{i}}(\mathrm{H}_{i})\times\prod_{j}\Out_{\mathrm{X}_{j}}(\mathrm{H}_{j});

in fact, it is easy to see in the latter case that

\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}_{j}\times{\mathrm{G}}_{j}}(\mathrm{X}_{j})\simeq Z({\mathrm{G}}_{j}),

so that
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})=\{1\}
. Since
\mathrm{X}_{j}
has no distinguished roots, this is compatible with Theorem
6.1
. Moreover, in this case
{\mathrm{G}}_{j}\times{\mathrm{G}}_{j}
is quasi-split if and only if
\mathrm{H}_{j}\simeq{\mathrm{G}}_{j}
is so that Theorem
6.4
also holds in this case.

Since a similar decomposition holds for
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
, we may project to the
i^{th}
factor, thereby reducing the claim to the setting that
{\mathrm{G}}
is
k
-simple and simply connected, with
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
. There thus exists a finite separable extension
k^{\prime}/k
and an absolutely simple group
{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
over
k^{\prime}
such that
{\mathrm{G}}\simeq\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}({\mathrm{G}}^{\prime})
uniquely up to unique isomorphism of pairs
(k^{\prime},{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime})\simeq(k^{\prime\prime},{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime\prime})

[
Con10
, Example 6.4.6]
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is
k
-simple and simply connected, and
\theta
is an involution with
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
. Then either

(1)

({\mathrm{G}},\theta)
is a Galois symmetric pair; that is, there exists quadratic extension
K/k
and a
k
-simple group
\mathrm{H}
such that
{\mathrm{G}}=\Res_{K/k}(\mathrm{H}_{K})
, and
\theta
is a Galois involution; or

(2)

there is an involution
\theta^{\prime}
of
{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
over
k^{\prime}
such that
\theta=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}(\theta^{\prime})
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward application of the general result of
[
CGP10
, Proposition A.5.14]
where
{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}={\mathrm{G}}^{\prime\prime}
and
k^{\prime}=k^{\prime\prime}
. In the first case,
\alpha:k^{\prime}\to k^{\prime}
is a non-trivial automorphism over a subfield of index
2
, while
\alpha=1_{k^{\prime}}
in the second case.
∎

The next two lemmas complete the proof of Theorem
6.4
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is
k
-simple, simply connected, and quasi-split over
k
with
\theta
a
k
-rational involution. Set
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
and
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. Suppose one of the following holds:

(1)

there exists quadratic extension
K/k
and a
k
-simple group
\mathrm{H}
such that
{\mathrm{G}}=\Res_{K/k}(\mathrm{H}_{K})
, and
\theta
is a Galois involution,

(2)

k
is perfect and
\theta
is an inner involution. That is, we assume that
\theta=\Ad(g_{\theta})\in{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}(k)
for some
g_{\theta}\in{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
.

Then there exists a
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner form
\mathrm{X}_{qs}\simeq\mathrm{H}_{qs}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
with
\mathrm{H}_{qs}^{\circ}
quasi-split over
k
.

Proof.

We first consider (
1
), so that
{\mathrm{G}}=\Res_{K/k}(\mathrm{H}_{K})
for a quadratic extension
K/k
. Letting
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}
denote a quasi-split inner form of
\mathrm{H}
, we see that
{\mathrm{G}}^{\ast}=\Res_{K/k}(\mathrm{H}^{\ast}_{K})
is also quasi-split over
k
and an inner form of
{\mathrm{G}}
. This implies the existence of a
k
-rational isomorphism
\psi:{\mathrm{G}}^{\ast}\to{\mathrm{G}}
. The image
\psi(\mathrm{H}^{\ast})
gives the desired
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner twist.

Now suppose that
\theta
is inner in the sense of (
2
). Thus we have a semi-simple element
\Ad(g_{\theta})\in{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}(k)
such that the centralizer in
{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}
is
{\mathrm{G}}_{ad,\theta}:=N_{{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}}(\mathrm{H}_{Z})
. Here,
\mathrm{H}_{Z}:=\mathrm{H}/(Z({\mathrm{G}})\cap\mathrm{H})
is the image of
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
in the adjoint quotient. Lemma 3.3 of
[
Kot82
]
now implies the existence of an element
\theta^{\ast}=\Ad(g^{\ast}_{\theta})\in{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}(k)
which is stably conjugate to
\theta
and satisfies that
{\mathrm{G}}_{ad,{\theta^{\ast}}}^{\circ}=:\mathrm{H}_{Z}^{\ast}
is quasi-split over
k
. Unwinding the definitions, we see that
\theta^{\ast}
gives an involution of
{\mathrm{G}}
in the same
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner class as
\theta
such that if
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta^{\ast}}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
, then
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}\to\mathrm{H}^{\ast}_{Z}
is an isogeny. In particular, since
\mathrm{H}_{Z}
is quasi-split, so is
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}
.
∎

In particular, the only cases which remain to prove Theorem
6.4
are symmetric pairs of the form
(\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}({\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}),\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}(\theta^{\prime}))
such that
{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
is absolutely simple, simply connected, and quasi-split over
k^{\prime}
and
\theta^{\prime}
induces a nontrivial outer automorphism of
{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
. It thus suffices to base change to
k^{\prime}
and assume that
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{H})
is an absolutely simple symmetric pair.

Lemma 6.11
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split and absolutely simple over
k
and
\theta
is a
k
-involution which induces a nontrivial outer automorphism of
{\mathrm{G}}
. There exists a
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner twist of
\theta
such that
{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
is quasi-split.

Proof.

Consider the short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}\longrightarrow\Aut({\mathrm{G}})\longrightarrow\Out({\mathrm{G}})\longrightarrow 1.

By assumption, the involution
\theta\in\Aut({\mathrm{G}})(k)
induces by assumption a non-trivial element
[\theta]\in\Out({\mathrm{G}})(k)
(in particular,
{\mathrm{G}}
is a quasi-split
k
-form of
\SL_{n}
,
\mathrm{Spin}_{2n}
, or
E_{6,sc}
). Choose a
k
-rational Borel
\B\subset{\mathrm{G}}
, a maximally
k
-split maximal torus
\mathrm{A}\subset\B
, and a
\Gamma
-stable pinning
\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Delta}
. This induces a splitting
p:\Out({\mathrm{G}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Aut({\mathrm{G}},\B,\mathrm{A},\{x_{\alpha}\})\subset\Aut({\mathrm{G}})
and we set
\theta^{\ast}=p([\theta])
. Then
\theta^{\ast}
is a
k
-rational involution such that
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}:={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta^{\ast}}
is quasi-split. Moreover, there exists
g^{\ast}\in{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
such that
\Ad(g^{\ast})\in{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}(k)
and
\theta=\Ad(g^{\ast})\circ\theta^{\ast}
. This implies
\theta^{\ast}(g^{\ast})=(g^{\ast})^{-1}
.

We thus consider the group cohomology

H^{1}(\langle\theta^{\ast}\rangle,{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k}))=\{g\in{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k}):\theta^{\ast}(g)=g^{-1}\}/{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})

to determine the possible involutions obtained in this fashion and verify the claim case-by-case.

We proceed case-by-case:

(1)

(type
A_{2n}
, for
n\geq 1
) The outer automorphism induces the Chevalley involution
\theta^{\ast}
, so that
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}
is a
k
-form of
\SO_{2n+1}
. In this case,
H^{1}(\langle\theta^{\ast}\rangle,{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k}))=\{\ast\}
so that
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}
is a
{\mathrm{G}}
-inner form of
\mathrm{H}
.

(2)

(type
A_{2n-1}
, for
n\geq 2
) The unique outer automorphism induces the Chevalley involution
\theta^{\ast}
, so that
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}
is a
k
-form of
\mathrm{SO}_{2n}
. In this case,
H^{1}(\langle\theta^{\ast}\rangle,{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k}))=\{\theta^{\ast},\theta_{2}\}
has two elements. Here,
{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta_{2}}
is of type
C_{n}
. In either case, since
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split, the split form
\Sp_{2n}
injects into
{\mathrm{G}}
.

(3)

(type
D_{n}
, for
n\geq 4
) In this case, it is simpler to work with the special orthogonal group rather than
\Spin_{2n}
. We may argue geometrically in this case. Thus, we let
(V,q)
be a quadratic space of dimension
2n
such that
{\mathrm{G}}=\SO(V)
is a quasi-split group. If

(V,q)\simeq(V_{a},q_{a})\oplus(V_{s},q_{s})

is the Witt decomposition of
V
, where
(V_{a},q_{a})
is anisotropic and
(V_{s},q_{s})
is a split quadratic space. Thus,
V_{s}
is isometric to a direct sum
H_{2}^{\oplus k}
of hyperbolic planes. Since
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split,
\dim(V_{a})\leq 2
so that
\dim(V_{a})\in\{0,2\}
.

All outer involutions of type
D
correspond to orthogonal decomposition
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
where both summands have odd dimension. Moreover, such decompositions are all
{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k})
-conjugate, so it suffices to exhibit an orthogonal decomposition such that the stabilizing subgroup is quasi-split.
But for any partition
2n=(2k_{1}+1)+(2k_{2}+1)
, it is elementary to find a decomposition with
\dim(V_{i})=2k_{i}+1
and
\SO(V_{i})
quasi-split: fix an orthonormal basis
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
for either
V_{a}
if
\dim(V_{a})=2
or for a fixed hyperbolic plane if
\dim(V_{a})=0
, and set
V_{i}=H_{2}^{\oplus k_{i}}\oplus ke_{i}
.

(4)

(type
E_{6}
) The group
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}
is of type
C_{4}
, and
H^{1}(\langle\theta^{\ast}\rangle,{\mathrm{G}}(\overline{k}))=\{\theta^{\ast},\theta_{2}\}
, where
\mathrm{H}_{2}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta_{2}}
has type
F_{4}
. If
{\mathrm{G}}
is split, then both split forms embed to
{\mathrm{G}}
. If
{\mathrm{G}}
is the quasi-split form of
E_{6,ad}
of rank
4
, it is also true that both the split forms of
\Sp_{8}
and
F_{4}
occur as symmetric subgroups.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Finally, we make a few remarks toward Theorem
6.1
. Noting that
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})=\{1\}
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}=\emptyset
when
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{H})
is a Galois symmetric pair, we may restrict out attention to the case when
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{H})
is the Weil restriction of a canonically associated (up to canonical isomorphism of pairs) absolutely simple symmetric pair
({\mathrm{G}}^{\prime},\mathrm{H}^{\prime})
over a finite separable extension
k^{\prime}/k
.

Lemma 6.12
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}\simeq\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}({\mathrm{G}}^{\prime})
and
\theta\simeq\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}(\theta^{\prime})
for a
k^{\prime}
-rational involution
\theta^{\prime}
on
{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
. There is a natural isomorphism

\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\simeq\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}(\Out_{\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{H}^{\prime})).

Proof.

If
{\mathrm{H}^{\prime}}^{\flat}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{H}^{\prime})
is the subgroup corresponding to
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}^{\flat}
. Then

N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}(N_{{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{H}^{\prime}))

and
\mathrm{H}^{\flat}=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}({\mathrm{H}^{\prime}}^{\flat})
corresponds to
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\flat}
in

\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})\simeq\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}(\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{X}^{\prime})).

Thus

\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\simeq N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})/\mathrm{H}^{\flat}\simeq\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}(N_{{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{H}^{\prime}))/\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}({\mathrm{H}^{\prime}}^{\flat})\simeq\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}(\Out_{\mathrm{X}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{H}^{\prime})),

where the last isomorphism is shown in
[
CGP10
, Corollary A.5.4(3)]
.

∎

The calculation of
\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})
in (
3.10
) now implies that
\mathrm{X}=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}(\mathrm{X}^{\prime})
is well-adapted as a
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety if and only if
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
is well-adapted as a
{\mathrm{G}}^{\prime}
-variety.

6.3.1.
The absolutely simple case

We have reduced the calculation to the case when
{\mathrm{G}}
is absolutely simple and simply connected and
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
, where we shall compute
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
directly. We summarize these calculations below.

Proposition 6.13
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is absolutely simple and simply connected over
k
and
\theta
is an involution. Set
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
and
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
.

(1)

Suppose that
\mathrm{X}
is not a variety of Chevalley type. Then

\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\simeq\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X}).

(2)

Suppose that
\mathrm{X}
is a variety of Chevalley type. If
\mathrm{X}
is a form of either

\Spin_{2}\times^{\mu_{2}}\Spin_{2n-1}\backslash\Spin_{2n+1}
or
\GL_{n}\backslash\Sp_{2n}
for
n\neq 1
,

or if
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})=\{1\}
, it is well adapted. Otherwise, the map

\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\to\Aut_{d}(\mathrm{X})

is not injective. In each case, the set of
\mathrm{H}
-orbits on the regular part of the nilpotent cone
\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{X}}^{reg}
gives a
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
-torsor.

This proposition completes the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

The rest of this section proves this proposition in a case-by-case fashion. First note that the claim holds when
N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
is contained in a proper parabolic subgroup
P\subset{\mathrm{G}}
, since then
\mathrm{H}=N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
is of Hermitian type. Indeed, since
N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})\supset\mathrm{H}
,
\mathrm{H}_{\overline{k}}
is contained in a proper Levi subgroup
L
of
P
. This forces
\mathrm{H}=L
and the claim now follows from standard results on normalizers of Levi subgroups. In particular,
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
is trivial when
N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
is contained in a proper parabolic subgroup. Note that Losev shows in
[
Los09
, Section 4.3]
that no distinguished roots exist in this case, so that this verifies cases of the Proposition.

We may now assume throughout this section that
N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})
is not contained in a proper parabolic subgroup of
{\mathrm{G}}
. Recall the quotient and symmetrization maps

{\mathrm{G}}\overset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{X}\overset{s}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{G}};

Lemma 1 of
[
Vus90
]
implies that

\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})\simeq s(\mathrm{X})\cap Z({\mathrm{G}})\subset Z({\mathrm{G}})^{-}=\{a\in Z({\mathrm{G}}):\theta(z)=z^{-1}\}.

More precisely, if
\mathrm{A}
is a
(\theta,k)
-admissible torus, then we obtain two identifications under the symmetrization map
s

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\simeq\mathrm{A}^{-}\cap Z({\mathrm{G}})\cong\{\tau(a)=a^{2}\in\mathrm{X}:a\in A^{Z}\},

where
A^{Z}:=\{a\in A^{-}:a^{2}\in Z({\mathrm{G}})\}
. Thus,

\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\simeq(\mathrm{A}^{-}\cap Z({\mathrm{G}}))/\tau(Z({\mathrm{G}})).

Lemma 6.14
.

Suppose
{\mathrm{G}}
is absolutely simple and simply connected over
k
,
\theta
is an involution and
\mathrm{X}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. When
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}\neq\emptyset
,

X^{\ast}(\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H}))\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},

with the non-trivial element given by the
doubling automorphisms
of
[
Los09
]
.

Proof.

Roots of type
B
:
Suppose that
\mathrm{X}
possesses a distinguished root of type (
B
). Arguing as in the proof of
[
Los09
, Proposition 4.2.1]
(this is where we use the assumption that
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{H})
is not contained in a proper parabolic subgroup), such roots occur only if
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a
k
-form of

\Spin_{2n}\backslash\Spin_{2n+1},\qquad\text{or}\qquad\Sp_{2n}\times\Sp_{2n}\backslash\Sp_{4n}.

In each case,
|\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}|=1
and the doubling automorphism is outer.

Double roots:
By Lemma 7.3 of
[
Kno96
]
,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}=\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{X})
unless

\mathrm{rank}(Z(\mathrm{H})/Z({\mathrm{G}})\cap\mathrm{H})>1.

The symmetric varieties of this form are called of
Hermitian type

[
Vus90
, (2.4)]
and
\mathrm{H}_{\overline{k}}
is a Levi subgroup of
{\mathrm{G}}_{\overline{k}}
. Since
{\mathrm{G}}
is absolutely simple,
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{H})
has rank
1
.

Passing to
\overline{k}
, we may suppose now that
\mathrm{H}
is a Levi subgroup of
{\mathrm{G}}
. If
s\in N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{H})(\overline{k})
such that
\Ad(s)
acts trivially on
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{H})
, then
s
acts trivially on
Z(\mathrm{H})
. But this then forces
s\in\mathrm{H}
so that
\Ad(s)=1\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})
. In particular, any non-trivial element of
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}(\overline{k})
acts non-trivially on
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{H})
. Lemma 7.3 of
[
Kno96
]
now forces
\varphi\notin\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\sharp}(\overline{k})
. Lemma
3.22
now gives the claim.

In each case, one has
|\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}|=1
.
∎

The next two lemmas complete the proof of Proposition
6.13
. They also highlight the connection with the irreducible components of the regular locus of the nilpotent cone in the Chevalley-type case. More precisely, recall that the involution
\theta
induces a grading

\mathfrak{g}=\Lie({\mathrm{G}})=\mathfrak{g}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{1},

where
\mathfrak{g}_{i}=\{X\in\mathfrak{g}:d\theta(X)=(-1)^{i}X\}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}_{1}\simeq T^{\ast}_{x_{0}}(\mathrm{X})
is the infinitesimal symmetric variety. If
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathfrak{g}
denotes the nilpotent cone of
\mathfrak{g}
, one then defines

\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{X}}:=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\cap\mathcal{N}.

Over
\overline{k}
, there is then a unique
N_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{H})
-orbit of
regular nilpotent elements

\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{X}}^{reg}
, which decomposes into finitely many geometric orbits of
\mathrm{H}
. In Sections 5 and 6 of
[
Lev07
]
(see also
[
Sek84
]
for the original characteristic zero calculation), the number of geometric
\mathrm{H}
-orbits in
\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{X}}^{reg}
is computed.

We saw previously that
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\simeq(\mathrm{A}^{-}\cap Z({\mathrm{G}}))/\tau(Z({\mathrm{G}}))
. This quotient is computed for the relevant symmetric pairs in
[
Lev07
, Section 6.4]
in the analysis of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{X}}^{reg}
. As shown in Section 5 of
[
Lev07
]
, the set of connected components
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{X}}^{reg})
is naturally a torsor of a quotient of
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
(in the notation of
[
Lev07
, Theorem 5.17]
, this is
(\mathrm{A}^{-}\cap Z({\mathrm{G}}))/\tau(C)
), so that
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
acts transitively on
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{X}^{reg})
.

In particular,
(\mathrm{A}^{-}\cap Z({\mathrm{G}}))/\tau(Z({\mathrm{G}}))
is computed for many symmetric pairs in
loc. cit.
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is absolutely simple and simply connected, and that
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
. Suppose
\theta
is not of Chevalley type and
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{dist}=\emptyset
. Then
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})=\{1\}
.

Proof.

The relevant calculations are carried out in the proofs of Lemmas 6.19 and 6.21, as well as the discussion leading up to Proposition 6.22 of
[
Lev07
]
. A case-by-case check establishes the claim.
∎

Lemma 6.16
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is absolutely simple and simply connected, and assume that
\mathrm{X}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is of Chevalley type. Then
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})
acts simply transitively on the set of
\mathrm{H}
-orbits on the regular part of the nilpotent cone
\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{X}}^{reg}
.

(1)

Suppose
\theta
is a Chevalley involution. Then

X^{\ast}(\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H}))=(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{\Omega_{2}},

where
\Omega_{2}:=\{\omega\in X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})\text{ minuscule},2\omega\in\mathbb{Z}\Phi\}
.

(2)

Suppose
\mathrm{X}
is a
k
-form of
\Spin(V)/\Spin(V_{1})\times^{\mu_{2}}\Spin(V_{2})
with
|\dim(V_{1})-\dim(V_{2})|\geq 2
. Then

X^{\ast}(\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H}))=(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{\Omega_{2}},

where

\Omega_{2}:=\begin{cases}\langle\omega_{sp}-\theta(\omega_{sp})\rangle&:\theta\text{ inner},\\
\emptyset&:\theta\text{ outer}.\end{cases}

When
\theta
is inner, the image of the fundamental weight of the (half-)spin representation(s) generates
X^{\ast}(\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H}))
.

Remark 6.17
.

In the case of Chevalley involutions, the set
\Omega_{2}
is equal to the set of minuscule fundamental weights except in type
A
, where it is trivial for
A_{2n}
and consists of the fundamental representation
\wedge^{n}V
of
\SL(V)
for type
A_{2n-1}
. In particular,

\#\Omega_{2}=\begin{cases}1&:{\mathrm{G}}\text{ of type }A_{2n-1},B_{n},C_{n},D_{2n+1},E_{7},\\
2&:{\mathrm{G}}\text{ of type }D_{2n},\\
0&:\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We handle the cases of Chevalley involutions first. In this case,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\simeq Z({\mathrm{G}})
, where

X^{\ast}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}})=2X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})/2\mathbb{Z}\Phi\simeq X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})/\mathbb{Z}\Phi=X^{\ast}(Z({\mathrm{G}})).

In particular, we see
\Out_{\mathrm{X}}(\mathrm{H})\simeq Z({\mathrm{G}})/Z({\mathrm{G}})^{2}
. These are listed in the following table, where in type
A_{n}
,
\delta\in\{0,1\}
with
n\equiv\delta\pmod{2}
.

Table 3.
Centers of simply connected groups

{\mathrm{G}}

A_{n}

B_{n}

C_{n}

D_{2n}

D_{2n+1}

E_{6}

E_{7}

G_{2}
,
F_{4}
,
E_{8}

Z({\mathrm{G}})

\mu_{n+1}

\mu_{2}

\mu_{2}

\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2}

\mu_{4}

\mu_{3}

\mu_{[1+\delta]}

\mu_{2}

\mu_{2}
