\frac{L(\phi_{a},\mathfrak{gl}_{a}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{\omega})L(\phi_{b}.\mathfrak{gl}_{b}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{\omega})}{L(\phi_{a},\mathfrak{gl}_{a})L(\phi_{b}.\mathfrak{gl}_{b})};

These additional terms correspond precisely to the
L
-values for the “endoscopic periods” associated to the endoscopic variety
{\mathrm{Y}}_{a}\times{\mathrm{Y}}_{b}
, but the precise point of evaluation needs to be corrected as these will correspond to the “non-tempered parameter” case in
[
BZSV23
, Section 14]
. This is compatible with the observation of Section
9.3
that the Lagrangian correspondence will not preserve the gradings. Once appropriated corrected in this way, the conjecture agrees with the evaluation of (regularized) unitary periods of Eisenstein series
[
FLO12
, Theorem 11.2]
, which expresses the period as a sum of
L
-values indexed by the fiber of the quadratic base change map from
\GL_{a}\times\GL_{b}
to
\Res_{E/k}(\GL_{a}\times\GL_{b})
.

10.2.
Pre-stabilization for the regular elliptic part

In this subsection, we establish the stabilization of the regular elliptic part of the relative trace formula associated to a symmetric variety. For this, we assume several preparatory results from
[
Les24
]
. For brevity, we omit the definition of several notations (eg. abelianized cohomology, etc.).

We now assume that
k
is a global field of characteristic zero. We also assume that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split over
k
,
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
is simply connected, and
Z({\mathrm{G}})
is anisotropic. We assume that
\mathrm{X}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is excellent in the sense of Definition
7.4
. Lemma
B.4
implies that this is enough to ensure that all semi-simple stabilizers in
\mathrm{X}
are geometrically connected. Set
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
. The assumptions on
{\mathrm{G}}
are not necessary and are adopted here only to avoid unhelpful technical complications. More importantly, we are forced to adopt the Assumption
7.15
, so that we have access to Proposition
7.16
. In particular, we need to work with the quotient stack

[\mathrm{X}\times\mathrm{X}]/{\mathrm{G}}\simeq\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{H}.

One of purposes of
[
Les24
]
is the formulate the cohomological prestabilization of the “stack-theoretic” relative trace formula for
\mathrm{X}
discussed below.

10.2.1.
The regular elliptic part of the
\mathrm{X}
-trace formula

We say that an element
x\in{\mathrm{X}}^{ss}(k)
is
\mathrm{H}
-elliptic if
Z(\mathrm{H}_{x})^{\circ}/(\mathrm{H}_{x}\cap Z({\mathrm{G}}))^{\circ}
is anisotropic over
k.
We say it is regular elliptic if it is
\mathrm{H}
-elliptic and regular semi-simple.

Let
\mathbb{A}
denote the adele ring of
k
and let
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{X}(\mathbb{A}))
. Consider the theta function

\Theta_{f}(h)=\sum_{x\in\mathrm{X}(k)}f(x\cdot h);

this is a locally-finite sum as
\mathrm{X}
is smooth
[
GH19
, Lemma 17.6.11]
. It gives an automorphic function on
\mathrm{H}(k)\backslash\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{A})
. In particular, for any
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{X}(\mathbb{A}))
there exist
f_{\xi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{A}))
for
\xi\in\ker^{1}(\mathrm{H},{\mathrm{G}};k)
such that

\Theta_{f}(h)=\sum_{\xi\in\ker^{1}(\mathrm{H},{\mathrm{G}};k)}\displaystyle\int_{[\mathrm{H}^{\xi}]}\left(\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathrm{G}}(k)}f_{\xi}(h^{\prime}\gamma h)\right)dh^{\prime},

where
\mathrm{H}^{\xi}
is a pure inner twist of
\mathrm{H}
representing the class
\xi
,
[\mathrm{H}^{\xi}]=\mathrm{H}^{\xi}(k)\backslash\mathrm{H}^{\xi}(\mathbb{A})
, and
dh^{\prime}
is an appropriately normalized Haar measure. For our purposes, we restrict to the sub-sum

\Theta^{re}_{f}(h)=\sum_{x\in\mathrm{X}^{re}(k)}f(x\cdot h)

over the regular elliptic locus of
\mathrm{X}(k)
.

Remark 10.1
.

We deal with the full elliptic locus in
[
Les24
]
, where certain new features arise. We point out where in the argument this occurs soon.

The period integral we are interested in is

\TF^{re}_{0}(f)=\displaystyle\int_{[\mathrm{H}]}\Theta^{re}_{f}(h)dh

where

[\mathrm{H}]=\mathrm{H}(k)\backslash\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{A}).

If
\mathrm{X}
is quasi-split, a standard unfolding of the right-hand side (subject to constraints on
f
) relates this sum to automorphic periods of automorphic forms on
{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{A})
, at least under local assumptions on
f
so that the respective spectral terms converge: this is the context of many “simple” relative trace formulae in the literature. More generally, one needs a regularization procedure to analyze the spectral side.

For cohomological reasons, we enlarge this sum. Due to the difference between global and local inner forms, we include a sum over the cohomology set

\ker^{1}(k,\mathrm{H}):=\ker[H^{1}(k,\mathrm{H})\overset{Has}{\longrightarrow}H^{1}(\mathbb{A},\mathrm{H})],

which is trivial if
\mathrm{H}
satisfies the Hasse principle. For
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathrm{X}(\mathbb{A}))
, we now consider the sum

\TF^{re}_{\mathrm{X}}(f)=\sum_{\beta\in\ker^{1}(k,\mathrm{H})}\TF^{re}_{\beta}(f),

where
\TF^{re}_{\beta}(f)
is the analogue of
\TF^{re}_{0}(f)
with the sum over the regular elliptic orbits of
\mathrm{X}^{\xi}(k)
.
Since
\mathrm{H}
is connected, the cohomology set
\ker^{1}(k,\mathrm{H})
is finite, implying that this sum is absolutely convergent. This step is quite natural and occurs, largely for purposes of exposition, in the stabilization of the trace formula as discussed in
[
Ngô10
, Section 1.13]
.

The necessity of Assumption
7.15
motivates us to further extend the sum as follows. Define

\im^{1}(F,\mathrm{H}):=\im[H^{1}(F,\mathrm{H})\overset{Has}{\longrightarrow}H^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{F},\mathrm{H})].

Let
\underline{f}:=(f^{\xi})\in\bigoplus_{\xi\in\im^{1}(F,\mathrm{H})}C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{X}^{\xi}(\mathbb{A}))
, and consider the sum

(10.2)

\displaystyle\TF^{re}_{\mathrm{X}}(\underline{f})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\xi\in\im^{1}(F,\mathrm{H})}\TF^{re}_{\mathrm{X}^{\xi}}(f^{\xi}),

where

\TF^{re}_{\mathrm{X}^{\xi}}(f^{\xi})=\sum_{\beta\in Has^{-1}(\xi)}\TF^{re}_{\beta}(f^{\xi}),

By definition, for any collection
\underline{f}
there are only finitely many non-zero terms on the right-hand side. In particular, this sum is absolutely convergent as well.

The natural unfolding gives the formula

\TF_{\mathrm{X}}^{re}(\underline{f})=\sum_{a\in(\mathrm{X}^{re}//\mathrm{H})(k)}\tau(\mathrm{H}_{x})\sum_{\xi\in H^{1}(F,\mathrm{H})}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x\in\mathrm{X}^{\xi,re}(k)/\sim}}{{\textbf{car}^{\mathfrak{e}}(x)=a}}}\Orb_{x}(f^{\xi}),

where
\textbf{car}^{\xi}:\mathrm{X}^{\xi}\to\mathrm{X}^{\sslash}\mathrm{H}
is the categorical quotient,
\tau(\mathrm{H}_{x})
is the Tamagawa number, and

\Orb_{x}(f^{\xi}):=\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{H}_{x}^{\xi}(\mathbb{A})\backslash\mathrm{H}^{\xi}(\mathbb{A})}f^{\xi}(x\cdot h)dh

is the adelic orbital integral. This is absolutely convergent by our ellipticity assumption. Note that for any two classes
[x]
and
[x^{\prime}]
lying over
a\in[\mathrm{X}^{rss}\sslash\mathrm{H}](k)
, the stabilizers
\mathrm{H}_{x}
and
\mathrm{H}_{x^{\prime}}
are pure inner forms. Thus, the Tamagawa numbers agree
[
Kot88
]
and we set the common values as
\tau(\mathrm{H}_{a})
.

The following theorem is the first pre-stabilization of this sum, and is a special case of the formula established in
[
Les24
]
.

Theorem 10.2
.

Suppose
\underline{f}=(\prod_{v}f^{\xi}_{v})\in\bigoplus_{\xi}C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathrm{X}^{\xi}(\mathbb{A}))
is a family of pure tensors. With notation as above, we have

(10.3)

\displaystyle\TF_{\mathrm{X}}^{re}(\underline{f})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in(\mathrm{X}^{re}\sslash\mathrm{H})(k)}\sum_{\kappa\in H^{1}_{ab}(\mathbb{A}/k,\mathrm{H}_{x})^{D}}\Orb_{x}^{\kappa}(\underline{f}),

where for
\kappa\in H^{1}_{ab}(\mathbb{A}/k,\mathrm{H}_{x})^{D}
, we define the adelic
\kappa
-orbital integral by

\Orb_{x}^{\kappa}(\underline{f})=\prod_{v}\Orb_{x}^{\kappa_{v}}(\underline{f}_{v})

is a product of local
\kappa
-orbital integrals (here,
e(\mathrm{H}_{x^{\prime}})
denotes the Kottwitz sign)

\displaystyle\Orb^{\kappa}_{x}(\underline{f}_{v})=\sum_{[x^{\prime}]\in[\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{H}]_{x}(k)}e(\mathrm{H}_{x^{\prime}})\langle\kappa,\inv(x,x^{\prime})\rangle\Orb_{x^{\prime}}(f^{\xi(x^{\prime})}_{v}).

The necessary definitions and details will appear in
[
Les24
]
. The important upshot is that the regular elliptic part of the relative trace formula has been expressed as a sum (
10.3
) of
\kappa
-orbital integrals indexed by pairs
(a,\kappa)
where

•

an elliptic invariant
a\in(\mathrm{X}^{re}\sslash\mathrm{H})(k)
encodes a regular elliptic stable orbit, and

•

a character
\kappa\in H^{1}_{ab}(\mathbb{A}/k,\mathrm{H}_{x})^{D}
of the abelianized cohomology of the centralizer of
x
in
\mathrm{H}
.

Our present goal is to re-index this sum in terms of our notion of relative endoscopic data.

Remark 10.3
.

We hasten to remark that this some is simpler than the relative trace formula associated to a single period of the form

\displaystyle\int_{[\mathrm{H}]}\int_{[\mathrm{H}]}\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathrm{G}}(k)}f(h_{1}\gamma h_{2})dh_{1}dh_{2};

Isolating individual terms of this form in the stabilization is delicate and introduces contributions of new endoscopic varieties (such contributions can be seen locally in
[
BPW23
]
). This will be clarified in future work, and ultimately relies on the existence results of the present article.

10.2.2.
Pre-stabilization of the RTF

We recall the relevant statement of Tate–Nakayama duality.

Lemma 10.4
.

[
Lab99
, Proposition 1.7.3]

Suppose
{\mathrm{G}}
is a connected reductive group over
k
. If
k
is local, there is a canonical injection

H^{1}_{ab}(k,{\mathrm{G}})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(Z(\check{{\mathrm{G}}})^{\Gamma})^{D}

which is bijective when
k
is non-archimedean. If
k
is global, there is a canonical bijection

H_{ab}^{1}(\mathbb{A}/k,{\mathrm{G}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\pi_{0}(Z(\check{{\mathrm{G}}})^{\Gamma})^{D}.

∎

Now suppose that
x=x_{a}\in\mathrm{X}^{\xi,re}(k)
lies over
a
for some
\xi\in H^{1}(k,\mathrm{H})
(this is viable by Assumption
7.15
). One notes that the adelic
\kappa
-orbital integral
\Orb_{x}^{\kappa}(\underline{f})
only depends on
a
, so we write this
\Orb_{a}^{\kappa}(\underline{f})
. Let
\mathrm{H}_{x}
be the (connected, reductive) centralizer.
Tate–Nakayama duality allows us write our sum (using the elliptic assumption) as

(10.4)

\TF_{\mathrm{X}}^{ell}(\underline{f})=\sum_{a\in(\mathrm{X}^{re}\sslash\mathrm{H})(k)}\sum_{\kappa\in Z(\check{\mathrm{H}}_{a})^{\Gamma}}\Orb_{a}^{\kappa}(\underline{f}),

where the notation
Z(\check{\mathrm{H}}_{a})
indicates that even though the
k
-form
\mathrm{H}_{x}
depends on our choice of lift
x=x_{a}
, this center does not since all choices yield inner forms.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{e}=({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}},\theta_{\mathfrak{e}},\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa,\eta_{\mathfrak{e}})
is an elliptic endoscopic datum for
({\mathrm{G}},\mathrm{X})
and let
x_{\mathfrak{e}}\in\mathrm{X}^{ss}_{\mathfrak{e}}(k)
. Let
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}:\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}\to\mathrm{X}\sslash\mathrm{H}
denote the morphism from Theorem
8.13
. We say that
x_{\mathfrak{e}}
is
(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}})
-regular or just
\mathrm{X}
-regular
if the class
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}([x_{\mathfrak{e}}])\in(\mathrm{X}\sslash\mathrm{H})(k)
is regular semi-simple in the sense that any lift
x
in
\mathrm{X}(k)
or to any pure inner twist of
\mathrm{X}
such that
[x]=\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}([x_{\mathfrak{e}}])
is a regular semi-simple element. Let
[\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}]^{\mathrm{X}}(k)
denote the set of
\mathrm{X}
-regular classes. Note that an
\mathrm{X}
-regular class
a
gives rise to a
({\mathrm{G}},{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}})
-regular element
s_{\mathfrak{e}}(a)\in{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\sslash{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}(k)
in the sense of
[
Kot86
]
, as the regular stabilizers in
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
are inner forms of those in
\mathrm{X}
.

Proposition 10.5
.

For each pair
(a,\kappa)\in(\mathrm{X}^{re}\sslash\mathrm{H})(k)\times Z(\check{\mathrm{H}}_{a})^{\Gamma}
, there exists an elliptic endoscopic datum
\mathfrak{e}=({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}},\theta_{\mathfrak{e}},\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa,\eta)
and a regular elliptic
b\in[\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}](k)
such that
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}(b)=a
. The pair
(\mathfrak{e},b)
is uniquely determined up to inner isomorphism in the sense that if
(\mathfrak{e}_{1},b_{1})
is another pair corresponding to
(a,\kappa)
, then there is an isomorphism
\mathfrak{e}\simeq\mathfrak{e}_{1}
identifying
b=b_{1}
, and that this isomorphism is uniquely determined up to
[{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}/(\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}\cap Z({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}))]/(k)
-conjugacy.

Proof.

Fix a pair
(a,\kappa)
and let
x=x_{a}
be a representative as above; for notational simplicity, we assume that
x\in\mathrm{X}^{re}(k)
. We let
({\mathrm{G}}_{x},\mathrm{H}_{x})
denote the descendent at
x
and let
S
denote a maximally
\theta
-split maximal torus of
{\mathrm{G}}_{x}
. Set
\T_{x}=S^{\theta}\subset\mathrm{H}_{x}
and

1\longrightarrow\T_{x}\longrightarrow S\longrightarrow S_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow 1

denote the quotient. Since
x
is regular, we also have
S_{\mathrm{X}}\simeq{\mathrm{G}}_{x}/\mathrm{H}_{x}
.

Fix also a
(\theta,k)
-admissible pair
(\mathrm{A},\B)
; there is an analogous short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow{\T}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow 1.

where
\T_{\mathrm{X}}=\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\theta}
. Following Proposition
7.10
, there exists an involution
\vartheta:\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
and a unique
\check{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{X}}
-conjugacy class of distinguished morphism

\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}:\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\check{{\mathrm{G}}},

such that
\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}(\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}})=\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}^{\vartheta,\circ}
; fixing such a morphism gives a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\mathrm{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\T}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mathrm{X}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\tau}}

As
S
is maximally
\theta
-split, Lemma
7.14
now implies the existence of a unique
\Gamma
-invariant
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{X}}
-orbit of such embeddings
\check{S}\longrightarrow\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
realizing
\check{S}
as a maximally
\vartheta
-split maximal torus of
\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
. For any such embedding, the quotient
\check{\tau}
induces an orbit map
\check{\T}_{x}\to\hat{\mathrm{X}}
, the image of which is a flat in
\hat{\mathrm{X}}
.

Since
{\mathrm{G}}_{x}/\mathrm{H}_{x}
is a torus and
S\subset{\mathrm{G}}_{x}
is maximal, we see that
T_{x}\subset\mathrm{H}_{x}
is a maximal torus of
\mathrm{H}_{x}
, so that duality gives rise to the canonical
\Gamma
-equivariant embedding
Z(\check{\mathrm{H}}_{x})\subset\check{\T}_{x}
.

Remark 10.6
.

For a general
x\in\mathrm{X}^{ell}(k)
, one can obtain a
\Gamma
-equivariant morphism
Z(\check{\mathrm{H}}_{x})\to\check{\T}_{x}
, which need not be injective (cf. Remark
10.1
).

By Tate-Nakayama duality and the ellipticity assumption, we obtain
\kappa\in Z(\check{\mathrm{H}}_{x})^{\Gamma}\to\check{\T}_{x}\to\hat{\mathrm{X}}
. Passing to the descendant at
\kappa
, we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},\kappa}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},\kappa}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mathrm{X}}_{\kappa}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mathrm{X}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}}

This gives rise to an endoscopic datum
\mathfrak{e}=({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}},\theta_{\mathfrak{e}},\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa,\eta_{\mathfrak{e}})
where
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\simeq\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\kappa}
and the symmetric variety
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
8.5
. Note that since
\mathrm{X}
is assumed excellent, it has no type
N
roots. This implies the same for
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
as discussed in Section
9.2
. By Corollary
8.6
, we may assume that
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is the quasi-split endoscopic variety.

Moreover, we also obtain a morphism
\check{S}\to\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},\kappa}\simeq\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}}
with image a maximally
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{e}}
-split maximal torus, the
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},\kappa}
-orbit of which is
\Gamma
-stable. Proposition
7.16
thus implies that up to replacing
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
by a pure inner twist
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\xi}
corresponding to a class in
H^{1}(k,\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}})
, we obtain a
k
-rational embedding
S^{-}\to{\mathrm{G}}^{\xi}_{\mathfrak{e}}
as a maximal
\theta_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\xi}
-split maximal torus; replacing
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
by such a twist if necessary, we drop the superscript
\xi
for notational simplicity. Let
S_{\mathfrak{e}}\subset{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
be a maximally
\theta_{\mathfrak{e}}
-split maximal torus such that
S_{\mathfrak{e}}^{-}
agrees with the image of
S^{-}
.

Let
x_{\mathfrak{e}}\in\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}(k)
correspond to
\theta_{\mathfrak{e}}
in the sense that
\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}={\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},x_{\mathfrak{e}}}={\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\theta_{\mathfrak{e}}}
and consider the flat
S^{-}_{\mathfrak{e}}\cdot x_{\mathfrak{e}}=S_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot x_{\mathfrak{e}}
. Recall that if
x_{0}\in\mathrm{X}(k)
is the distinguished base point of
\mathrm{X}
with respect to
\theta
, our point
x\in(S_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot x_{0})(k)
so there exists
t\in S_{\mathrm{X}}(k)
such that
x=t\cdot x_{0}
. Now set
x_{1}:=t\cdot x_{\mathfrak{e}}\in\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}(k)
.
Via the Chevalley isomorphism and Theorem
8.13
we see that
b:=[x_{1}]\mapsto[x]=a
under the map

\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}\simeq S_{\mathrm{X}}/W_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}}\overset{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}}{\longrightarrow}S_{\mathrm{X}}/W_{\mathrm{X}}\simeq\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}.

Thus
(\mathfrak{e},b)\mapsto(a,\kappa)
.

To establish uniqueness, we first show how to associate a pair
(a,\kappa)
as in (
10.4
) to a pair
(\mathfrak{e},b)
of an endoscopic datum
\mathfrak{e}
and an
\mathrm{X}
-regular class
b\in[\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}]^{\mathrm{X}}(k)
. Set
a:=\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}(b)
, where
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is the morphism of Theorem
8.13
. By Assumption
7.15
, up to replacing
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
by pure inner twists, we may let
x=x_{a}\in\mathrm{X}(k)
be a representative of
a
in
\mathrm{X}^{rss}(k)
and
y\in\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}(k)
be a representative of
b
in
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{rss}(k)
. The
\mathrm{X}
-regular assumption implies that the stabilizers
\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e},y}
and
\mathrm{H}_{x}
are inner forms of one another, so that
x
is elliptic if
y
is and there is a canonical isomorphism

Z(\check{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathfrak{e},b})\simeq Z(\check{\mathrm{H}}_{a}).

By assumption, the endoscopic datum
\mathfrak{e}
induces a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mathrm{X}}_{\mathfrak{e}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mathrm{X}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathrm{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}}

with
\kappa\in Z(\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathfrak{e}})^{\Gamma}\subset Z(\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}})
giving an element of
\mathcal{A}_{\hat{\mathrm{X}}_{\mathfrak{e}}}\simeq\hat{s}_{\mathfrak{e}}(\hat{\mathrm{X}}_{\mathfrak{e}})\cap Z(\hat{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}})

[
Vus90
, Lemma 1]
. Using (
8.1
), we obtain

\kappa\in\mathcal{A}_{\hat{\mathrm{X}}_{\mathfrak{e}}}^{\Gamma}\subset Z(\check{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathfrak{e},b})^{\Gamma}\simeq Z(\check{\mathrm{H}}_{a})^{\Gamma},

giving our pair
(a,\kappa)
.

Write
(\mathfrak{e}_{1},b_{1})=(\mathfrak{e},b)
. We now show that
(\mathfrak{e}_{2},b_{2})\mapsto(a,\kappa)
if and only if there is an isomorphism
\mathfrak{e}_{1}\simeq\mathfrak{e}_{2}
identifying
b_{1}=b_{2}
. We fix a lift
x_{1}\in\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}_{1}}(k)
as above and let
x_{2}\in\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}_{2}}(k)
be a lift of
b_{2}
. Fix also maximally
\theta
-split maximal tori
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
in
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},1}
and
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}
respectively containing these lift.

Using
\eta_{2}
and Proposition
7.16
, we may fix embeddings

j_{1}:S_{1}\to{\mathrm{G}}^{\xi_{1}},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad j_{2}:S_{2}\to{\mathrm{G}}^{\xi_{2}},

where
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in H^{1}(k,\mathrm{H})
may be distinct classes, each realizing
j_{i}(S_{i}^{-})
as a
k
-rational maximal
\theta^{\xi_{i}}
-split maximal torus. Arguing as above, this gives points
x_{1}\in\mathrm{X}^{\xi_{1}}(k)
and
x_{2}\in\mathrm{X}^{\xi_{2}}(k)
over the invariant
a\in(\mathrm{X}\sslash\mathrm{H})(k)
. But this implies that we may arrange for
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
and
x_{1}=x_{2}
by replacing
j_{2}
with a
\mathrm{H}(\overline{k})
-conjugate. At the cost of dropping the assumption that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split, we may drop the superscript
\xi
and denote by
\theta
the involution on
{\mathrm{G}}
with respect to which the above embeddings give rise to maximal
\theta
-split tori. We thus have a diagram (equivariant with respect to the various involutions) of
\overline{k}
-morphisms

\displaystyle S_{1}\overset{j_{1}}{\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle{\mathrm{G}}\overset{j_{2}}{\longleftarrow}S_{2}

\displaystyle s_{1}(x_{1})\longmapsto

\displaystyle s(x)\longmapsfrom s_{2}(x_{2}).

This implies that
j(S_{2})\subset{\mathrm{G}}_{x}
is a maximally
\theta
-split maximal torus, so that there exists
h\in\mathrm{H}_{x}(\overline{k})
such that
\Ad(h)\circ j_{2}(S_{2})=j_{1}(S_{1})
, allowing us to identify
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
over
\overline{k}
via
j_{2}^{-1}\circ j_{1}
. Since
\kappa
determines the root systems for
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},1}
and
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}
as transferred to
X^{\ast}(S)
, we see that there is a
\overline{k}
-isomorphism
\phi_{0}:{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},1}\to{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}
extending
j_{2}^{-1}\circ j_{1}:S_{1}\to S_{2}.
Moreover, since
j_{2}^{-1}\circ j_{1}
intertwines the two involutions, it follows that
\phi_{0}
may be chosen to be
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
-equivariant.

Since
j_{2}
is
(\theta_{2},\theta)
-equivariant
j_{2}(s_{2}(x_{2}))=s(x)
, we see that for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
,
{}^{\sigma}j_{2}=\Ad(h)\circ j_{2}
for some
h\in\mathrm{H}_{x}(\overline{k})
; here we have used the rationality of
x
and the assumption that
x\in\mathrm{X}^{rss}(k)
. On the other hand,
\mathrm{X}
-regularity of
b_{2}\in[\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}](k)
implies that
s_{2}(x_{2})
is
({\mathrm{G}},{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2})
-regular in the sense of
[
Kot86
]
, so that
{}^{\sigma}j_{2}=j_{2}\circ\Ad(h_{2})
for
h_{2}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}_{2},x_{2}}(\overline{k})
. This shows that
{}^{\sigma}(j_{2}^{-1}\circ j_{1})
is conjugate to
j_{2}^{-1}\circ j_{1}
by an element of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e},2,x_{2}}(\overline{k})
. It follows that the
\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e},2,x_{2}}
-conjugacy class of
\phi_{0}:{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},1}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}
is defined over
k
.

Recall that the pure inner twists which arise in Assumption
7.15
only correspond to twists of the symmetric pair. Fixing pure inner twists
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
to the quasi-split inner forms, we have a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},1},\theta_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2},\theta_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},1}^{\ast},\theta_{1}^{\ast})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}^{\ast},\theta_{2}^{\ast}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{2}}

where each arrow is equivariant with the involutions on the domain and codomain. An argument as in the proof of Lemma
7.14
, we see that a
\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e},2}^{\ast}(\overline{k})
-conjugate
\phi
of
\psi_{2}\circ\phi_{0}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1}
is defined over
k
. This implies that
\phi\circ\psi_{1}(s_{1}(x_{1}))
is stably conjugate to
\psi_{2}(s_{2}(x_{2})),
and we see that
\phi
induces an isomorphism
({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},1},\hat{s}_{1}(\kappa_{1}),\eta_{1})\longrightarrow({\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2},\hat{s}_{2}(\kappa_{2}),\eta_{2})
.

We claim that
\phi
intertwines
\theta_{1}^{\ast}
with a pure inner twist of
\theta_{2}^{\ast}
; that is
\theta_{1}^{\prime}:=\phi\circ\theta_{1}^{\ast}\circ\phi^{-1}
is a pure inner twist of
\theta_{2}^{\ast}
. To see this, note that they are both
k
-rational involutions on
{\mathrm{G}}^{\ast}_{\mathfrak{e},2}
which agree on the maximally
\theta
-split torus
10
10

10

By construction, the involutions all agree on
\psi_{2}(S_{2})
.
. By
[
Hel88
, Theorem 3.7]
, there exists
h\in{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}^{\ast}(\overline{k})
satisfying
\theta^{\prime}_{1}=\Ad(h)^{-1}\circ\theta_{2}^{\ast}\circ\Ad(h)
and that
\Ad(h^{-1}\theta_{2}^{\ast}(h))\in S_{ad}(k)
, where
S_{ad}^{\ast}\subset{\mathrm{G}}^{\ast}_{\mathfrak{e},2,ad}
is the image in the adjoint group. This implies that the involutions are geometrically conjugate, so that the symmetric varieties

X_{1}^{\prime}:={\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}^{\ast,\theta_{1}^{\prime}}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}^{\ast}
and
X_{2}^{\ast}:={\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}^{\ast,\theta^{\ast}_{2}}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}^{\ast}_{\mathfrak{e},2}

are
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}^{\ast}
-forms of each other. Thus, there is a canonical isomorphism

\Aut_{d}({X_{1}^{\prime}})\simeq\Aut_{d}({\mathrm{X}^{\ast}_{2}}),

as this group depends only on
{\mathrm{G}}^{\ast}_{\mathfrak{e},2}
by Lemma
3.13
. Since both forms have geometric cocycles determined by (
8.4
) and they are well-adapted, it follows that they are
{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e},2}
-inner forms. Since they are both quasi-split in the sense of Corollary
8.6
, it follows that they are isomorphic. Lemma
5.1
now implies that this forces
\theta_{1}^{\prime}=\phi\circ\theta_{1}^{\ast}\circ\phi^{-1}
to be a pure inner twist of
\theta_{2}^{\ast}
, proving the claim. It is now straightforward to verify that
\phi
induces an isomorphism of endoscopic data, since it intertwines maximally
\theta
-split tori, so that maps
\eta_{1}\circ\check{\phi}
is conjugate by
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{1}}
.

Finally, we verify that this isomorphism is unique up to inner automorphisms, which amounts to showing that any automorphism
\phi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{e})
which preserves the
\mathrm{X}
-regular semi-simple class
b
(in the sense that if
y\in\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}(k)
lies in the stable orbit over
b
, then so does
\phi_{\mathrm{X}}(y)
where
\phi_{\mathrm{X}}:\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}\to\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is the induced automorphism) is automatically inner.

The observation (already used above) is that the matching
a=\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}(b)
induces a matching of semi-simple orbits
\chi_{\mathfrak{e}}(s_{\mathfrak{e}}(b))=s(a)
as in Corollary
8.18
. Since
b
is
\mathrm{X}
-regular, it follows that
s_{\mathfrak{e}}(b)
is a
({\mathrm{G}},{\mathrm{G}}_{\mathfrak{e}})
-regular class. Since
\phi
preserves
b
, it follows that
f_{\phi}
preserves
s_{\mathfrak{e}}(b)
. The proof of
[
Kot86
, Lemma 9.2]
now goes through, showing that
\phi
must be inner.
∎

In particular, we may rewrite the sum

\TF_{\mathrm{X}}^{re}(\underline{f})=\sum_{\mathfrak{e}/\sim}\left(\sum_{b\in[\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{re}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}]^{\mathrm{X}}(k)}\Orb_{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{e}}(b)}^{\kappa}(\underline{f})\right),

where
[\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{re}\sslash\mathrm{H}_{\mathfrak{e}}]^{\mathrm{X}}
denotes those orbits in
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{re}
which are
(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}})
-regular. At this stage, one is in a position to formulate the local conjectures necessary to relate the inner sum to (stable) orbital integrals on
\mathrm{X}_{\mathfrak{e}}
. We defer to
[
Les24
]
for the details.

Appendix A
Functoriality of the dual group

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}
is reductive over
k
and let
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
denote the derived subgroup. Suppose that
\mathrm{H}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
is a spherical
k
-subgroup and set
\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{G}},der}:=\mathrm{H}\cap{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
. The purpose of this section is to clarify the effect of viewing a spherical
{\mathrm{G}}
-variety as a (potentially non-spherical)
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
-variety.

Lemma A.1
.

The subgroup
\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{G}},der}\subset{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
is spherical.

Proof.

The assumption that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is spherical is equivalent to the existence of a Borel subgroup
\B\subset{\mathrm{G}}
such that
B\cdot\mathrm{H}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
is Zariski-open. Since
{\mathrm{G}}={\mathrm{G}}_{der}\cdot Z({\mathrm{G}})^{\circ}
and
Z({\mathrm{G}})^{\circ}\subset\B
, it is immediate that
\B_{der}\cdot\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{G}},der}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
is Zariski-open.
∎

Setting
\mathrm{X}_{der}={\mathrm{G}}_{der}/\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{G}},der}
, we obtain a closed embedding
\iota:\mathrm{X}_{der}\to\mathrm{X}
sitting in a sequence

\mathrm{X}_{der}\longrightarrow\mathrm{X}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{G}}_{ab}/\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{G}},ab},

where
\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{G}},ab}
is the schematic image of
\mathrm{H}
under the abelianization map. In general,
\mathrm{X}
need not be spherical as a
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
-variety. Happily, the dual group of Knop–Schalke exists for a general (normal)
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
-variety. In particular, there exists a dual group
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},der}
and a distinguished morphism
\varphi_{\mathrm{X},der}:\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},der}\to\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{der}
.

Lemma A.2
.

For any choice of distinguished morphisms
\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}
and
\varphi_{\mathrm{X},der}
, there exists a unique surjective morphism
p_{\mathrm{X}}:\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}\longrightarrow\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},der}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},der}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{der}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathrm{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathrm{X},der}}

commutes.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathrm{A}=\B/[\B,\B]
is the canonical maximal torus for
{\mathrm{G}}
, and let
\mathrm{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
be the quotient through which
\mathrm{A}
acts on
\mathring{\mathrm{X}}\sslash[\B,\B]
where
\mathring{\mathrm{X}}
denotes the open
\B
-orbit.
Now suppose that
\mathrm{A}_{der}=\B_{der}/[\B_{der},\B_{der}]
is the canonical torus of
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
, and let
\mathrm{A}_{der}\to\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X},der}
be the corresponding quotient. The local structure theorem implies that
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X},der})
is the lattice of
\B_{der}
-characters associated to
\B_{der}
-semiinvariant functions on
\mathrm{X}
. In particular, since there is a natural embedding
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X},der}\subset\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
. It follows that the restriction map
\mathfrak{X}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{der}
is surjective.

Passing to dual side, we obtain a commutative diagram

(A.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\mathrm{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{X},der}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\mathrm{A}}_{der}.}

Considering now the dual groups, a choice of distinguished morphism (unique up to
\check{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{X},der}
-conjugacy) induces a natural extension of three of these morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},der}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{der},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathrm{X},der}}

and the claim is that
p\circ\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}
factors through
\varphi_{\mathrm{X},der}
and a morphism
p_{\mathrm{X}}
extending the left vertical arrow in (
A.1
). But this follows since the sublattice
X^{\ast}(\check{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{X},der})\subset X^{\ast}(\check{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{X}})
contains the set of roots
\check{\Phi}_{\mathrm{X}}
for
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}
, inducing such a morphism. Moreover, the commutativity of (
A.1
) and the definition of distinguished morphisms imply that
p\circ\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}
and
\varphi_{\mathrm{X},der}
have a common image in
\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{der}
.

∎

Recalling the functoriality properties of dual groups
[
Kno19
, Theorem 2]
, the injective
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
-morphism
\iota
induces a dual map

(A.2)

\check{\iota}:\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{der}}\longrightarrow\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X},der}.

Upon fixing distinguished morphisms
\varphi_{\mathrm{X},der}
and
\varphi_{\mathrm{X}_{der}}
,
\check{\iota}
is unique and has a finite kernel.

Lemma A.3
.

The morphism (
A.2
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The tori
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X},der}
and
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}_{der}}
are isomorphic as they share a common character lattice. Since the kernel of
\check{\iota}
agrees with the kernel of the natural map
\check{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{X},der}\to\check{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{X}_{der}}
, the claim follows.
∎

In particular, if
\check{Z}_{\mathrm{X}}
denotes the kernel of
p_{\mathrm{X}}
, then
\check{Z}_{\mathrm{X}}
is dual to
{\mathrm{G}}_{ab}/\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{G}},ab}
, which has the character lattice
\mathfrak{X}_{Z}
satisfying

0\longrightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{Z}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{X}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{der}\longrightarrow 0.

Finally, Lemma
A.2
implies that there is commutative diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{Z}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{\mathrm{X}_{der}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{ab}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{{\mathrm{G}}}_{der}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathrm{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathrm{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

Appendix B
Spherical data of symmetric varieties

In this appendix, we make explicit the spherical data of a symmetric variety. Let us assume that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a symmetric
k
-variety associated to a
k
-rational involution
\theta
. For simplicity, we assume that
{\mathrm{G}}
is quasi-split, though this is not essential. We fix a
(\theta,k)
-admissible Borel pair
(\mathrm{A},\B)
as in Section
4.3.1
; in particular,
\B
is maximally
\theta
-split so that the associated set of simple roots gives a
(\Gamma,\theta)
-basis for
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
.

Remark B.1
.

We worked with the canonical torus
\mathrm{A}=\B/[\B,\B]
in Section
3.2
. In the current setting, we opt to utilize maximally
\theta
-split maximal tori as their existence is very useful. If one preferred to work with the canonical torus, when
\B
is chosen as above, then
\mathrm{A}=\B/[\B,\B]
is isomorphic to any
(\theta,k)
-admissible torus
A\subset\B
in a
\theta
-equivariant way.

There is a short exact sequence of diagonalizable groups

1\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}^{\theta}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\theta}\longrightarrow 1.

Let
X^{\ast}_{\theta}:=X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\theta})=\{\lambda-\theta(\lambda):\lambda\in X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})\}
and

\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}:=\{\alpha-\theta(\alpha):\alpha\in\Delta,\>\theta(\alpha)\neq\alpha\}.

We compute the little Weyl group
[
Ric82
, Section 4]

W_{\mathrm{X}}\simeq N_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A}^{-})/Z_{{\mathrm{G}}}(\mathrm{A}^{-})\simeq N_{({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}}(\mathrm{A}^{-})/Z_{({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}}(\mathrm{A}^{-}),

and the relative roots
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathrm{X}}=W_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}
; this is the reduced root system associated to the (possibly non-reduced) relative root system of
\mathrm{X}
with base
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}
. With respect to the
k
-structure of
\mathrm{A}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
, the associated
\Gamma
-action preserves
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}
.

Lemma B.2
.

For
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})
, let
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
. The root lattice
\Lambda_{\mathrm{X}}
is given by
\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}
.

Proof.

When
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
, this follows from the claim
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}={\Delta}^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}
which is
[
Kno96
, Theorem 6.7]
. Lemma 3.1.5 (4) of
[
Los09
]
implies the claim for any
\mathrm{H}
such that
[\mathrm{H}:({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}]
is finite. On the other hand,
N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
descends to a symmetric variety of
{\mathrm{G}}_{ad}
so that the root lattice is also given by the root lattice of the relative root system, that is by
\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}
. Finally, since

({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}\to\mathrm{X}\to N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})\backslash{\mathrm{G}}

are each surjective
{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariant morphisms with the first and last varieties possessing the same root lattice, the claim follows.
∎

The quotient torus
\mathrm{A}_{\theta}
is isomorphic to the canonical torus
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}_{\theta}}
when
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
. In general, the quotient through which
\mathrm{A}
acts freely on the distinguished base point
x_{0}\in\mathrm{X}(k)
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
. When
\mathrm{X}^{\circ}=({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
, the combination of
[
Hof18
, Corollary 1.5]
(see also
[
Kno24
]
) with Proposition
B.7
below shows that

(B.1)

X^{\ast}_{\theta,\circ}:=X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}^{\circ}})=\{\lambda\in X^{\ast}_{\theta,\mathbb{Q}}\cap X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}):\langle\check{\gamma}^{n},\lambda\rangle\in\mathbb{Z},\text{ for all }\gamma^{n}\in{\Delta}^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}\}.

Example B.3
.

If
{\mathrm{G}}=\GL_{n}
and
\theta(g)={}^{T}g^{-1}
so that
{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}=\mathrm{O}_{n}
, then
X^{\ast}_{\theta}=2X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
and

\check{{\Delta}}^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}=\left\{\frac{1}{2}\check{\alpha}:\alpha\in\Delta\right\}.

Thus
X^{\ast}_{\theta,\circ}=2X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})+\mathbb{Z}(e_{1}+\cdots+e_{n})
gives the weight lattice of
\GL_{n}/\SO_{n}
.∎

More generally, let
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
with any
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})
. Since
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}\to\mathrm{X}
, we have a
\Gamma
-stable chain of lattices

\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}\subset\mathfrak{X}\subset X^{\ast}_{\theta,\circ}

corresponding uniquely to the quotient
\Aut^{{\mathrm{G}}}(({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\backslash{\mathrm{G}})\to\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
.

As an application, we recall from
[
Les22
, Section 4]
that a symmetric pair
({\mathrm{G}},{\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})
is called
simply connected
if for each semi-simple
x\in\mathrm{X}_{\theta}^{ss}(k)
the stabilizer
\mathrm{H}_{x}
in
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
is geometrically connected.

Lemma B.4
.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{G}}_{der}\subset{\mathrm{G}}
is simply connected and that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a symmetric variety with
\mathrm{H}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
. The stabilizers
\mathrm{H}_{x}
are geometrically connected for all
x\in\mathrm{X}^{ss}(k)
if and only if
\mathrm{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}
has a geometrically connected kernel.

Proof.

Clearly we may pass to
\overline{k}
and assume that
k
is algebraically closed. For a general
x\in\mathrm{X}^{ss}(k)
,
we denote the descendent pair
({\mathrm{G}}_{x},\mathrm{H}_{x})
. Note that any
x\in\mathrm{X}^{ss}(k)
lies in a maximally
\theta
-split torus
A^{-}
. Letting
A
be a maximal torus containing
A^{-}
, we may assume that
x\in s(A)(k)
lies in the image of
A
under the symmetrization map
s
; for notation set
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}=s(A)
for this quotient. Setting
\mathrm{A}_{0}=\ker(A\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
, clearly,
\mathrm{A}_{0}\subset\mathrm{H}_{x}.
In particular, for any
x\in\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}(k)
,
A\subset{\mathrm{G}}_{x}
is a maximally
\theta
-split maximal torus of the descent
({\mathrm{G}}_{x},\mathrm{H}_{x})
.

Sufficiency follows since if
x
is regular semi-simple, then
\mathrm{H}_{x}\supset\mathrm{A}_{0}
satisfies
[
GVX18
, pg.9]

\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}_{x})=\pi_{0}(\mathrm{A}_{0}).

Passing to the derived subgroup, we consider the
\theta
-stable maximal torus of the derived subgroup
A_{der}\subset{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
. By Steinberg’s theorem
[
Ste68
]
, the fixed-point subgroup
\mathrm{H}\cap{\mathrm{G}}_{der}
of the semisimple automorphism
\theta|_{{\mathrm{G}}_{der}}
is connected.

Now assume that
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{A}_{0})=\{\ast\}
. Arguing by induction on
\dim(\mathrm{X}_{x})
, the claim reduces to seeing that
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H})=\{\ast\}
. As stated above, we have that the lattice for
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is
X^{\ast}_{\theta,\circ}\subset X_{\theta,\mathbb{Q}}^{\ast}\cap X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
. The assumption forces
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})=X_{\theta}^{\ast}
to be saturated as a sublattice of
X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A})
, so that

X^{\ast}_{\theta,\circ}=X_{\theta}^{\ast}=X^{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}}).

This forces
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}={\mathrm{G}}^{\theta}
, proving the claim.∎

B.1.
Normalized and associated roots

We make a few remarks on the normalized roots
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{sv}
of a symmetric variety.

Lemma B.5
.

Suppose that
({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})^{\circ}\subset\mathrm{H}\subset N_{{\mathrm{G}}}({\mathrm{G}}^{\theta})
is a symmetric subgroup and
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
the associated symmetric variety. Suppose that
\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
is a spherical root of type
G
and

\gamma^{sv}=\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}

are the associated roots. Then
\theta(\gamma_{1})=-\gamma_{2}
.

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
B.2
shows that the spherical roots are obtained by re-normalizing the reduced roots

\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{X}}=\{\alpha-\theta(\alpha):\alpha\in\Delta-\Delta^{p}_{\mathrm{X}}\},

so that there exists
c\in\{1/2,1\}
and
\alpha\in\Delta-\Delta^{p}_{\mathrm{X}}
such that

\gamma=c(\alpha-\theta(\alpha)).

In particular,
-\theta(\gamma)=-\theta(\gamma_{1})-\theta(\gamma_{2})=\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}=\gamma
. But note that
\{-\theta(\gamma_{1}),-\theta(\gamma_{2})\}
are then two strongly orthogonal roots of
{\mathrm{G}}
summing to
\gamma
.

By the duality between
\Phi
and
\check{\Phi}
, we see that
{(\theta(\gamma))}^{\vee}=\check{\theta}(\check{\gamma})
. Thus,

(-\theta(\gamma_{2}))^{\vee}-(-\theta(\gamma_{1}))^{\vee}=(-\theta(\delta_{2}))^{\vee}-(-\theta(\delta_{1}))^{\vee}.

An easy calculation handling the cases of
D_{2}
and
D_{n\geq 3}
separately shows that

(-\theta(\delta_{2}))^{\vee}-(-\theta(\delta_{1}))^{\vee}=\check{\delta}_{2}-\check{\delta}_{1}.

By uniqueness, this implies the claim. ∎

Suppose now that
\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
is of type
N
. That is,
\gamma=2\beta
for some
\beta\in\Phi^{+}
, but
\beta\notin\mathfrak{X}
. Using Lemma
B.2
, we see that
\gamma=c(\alpha-\theta(\alpha))
for some simple root
\alpha\in\Delta
and for
c\in\{1,1/2\}
. We claim that
c=1
. Otherwise, we have

4\beta=\alpha-\theta(\alpha).

But this is impossible since the simple root
\alpha
occurs in the support of the right-hand side with multiplicity
2
. Thus when
\gamma
is a root of type
N
, then

2\beta=\gamma=\alpha-\theta(\alpha),\text{ but }\beta\notin\mathfrak{X}.

This implies a strong connection between roots of type
N
and distinguished roots (cf. Lemma
3.9
).

Proposition B.6
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a symmetric variety and that
\gamma=\alpha-\theta(\alpha)\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
is a spherical root of type
N
. Then

(A)

(Type
N
(
A
))
\gamma=2\alpha
such that there exists
D\in{\mathcal{D}}
with
\rho(D)=\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{\vee}|_{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X}}}
;

(B)

(Type
N
(
B
)) there is a subset
\Sigma\subset\Delta
of type
B_{k}
with
k\geq 2
such that

\gamma=2(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{k}),

and
\alpha_{i}\in\Delta^{p}
for
i>1
;

Proof.

Let
\Delta_{\alpha}\subset\Delta
be the set of simple roots satisfying that
\alpha\in\Delta_{\alpha}
and if we write

\theta(\alpha)=-\sum_{\beta\in\Delta}c_{\beta}\beta,

then
\beta\in\Delta_{\alpha}\setminus\{\alpha\}
if and only if
c_{\beta}\neq 0
. The sub-root system generated by
\Delta_{\alpha}
is stable under
\theta
, and corresponds to a
\theta
-stable Levi
\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\supset\mathrm{A}
with the corresponding boundary degeneration (cf.
[
SV17
, Section 2]
) the parabolic induced variety from the symmetric Levi variety
\mathrm{X}_{\alpha}:=\mathrm{H}_{\Delta_{\alpha}}\backslash\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}
. This gives a symmetric varieties of rank
1
with
\Delta_{\mathrm{X}_{\alpha}}=\{2\beta\}
. Passing to this Levi variety
\mathrm{X}_{\alpha}
, the proposition may be verified via the classification of rank
1
symmetric varieties.
∎

For example, roots of type N(
B
) occur for the varieties
\Spin_{2b}\times^{\mu_{2}}\Spin_{2a+1}\backslash\Spin_{2n+1}
, for any
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
satisfying
a+b=n
. If we let
a=0
, the variety
\Spin_{2n}\backslash\Spin_{2n+1}
has a distinguished root of type (
B
), while
\mathrm{Pin}_{2n}\backslash\Spin_{2n+1}
has a root of type
N
.

B.2.
Colors of symmetric varieties

The reduced root system
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathrm{X}}
almost encodes the color data of
\mathrm{X}
; some analysis of the possible root systems is required. Fix a maximally
\theta
-split Borel subgroup
B\subset{\mathrm{G}}
and let
\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{X})
denote the corresponding set of colors.

Proposition B.7
.

For
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
symmetric over
\overline{k}
, the image
\rho({\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X}))\subset\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X},\mathbb{Q}}
corresponds to
\{\check{\gamma}^{n}:\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}\mapsto\gamma^{n}\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}\}
. That is, the images are dual to the simple vectors in the relative root system of
\mathrm{X}
.

Proof.

This is
[
Vus90
, Proposition 1]

∎

To enumerate the colors requires the consideration of different cases.
Now for any
\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
, Proposition
B.7
indicates that there exist colors
{\mathcal{D}}(\gamma)\subset{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})
satisfying that
\rho(\Delta(\gamma))=\check{\gamma}^{n}
, where
\gamma^{n}\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}
is the associated
n
-spherical root and
\check{\gamma}^{n}\in X_{\ast}(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{X}})
is the associated coroot. On the other hand, for each spherical root
\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
, we saw in Section
5.3
that there exists a well-defined
k
-simple factor
\mathrm{X}_{\gamma}
of
\mathrm{X}_{sc}
. Recalling notation from Section
5.3
, we set
\mathrm{H}_{\gamma}:=\mathrm{H}_{sc,\gamma}
.

If
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{H}\backslash{\mathrm{G}}
is a symmetric variety and
\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}
, there are three possibilities (cf.
[
Tim11
, Section 26.8]
):

(1)

\mathrm{X}_{\gamma}
has a non-reduced restricted root system of type
BC_{n}
, and
\check{\gamma}^{n}=\lambda^{\vee}-\theta(\lambda^{\vee})
is a short coroot. Then
\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}(\check{\gamma})=\{D_{\lambda},D_{\theta^{\ast}(\lambda)}\}
, and

\varsigma(D_{\lambda})=\{\lambda\},\text{ and }\varsigma(D_{\theta^{\ast}(\lambda)})=\{\theta^{\ast}(\lambda)\};

(2)

\mathrm{X}_{\gamma}
has a reduced restricted root system of type
B_{n}
, and
\check{\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{\vee}
is a (real) short coroot. Then
\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}(\check{\gamma})=\{D_{\alpha}^{+},D_{\alpha}^{-}\}
and

\varsigma(D_{\alpha}^{+})=\varsigma(D_{\alpha}^{-})=\{\alpha\}.

This is precisely the case of double roots;

(3)

otherwise,
\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}(\check{\gamma})=\{D_{\gamma}\}
. Here
\varsigma(D_{\gamma})=\{\lambda\}
where
\gamma=\lambda-\theta(\lambda)
.

In all cases
\rho(\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}(\check{\gamma}))=\check{\gamma}^{n}\in\check{\Delta}^{n}_{\mathrm{X}}
. Then

(B.2)

{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})=\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{n}}{\mathcal{D}}(\check{\gamma}),

where
{\mathcal{D}}(\check{\gamma})=\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}(\check{\gamma})/\sim
where
{\mathcal{D}}(\alpha^{\vee})=\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}(\check{\gamma})
when
|\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}(\check{\gamma})|=1
and

{\mathcal{D}}(\check{\gamma})=\begin{cases}\>\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}(\check{\gamma})&:|\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}_{\gamma})(\overline{k})|=1,\\
\{D_{1}=D_{2}\}&:|\pi_{0}(\mathrm{H}_{\gamma})(\overline{k})|>1,\end{cases}

when
|\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}(\check{\gamma})|=2
. We thus obtain the map

\rho\times\varsigma:{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{X})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{X}}\times\mathcal{P}(\Delta),

from which we may compute the sets
(\Omega^{(1)},\Omega^{(2)})
.
